# Agent Models Specification

**Last updated:** 2026-10-31
**Status:** Draft v1. Gates milestone M3 (batter vs pitcher); the fielding and running sections gate M4–M5.
**Parent:** [PLAN.md](../PLAN.md) §7, §8, §10.4 · **Siblings:** [physics-spec.md](physics-spec.md), [data-formats.md](data-formats.md), `calibration-targets.md` (P3), `rules-spec.md` (P4)

This document defines how every autonomous agent **perceives, believes, decides, and executes**: pitchers, batters, catchers, fielders, runners, base coaches, and the AI manager. It also defines the attribute-to-parameter mappings, the learned belief tables, and the belief snapshots each model must emit for play explanations.

Status tags: **[S]** sourced, **[D]** derived, **[E]** estimate (a named, configurable parameter, never a literal in code), **[C]** design choice the owner may revisit.

**D1 reminder.** Agents may hold probabilities, expected values, and learned tables. Nothing in this document decides an outcome. Agents produce **motor commands**; the world resolves them with physics. Every number below either shapes a belief, shapes a decision, or sets the size of physical noise on a motor command.

---

## 1. Common Framework

### 1.1 The agent loop

Every agent runs the same four stages, at its own rate:

```
Observation (bb_observe)  →  Belief update  →  Decision  →  Intended motor command
                                                                  │
                                              Execution (noise)   ▼
                                                           Executed command → world (bb_world)
```

- **Observations** are the only input. They are types in `bb_observe`, built by `bb_game` from world state plus perceptual noise. Agents never see a `bb_world` type (PLAN §4.1, §15).
- **Execution** is a separate module, `bb_agents::motor`. It takes an *intended* command and applies execution noise with the keyed RNG ([data-formats §2.3](data-formats.md)). Its output type, `Executed*`, is consumed only by `bb_game`, which passes it to the world. **Decision code never receives an `Executed*` value**, so an agent cannot know its own error except by observing the result like anyone else. This is enforced by a source scan, [bb_agents/tests/exec_isolation.rs](../sim/crates/bb_agents/tests/exec_isolation.rs), not by module visibility: `motor` and its `Executed*` types are `pub` (`bb_game` and the lab tests need them), and Rust cannot hide a `pub` item from the sibling modules of its own crate, so a compile-fail test inside `bb_agents` cannot fail either. The scan reads every file under `bb_agents/src/` except `motor.rs` and fails on any `Executed*` or `execute_*` name, on any mention of `motor` other than `lib.rs`'s `mod motor;`, and on a `path` attribute argument (also nested in `cfg_attr`) or the identifier `include` (also renamed with `use .. as ..`). It also checks `motor.rs` itself: no `trait`, no `macro_rules`, and every `impl` item must be for a `struct`, `enum` or `union` that `motor.rs` defines (a type alias or a tuple or array self type does not count), because an `impl IntendedRelease { fn run(..) -> Executed.. }` would let decision code call it without naming `motor`. The test header lists the limits. A compiler-enforced rule needs `motor` in its own crate, which changes the crate layout and AGENTS.md invariant 3; that is the owner's call. Whether `bb_game` passes an `Executed*` value to an agent is still checked in review.
- **Rates [E]:** batter perception 100 Hz from release to contact; fielder and runner re-planning 30 Hz; pitcher and manager decisions once per play.
- **Determinism:** agents run in a fixed order (by `AgentId`) within each tick. No agent reads another agent's state directly; shared information goes through observations or explicit communication messages (§5.7).

### 1.2 Attributes: internal units and the 20–80 scale

Attributes are stored in **physical units** (PLAN §7). The 20–80 scouting grade is a display transform over the league population for that attribute [C]:

- **"More is better" quantities** (speed, arm strength): `value(g) = μ + σ_pop · (g − 50) / 10`.
- **Noise and time quantities** (command σ, reaction time), where less is better and the scale is multiplicative: `value(g) = value_50 · ρ^(−(g − 50) / 10)`, with a per-attribute ratio ρ.
- Grades clamp to [20, 80] for display only; the stored physical value is not clamped.

μ, σ_pop, value_50, and ρ for every attribute are in §10. The population distributions themselves (correlations, position differences) belong to the generator and `calibration-targets.md`.

### 1.3 Noise conventions

- All execution and perception noise is **Gaussian, truncated at ±4σ** [C]. Truncation removes absurd outliers (a 6σ wild pitch from an elite pitcher) without affecting calibration.
- Effective noise is a product of independent factors:
  `σ_eff = σ_attr · K_global · F_fatigue · F_composure · F_situation`
  - `σ_attr` from the attribute (§10).
  - `K_global` is one of the global calibration knobs (calibration-targets §2.3) (perception, command, swing miss, swing effort, fielder reaction). Default 1. The swing has two (§3.6): the miss scale (placement and timing) and the effort scale (bat speed).
  - `F_fatigue` and `F_composure` from §1.5 and §2.5.
  - `F_situation` from the specific mechanism (a rushed throw, a short hop, a reach).
- Every factor is a named function in one module, so the sensitivity tool (PLAN §13.3) can sweep each one.

### 1.4 Agents' internal physics

Agents predict with the **same physics functions** as the world (`bb_physics`), fed with their own noisy estimates (PLAN §4.1). Two cost and realism rules:
- **Precompute per game, not per pitch.** Batter trajectory predictions use per-pitch-type tables and linear sensitivities built at game start (§3.3). Fielders run their flight predictions on demand as described in §5.1.
- **Agents do not model what they cannot perceive.** A fielder's prediction assumes the *typical* spin for the launch angle, not the true spin (§5.1). A fielder never predicts a bad hop (surface roughness, physics spec §9.3). These mismatches are how real misreads happen.

### 1.5 Composure and perceived leverage

Each agent perceives game leverage from its own beliefs: `L̂` is the leverage index looked up from the learned win-expectancy table (§8) for the agent's own reading of the situation. A rookie and a veteran see the same scoreboard, so v1 uses the true base-out-score state for `L̂` [C]; misreading leverage is a later option.

```
F_composure = 1 + κ_c · (1 − C) · clamp(L̂ − 1, 0, L_max)
```

- `C ∈ [0, 1]` is `Composure`. κ_c = 0.15 [E], L_max = 4 [E]. An average player (C = 0.5) in a leverage-3 spot gets 15% more noise; a C = 0.9 player gets 3%.
- Applies to **execution noise** (release, swing placement, glove, throw) and to **perceptual noise** (batter observations, fielder reads).
- It never shifts a mean. Pressure makes players less precise, not systematically worse in one direction. This is physiological stress, not a "clutch" modifier (PLAN §8.6).

---

## 2. Pitcher

### 2.1 Arsenal

Each pitch type `k` in a pitcher's arsenal is defined physically:

| Field | Unit | Meaning |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| `type` | enum | FF, SI, FC, SL, ST (sweeper), CU, KC, CH, FS, SV (slurve). Savant codes ([data-formats §9.4](data-formats.md)) |
| `speed_ratio` | — | Release speed / `ArmSpeed` (FF = 1.00, CH ≈ 0.88, CU ≈ 0.82) |
| `spin_rate_factor` | — | Spin rate / (fastball spin from `SpinCapacity`) |
| `tilt` | clock hours | Magnus direction at release, pitcher's perspective ([data-formats §9.3](data-formats.md)) |
| `efficiency` | 0–1 | Transverse fraction of spin |
| `gyro_sign` | ±1 | Direction of the gyro component |
| `command_mult` | — | Multiplier on the pitcher's base command σ for this pitch |
| `release_offset` | m (x, z) | Small per-pitch shift of the release point (a "tell" when large) |
| `usage` | table | Base usage by count group (ahead, even, behind, two strikes) and batter handedness |

The spin vector for a release velocity **v** is constructed from rate, tilt, efficiency, and gyro sign by inverting [data-formats §9.2](data-formats.md). That construction has a round-trip unit test.

### 2.2 Release point

Release position from body and delivery attributes [E, chosen to land in public ranges; check bands in §11]:

```
y_rel = 18.440 − Extension
z_rel = h_front + 0.65·H + ℓ·sin(ArmSlot)
x_rel = s_arm · (0.10·H + ℓ·cos(ArmSlot))        ℓ = 0.40·H
```

- H is height (m); `ArmSlot` is the angle above horizontal of the shoulder-to-hand line at release; `h_front` = 0.10 m [E] is the front-of-mound height at the landing foot.
- `s_arm` = −1 for right-handers (arm side is third base, −X), +1 for left-handers.
- Check: H = 1.88 m, slot 45°, extension 1.95 m → release (−0.72, 16.49, 1.85) m, i.e. Savant `release_pos_x` −2.4 ft, `release_pos_z` 6.1 ft, extension 6.4 ft. Typical.
- Release-point noise is part of execution (§2.3).

### 2.3 Aiming and execution

**Intent:** a pitch type `k` and a target `(x_t, z_t)` on the strike-zone plane (y = y_mid, physics spec §10).

**Aiming solve** (pitcher's intent, no noise): find release direction angles (yaw ψ, elevation φ) so that the nominal pitch (`speed_ratio·ArmSpeed`, pitch-type spin, release point) reaches the target.
- Initial guess from a per-pitch-type offset learned at game start (the "aim correction" for that pitch's break).
- Fixed-point update: `ψ ← ψ − Δx / D`, `φ ← φ + Δz / D`, with D the release-to-plate distance. Small-angle geometry makes this Jacobian nearly exact, so 2–3 flights converge to < 1 mm. No finite differences needed.
- Two stages (implementation, 2026-10-31): the search iterates with a 0.02 s flight step (`AIM_SEARCH_DT`) to 1e-5 m, then the answer is confirmed, and corrected if needed, with the world's pitch step (`DT_PITCH`, 0.002 s) to the 1e-4 m acceptance tolerance. At 0.02 s RK4 lands within ~3 nm of `DT_PITCH` at the plate for every arsenal type, so a warm-started solve costs one full-step flight plus a few at a tenth of the cost. The reported residual is always the full-step one. A batter's scouting models aim each believed shape from the pitcher's cached zone-center solve rather than re-solving the true pitch.
- Pitchers know their own pitches. The solve is exact for the intent (they aim correctly); misses come only from execution.

**Execution noise** (purpose `PitchRelease`), each independent:

| Quantity | σ | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Yaw and elevation of release direction | σ_cmd · command_mult · F_fatigue · F_composure · K_cmd | σ_cmd from `Command` (§10); ≈ 1.6 cm at the plate per mrad |
| Release speed | 0.45 m/s [E] (≈ 1 mph) · F_fatigue | |
| Spin rate | 2% [E] | |
| Spin tilt | 3° [E] | Moves break direction pitch to pitch |
| Release point x, z | 2 cm [E] | |

Elevation noise and release-speed noise are physically coupled in real deliveries; v1 treats them as independent [C].

### 2.4 Pitch selection

`choose_pitch` in `sim/crates/bb_game/src/pa.rs` samples pitch types from count-specific weights and samples targets from heuristic zones. The M6 learned policy picks `(k, target)` from candidates: each arsenal pitch × a 7 × 7 target grid spanning x ∈ [−0.45, 0.45] m and z from 0.15·H_b to 0.70·H_b of the batter's height [E].

**Believed value of a candidate:**

```
V(k, target) = Σ_j  π̃_j · E_exec[ RV(k, loc, count, batter_class, surprised = (j ≠ k)) ]
```

- `π̃_j`: the pitcher's **model of what the batter expects** (theory of mind). It is computed by running the batter's own prior-construction function (§3.1) on *observable* history only: the pitch sequence, the count, and the batter's visible swings and takes. It never reads the batter's internal state.
  - `PitchCraft` (w ∈ [0, 1]) sets how much of that history the model uses: `π̃ = w · π̃_history + (1 − w) · π̃_count_only`. A crafty pitcher sequences against what the batter just saw; a thrower only knows "hitters expect fastballs when behind."
- `E_exec[·]`: expectation over the pitcher's **own believed** command ellipse (a 9-point Gaussian quadrature over yaw and elevation). Wild pitchers therefore avoid aiming at edges they cannot hit.
- `RV`: the learned pitch run-value table (§8). The `surprised` key is what makes deception valuable.
- Lower V is better for the pitcher (run value to the batting team).

**Mixed strategy:** sample from `P(k, target) ∝ exp(−V / τ_sel)` with purpose `PitchSelection`. τ_sel = 0.01 runs [E]. Sampling makes the pitcher unpredictable, which a pure argmin would not be. This is consistent with D1: randomness picks what the pitcher *tries*, never what happens.

**Manager directives** add terms to V before sampling [E weights]:

| Directive | Term |
| :--- | :--- |
| AttackZone | +λ · P_exec(ball) |
| WorkEdges | −λ · edge_proximity(target) for in-zone targets; heavy penalty on the zone's center |
| PitchAround | +λ · P_exec(strike in heart of zone); targets outside the zone favored |
| InduceGrounder | −λ · (expected launch angle < 10° share), from the learned table |
| IntentionalWalk | handled by rules (no pitches thrown) |

**Bootstrap heuristic (M3, before tables exist):** usage-by-count sampling for `k`; targets drawn from count-based zone maps (behind → middle-low, ahead → chase regions). Replaced in M6 by the table-driven rule above.

### 2.5 Fatigue

Per-pitch effort `e = (v_rel / v_max)²` [E] (a max-effort fastball costs 1; a changeup about 0.8). Pickoff throws cost 0.3 [E]; warm-up pitches cost 0.4 [E].

```
f = Σ e / Stamina                        # Stamina: effort budget, "pitch-equivalents"
F_fatigue = 1 + k_σ · max(0, f − f₀)      # f₀ = 0.75, k_σ = 1.2 [E]
v_max(f)  = ArmSpeed · (1 − k_v · max(0, f − f₀))      # k_v = 0.06 [E]
spin(f)   = spin · (1 − k_s · max(0, f − f₀))          # k_s = 0.04 [E]
```

- Between innings, f recovers by `r_inn = 0.04` [E] (short rest).
- Emits `PhysicalLoad` events ([data-formats §4.3](data-formats.md)) with the effort amount.
- Multi-day recovery (`Recovery` attribute) belongs to M7. The model stays effort-based so it can feed physically grounded injuries later (D18).

**Bullpen warm-up** (M6): states cold → warming → ready → overwarmed. Ready after `n_warm` = 20 [E] warm-up pitches and at least 6 min [E] of elapsed game time ([data-formats §2.2](data-formats.md)). Each warm-up pitch adds effort, so a pitcher warmed twice starts the game partially fatigued. Ready decays to cold after 15 min [E] idle.

### 2.6 Holding runners and pickoffs (M5)

The pitcher compares the runner's observed lead with a learned "pickoff value" belief and chooses: deliver, pickoff throw, step-off, or vary hold time. `DeliveryTime` (first move to release) sets steal windows. A slide step trades 0.15 s [E] of delivery time for +20% command σ [E]. Full design deferred to M5 with balks.

---

## 3. Batter

### 3.1 Prior over pitch types

Before each pitch, the batter forms `π_k` over the pitcher's arsenal:

```
π_k ∝ usage_k(count, hand) · seq_k(history) · sit_k · exposure_k
```

- `usage_k`: the batter's belief about the pitcher's usage. v1: the true arsenal usage table (scouting reports are public) [C]. Scouting uncertainty comes later.
- `seq_k`: sequence expectation from this at-bat. After a pitch of type j, `seq_j` = 0.85 [E] (repeats are slightly less expected). A learned sequence table replaces this in M6.
- `sit_k`: the Ambush directive (or a batter's own sit decision at 2-0 / 3-1 [E]) multiplies the chosen type by `s_amb` = 3 [E] and renormalizes. Sitting on a pitch is a gamble the batter knowingly takes.
- `exposure_k`: from in-game adaptation (§3.8).

**Velocity anchoring:** the batter's expected release speed for type k moves toward the last seen pitch's speed: `μ_v,k ← μ_v,k + a_anchor · (v_last − μ_v,k)`, a_anchor = 0.2 [E]. That is the changeup-after-fastball effect, produced by the prior and nothing else.

### 3.2 Perception geometry

- **Eye point:** midpoint between the eyes in the stance, at height 0.86·H [E] and 0.75 m [E] toward the batter's side of the plate center, about at y = 0.3 m [E]. Comes from the batter's box position and handedness.
- **Release estimate:** at release, a noisy estimate of the release point (σ 5 cm [E]) and release speed (Weber fraction 5% [E] on the batter's expectation) starts every hypothesis.
- **Tracking blackout:** batters cannot follow the ball over the last part of its flight. Observations stop when the ball is within `d_blank` = 1.5 m [E] of the eye plane. Anything learned after that is unusable for the swing.

### 3.3 Hypothesis bank

For each pitch type k in the arsenal with `π_k > 0.01`, the batter maintains a **linear-Gaussian filter in plate coordinates**:

- **State:** `s_k = (x_p, z_p, t_p)`, where the ball would cross the plane y = y_mid, and when.
- **Prior:** mean from the batter's predicted trajectory for type k (§1.4 tables: nominal path from the release estimate, plus the expected spin for type k); covariance `Σ_k⁰` = within-type spread (§3.8) plus release-estimate uncertainty propagated through the precomputed sensitivities.
- **Observation model:** each observation is linearized around hypothesis k's current estimate (an extended Kalman filter). The Jacobian `H_k(t)` maps a change in `s_k` to a change in the observation at time t. *Implementation (2026-10-31):* `H_k` is analytic and recomputed at every observation. The predicted position is linear in every state except the arrival time, which enters through the flight progress, so `H_k` costs one nominal-path lookup; a unit test checks it against central finite differences. This replaces the "precomputed once per (pitcher, type) at game start" of the first draft. An earlier version built a finite-difference `H_k` per observation at about three times the cost.
- **Weights:** `w_k ← w_k · N(o_t; ô_k,t, S_k,t)`, the predictive likelihood, then normalized. A batter fooled early stays fooled until the observations diverge enough to move the weights.

**Observations every 10 ms** (purpose `BatterPerception`, `sub_index` = observation number), each with noise from §10:

| Observation | Model | σ |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Angular position (azimuth, elevation) from the eye point | true angles + noise | σ_ang from `Vision` · F_composure · K_perc |
| Time-to-arrival from optical expansion | τ̂ = τ · (1 + ε), ε ~ N(0, w²) | Weber fraction w from `Vision` |
| Spin cue | observed spin direction and rate, as a likelihood over types via each type's spin signature | σ_spin(t_view) = σ_s0 / √(1 + t_view / τ_s); σ_s0 from `SpinRecognition` |

The spin cue updates **only the weights** (it says which type, not where). Angular position and time-to-arrival update both the weights and the per-hypothesis states.

**Posterior:** a Gaussian mixture `Σ_k w_k · N(s_k, Σ_k)`.

**Cost:** about 6 hypotheses × 3-D Kalman updates × ~25 observations: a few microseconds per pitch. No flight integration per pitch.

### 3.4 Commit time

```
t_commit = t̂_arrival − T_swing − T_latency
```

- `t̂_arrival`: weighted mean of the hypotheses' `t_p`.
- `T_swing` from `QuickHands` (§10): start of swing to contact, ≈ 0.15 s average [E].
- `T_latency` = 0.05 s [E]: decision to first motor output.
- The batter re-evaluates `t_commit` each observation and commits when the current time reaches it. For a 95 mph fastball (0.40 s flight), this leaves about 0.2 s of viewing before commit.
- **No swing starts before the commit** (built 2026-11-13, `Swing::start_no_earlier_than`). The planned swing is timed from the believed arrival, and execution noise and the later timing adjustment (§3.6) move it; a start that would fall before the commit is moved to the commit, the whole swing with it, so he meets the ball that much later. A start inside `T_latency` after the commit is allowed (a shorter load). Before this, about 0.15% of swings started up to 18 ms before the batter had decided to swing (64 games: 12 of 8,172 at the commit before, 13 of 8,199 on the game paths of the overrun change; Codex overrun-samples review).

### 3.5 Swing or take

At commit, from the mixture:

**Aim point** `a = (x*, z*, t*)` is the **mean of the highest-weight component** (the dominant mode), not the mixture mean (PLAN §8.1). If two components are close in weight (within 0.1 [E]) and their means are within 3 cm and 5 ms [E], use their weighted average.

**Value of swinging:**

```
EV_swing = Σ_k w_k · W(s_k − a, a, count)
```

`W` is the learned **swing-value table** (§8): the expected run value of a swing aimed at `a` when the ball actually arrives with offset `s_k − a`, integrated over the batter's own believed execution noise. It captures "a 3 cm miss low still makes weak contact; a 10 cm miss is a whiff."

**Value of taking:**

```
EV_take = Σ_k w_k · [ P_k(strike) · RV_strike(count) + (1 − P_k(strike)) · RV_ball(count) ]
```

`P_k(strike)` from component k's Gaussian against the zone (circle-rectangle test of physics spec §10, integrated over the Gaussian).

**Discipline** acts on calibration, not on vision [C]. The batter evaluates `EV_take` with a distorted strike probability:

```
logit(P̃) = c_D · logit(P) + b_D
```

`c_D` (sharpness; 1 is perfectly calibrated) and `b_D` (bias; > 0 means "everything looks like a strike") come from `Discipline` (§10). Low discipline chases because it *believes* bad pitches are strikes and decides crisply on that wrong belief.

**Decision:** swing if `EV_swing − EV_take > θ_dir`, where θ_dir comes from the batting directive:

| Directive | Effect |
| :--- | :--- |
| Normal | θ = 0 |
| Take | no swing this pitch |
| Patient | θ = +0.02 runs [E] |
| Protect | θ = −0.02 runs [E]; swing-value table uses "avoid strikeout" weighting |
| Ambush | prior sharpening (§3.1); θ = −0.01 [E] when the dominant type is the sat-on type |
| Elevate | aim point shifted below the ball's predicted center by δ_elev = 1.0 cm [E] (undercut → lift) |
| Bunt, HitAndRun | separate behaviors: bunt template (M5); hit-and-run forces a swing and favors contact |

### 3.6 Swing model

A **template in a contact-relative frame** (PLAN §8.3): the batter plans where the sweet spot meets the ball, which way it is moving then, and how fast; the hand path and the bat's lag are laid out backward and forward in time from that instant. *v2, specified 2026-11-14 (docs/calibration/2026-11-14-swing-model.md); it replaces v1, a rigid bat turning about a fixed pivot at a fixed angular speed, whose sweet-spot speed grew with reach (64 mph on inside pitches against 84 on outside ones, where Statcast has 71–72 at both).*

**Kinematics: hands on an arc, a bat that lags and releases.** A kinematic double pendulum: the motion a double pendulum's proximal-to-distal sequencing produces is prescribed, not integrated from torques (a justified simplification; a torque-driven model can replace it behind the same interface).
- Everything moves in one **swing plane** Π with unit normal **n̂**, the direction of the swing's angular velocity (up-ish for a right-handed batter, down-ish for a left-hander). The out-of-plane tilt of the first draft is left out.
- The **hands** (the knob) move on a circle of radius ρ about the **hand-arc center C** in Π: `K(t) = C + ρ·ê(φ(t))`, where ê(φ) is the in-plane unit vector at angle φ, measured in the swing's direction of rotation.
- The **bat** points along `â(t) = ê(θ(t))`. The lag is `λ(t) = θ(t) − φ(t)`. A bat station u (from the knob) is at `K + u·â`.
- At the nominal contact time t_c the lag is λ_c = 0 [E]: the hands, the knob and the sweet spot lie on one line from C (the release is complete). Away from t_c the lag is not zero, so the bat's face (⊥ to â) and the sweet spot's path differ: out front the barrel has turned past the hands (hooked), deep it trails them (sliced). That is how early and late contact send the ball off the barrel's path.

**Timing profile.** The hands' and the bat's angular rates are Gaussian bumps about t_c, the hands' peaking before the bat's (τ = t − t_c):
```
φ̇(τ) = k·q·exp(−(τ − τ_h)² / 2w_h²) / exp(−τ_h² / 2w_h²)
θ̇(τ) = k·exp(−(τ − τ_b)² / 2w_b²) / exp(−τ_b² / 2w_b²)
```
so φ̇(0) = k·q and θ̇(0) = k. Angles are their integrals from t_c (closed forms in erf).
- **Speed is set per swing, not per radius.** With λ_c = 0 the sweet spot's speed at t_c is `(q·ρ + u_ss)·k`, so `k = BatSpeed / (q·ρ + u_ss)`: every planned swing meets its aim point at the batter's `BatSpeed`, whatever its reach. A long reach turns more slowly. Statcast (2025 sample, competitive swings) has 70.1 / 72.1 / 71.4 mph on outside / middle / inside pitches; v1 had 83.5 / 72.5 / 64.0.
- Contact away from t_c meets the ball at another speed and another station: on the rising side (late, deep) slower, on the falling side (early, out front) slower again after the bat's peak at τ_b. That is how getting jammed or caught out front weakens contact.
- **Swing start** is when the hands reach 10% of their peak rate: `t_start = t_c + τ_h − 2.146·w_h`, and it is set to `t_c − T_swing` (`QuickHands`). So `τ_h = 2.146·w_h − T_swing`. All four time constants scale with T_swing / 0.150 s.

| Parameter | Value at grade 50 | Status / source |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| T_swing (start to contact) | 0.150 s | `QuickHands` (§10); Blast's time to contact 0.149 s (vendor) |
| w_h, hands' bump width | 0.055 s | [E], with w_b and τ_b chosen so the sweet spot travels about 7.2 ft over the last 150 ms (Statcast swing length, mean 7.2–7.3 ft) |
| τ_h, hands' peak | −0.032 s (derived) | hands peak before contact and slow down as the bat releases (proximal-to-distal) |
| w_b, bat's bump width | 0.055 s | [E] |
| τ_b, bat's peak | +0.010 s | [E]; Statcast's bat speed rises with contact depth out to about 6–12 in in front of a batter's mean |
| q = φ̇/θ̇ at t_c | 0.32 | [E]; hands at about 14 mph when the sweet spot is at 71.5 (ρ = 0.555 m). No sourced MLB hand speed at contact was found |
| λ_c, lag at contact | 0 | [E]. *Proposed and measured (2026-11-14, docs/calibration/2026-11-14-bat-angle-at-contact.md, branch `work/swing-lambda`):* λ_c > 0 turns the face toward the pull side of the path by only about 0.2° per degree (the hands are slow), so it cannot supply Statcast's spray − AD of +7–9°; that offset is the collision's (undercut on a sloped barrel, and sidespin). Kept at 0; no published value found |
| ρ at a middle-middle aim | about 0.30·H (derived from C's lateral position) | [E] |
| ρ range | 0.15·H to 0.46·H | [E] hands in tight to arms fully extended |
| g, reach gain (step 7) | 0.6 | [E], *Proposed 2026-11-15*: the fraction of the hand-radius change from a middle-middle aim that the batter makes; set from Statcast's contact quality by location and in aggregate, checked against swing length by location (step 7) |

Derived at grade 50 (checks, not inputs): hands turn about 76° and the bat about 123° from the start to contact, so the bat starts about 47° behind the hands' line (the first draft's "λ₀ ≈ −90°" was an estimate); the sweet spot's direction turns at about 1.8° per ms at t_c.

**The contact pose (the inverse).** Given the aim point **A** (the sweet spot's position at t_c) and t_c, the plan is closed-form:
1. **Plane tilt** β (Statcast's swing path tilt, the arc's angle to the ground over the last 40 ms): `β = β₀ + β_h·(h − ½) + β_x·x_in` with h the aim's height in the batter's zone (0 at the bottom, 1 at the top) and x_in its distance inside of the plate's center (toward the batter, m). β₀ = 32°, β_h = −9.9° per zone height, β_x = +11° per m inside [E from the Statcast 2025 sample: tilt 34.8 / 32.2 / 28.2° by zone third, 29.7 / 32.3 / 35.1° outside / middle / inside].
2. **Attack angle** α (the sweet spot's elevation at t_c): `α = α₀ + α_h·(h − ½)`, α₀ = 8°, α_h = −8.7° per zone height [E from the same sample, whose means are 11.9 / 7.8 / 6.1° by third (17.4 below the zone, 6.0 above): the slope is the outer thirds' difference over the 2/3 of a zone height between their centers, the level is near the middle third's]. The line gives 10.9 / 8.0 / 5.1° at the thirds' centers, about 1° under the data at both ends, because the data are convex in height; a curve or a least-squares refit would change games and is left open (docs/calibration/2026-11-14-swing-recalibration.md §2.6). Execution's plane-tilt noise adds to α.
3. **Attack direction** ψ (horizontal direction of the sweet spot's velocity, pull-positive): `ψ = ψ₀ + ψ_x·x_in`, ψ₀ = 0, ψ_x = +16.6° per m inside [E from the sample's means: −1.6 / +0.6 / +6.5° outside / middle / inside].
4. **Velocity direction** `V̂ = cos α·ĥ + sin α·ẑ`, ĥ the horizontal unit vector at ψ (toward the pull side for positive ψ).
5. **Plane normal.** Up-normal `ν̂ = cos β·ẑ + sin β·(cos κ·ĥ + sin κ·p̂)` with `p̂ = ẑ × ĥ`, `cos κ = −tan α / tan β` (so ν̂ ⊥ V̂; requires |α| < β, else β is raised to |α| + 1° [E], `swing::TILT_GUARD_MARGIN`), and sin κ chosen so the plane falls away from the batter toward the plate (`sin κ = −side·√(1 − cos²κ)`, side = +1 for a right-handed batter). **n̂ = side·ν̂**.
6. **Bat axis** at t_c: `â_c = V̂ × n̂` (in the plane, ⊥ to the path, pointing knob to tip). The vertical bat angle `asin(â_c·ẑ)` comes out of α and β alone: about −30° at a middle aim, steeper low and flatter high (Blast: −25° to −35° for professionals, vendor).
7. **Hand radius.** C sits on the line through the knob along −â_c: `C = A − (u_ss + ρ)·â_c`, with C's lateral position fixed at `x_C = side·center_x` (center_x = −1.10 m for a right-handed batter [E]: puts ρ near 0.30·H at a middle aim). The radius the aim needs is `ρ_aim = (A.x − x_C)/â_c.x − u_ss`. **Partial reach** *(Proposed 2026-11-15, owner's Q6 branch; docs/calibration/2026-11-15-collision-q-and-poor-contact.md)*: the batter moves his hands only the fraction **g** of the way from the radius a middle-middle aim needs, ρ_ref (x = 0, the middle of his zone, the template's own α, β, ψ there; unclamped), toward ρ_aim: `ρ = ρ_aim − (1 − g)·(ρ_aim − ρ_ref)`, then clamped to the ρ range. C keeps x_C, so the shortfall moves the planned contact along â_c: the ball at A meets the bat at station `u_ss + (1 − g)·(ρ_aim − ρ_ref)` (beyond the clamp, by the clamp's excess as well), **inboard (jammed) on inside pitches and toward the tip on outside ones**, at the same sweet-spot speed. The aim stays on the bat's line, so contact still happens unless the station leaves the bat; the batter's decision model does not know the shortfall. With g = 1 every aim is met at the sweet spot (v2 as first built). Inside pitches are met with the hands in, outside pitches with the arms extended, at the same bat speed (Statcast: the intercept moves 17 in further from the batter from inside to outside with no rise in bat speed).

   *Why (Statcast 2025, twelve dates, competitive swings):* (a) contact quality depends on where across the plate the ball is met: the squared-up ratio of fair balls is 0.847 (72% ≥ 0.8) over the middle 8 in, 0.801 4–10 in inside, **0.699 10+ in inside**, 0.823 4–10 in outside and 0.770 10+ in outside; by the lateral intercept relative to the batter's own mean (−0.96 correlated with pitch location) 0.849 in the middle, 0.764 more than 6 in closer, 0.795 more than 6 in farther. The sim with g = 1 had 0.85–0.87 inside and 0.82 outside: it squared up every location. (b) Real swing length barely changes across the plate (7.65 / 7.36 / 7.25 / 7.17 / 7.25 ft from 10+ in outside to 10+ in inside); with g = 1 the sim's planned swing lengthens by 0.46 ft from the inside third to the outside third (and 1.19 ft in games), so its hands do the reaching that real batters do less of. *g is [E]:* no source measures how far a hitter moves his hands for location; 0.6 is set so the sim's fair-ball contact quality matches the real competitive-swing distribution (ratio ≥ 0.8: 66% against 63%; under 80 mph 23.4% against 23.1%; with q = 0.215 and f_taper 0.27). It moves the planned swing-length difference to 0.28 ft (Statcast's thirds 0.32). *Known residual:* one g fits the aggregate but not the shape by location: the sim's outside pitches lose too much (the contact already sits about 1.3 in toward the tip on average, from the batters' early timing, docs/calibration/2026-11-14-swing-model.md §3) and its slightly-inside pitches too little; separate inside and outside gains (0.45 / 0.85) fit the edges but not the middle, and are left as an owner question.
8. **Contact depth** `y_c(x*) = y₀ + k·x_in`, y₀ = 0.35 m [E], k = 0.125 [E from the sample: intercept depth 29.4 in outside to 31.8 in inside; v1's 0.6 was authored for its radial bat].

C is the center of the hands' effective arc, not the spine; it moves up and down with the pitch's height (the shoulders tilt). Its range is reported in the measurement note.

**Placement** `θ = (planeTilt α, verticalOffset δ_v, reach δ_r, startTime t₀)` keeps its meaning: δ_v moves the whole swing vertically, δ_r moves it along â_c (the ball meets another station), the plane tilt adds to the planned attack angle and the plane is rebuilt, t₀ moves t_c. Execution does change the speed in two ways, and the code does exactly this (`swing::apply_placement`): (1) a plane-tilt deviation rebuilds the contact geometry (the solved ρ and the plane), and the rebuild is normalised to `BatSpeed` again, so the rate scale k is recomputed on the rebuilt geometry and the sweet-spot speed at the nominal contact instant is still `BatSpeed`; (2) the effort draw then multiplies both angular-rate bumps (hands and bat) by `1 + effort`, so the sweet-spot speed at the executed nominal contact instant t_c + t₀ is `BatSpeed × (1 + effort)` (the rates scale linearly, and with them the velocity). δ_v and δ_r are translations of the whole swing and t₀ a shift of time, so none of the three changes any velocity. Without the effort step an implementation would lose the modelled within-batter speed spread (the effort row below).

**Measured against Statcast** (each a check of the geometry, not a fitted value except where marked): sweet-spot speed by horizontal location and by contact depth; swing length (fitted through w_b, τ_b); attack angle and its slope against attack direction (MLB +0.41° per degree); attack direction's slope against intercept depth (MLB +1.48° per inch out front); swing path tilt (set from the sample); vertical bat angle by height (Blast, vendor). The tables are in docs/calibration/2026-11-14-swing-model.md.

**Execution noise** (purpose `SwingExecution`) on θ, from §10:

| Component | σ source |
| :--- | :--- |
| δ_v (vertical offset) | `BatControl` |
| α (plane tilt) | `BatControl`: 1.5 rad per m of δ_v's σ [E] (`motor::PLANE_TILT_PER_M`; 1.0° at grade 50; an M2 engineering value, unsourced) |
| δ_r (reach) | `BatControl` × 0.7 [E] (`motor::REACH_PER_BAT_CONTROL`) |
| t₀ (timing) | `Timing` |
| effort (relative sweet-spot speed) | 4.5% [E] (`motor::EFFORT_SIGMA`; purpose `SwingEffort`, its own stream). Statcast 2025 sample: within-batter SD of competitive bat speed 3.2 mph at 71.7 for swings met within 3 in of the batter's own mean depth over the middle of the plate (4.3 mph without that restriction, which includes depth and location). v2 sets every planned swing to `BatSpeed`, so without this term every grade-50 swing would be 71.5 mph |

All widen with F_composure. The global knobs split them in two (*2026-11-14, the refit after PLAN D20*):

- **`K_swing` (the miss scale; `Knobs::swing_miss`)** multiplies the σ of δ_v, α, δ_r and t₀: where and when the bat arrives. This is calibration-targets §2.3's `K_swingplane`. The batter's model of his own execution (`OwnExecution` in the swing decision, §3.5) uses the same scaled σ_v and σ_t, so a batter whose misses widen knows it.
- **`K_effort` (the effort scale; `Knobs::swing_effort`)** multiplies the effort σ alone: how fast the bat arrives. It stays 1 in calibration, because the 4.5% is sourced (Statcast's within-batter SD); it exists so the two can be swept apart. The batter's decision model has no effort term and does not read it.

The two are separate physical inputs and were already drawn apart: the placement from the `SwingExecution` stream in the fixed order (δ_v, α, δ_r, t₀), the effort from its own `SwingEffort` stream. Splitting the scales changes neither stream nor its draw order, so with both knobs at one shared value every game replays exactly as with the old single knob. *Why:* the old single knob widened the effort with the miss, and at 1.8 the effort tail (`1 + effort` on both angular-rate bumps) put the fastest batted ball at 128 mph against the "none over 125" band (collision note §8.3); the miss alone carries K and contact quality. **M2 measures** the offset → (exit velocity, launch angle) map (physics spec §8.3); these σ values are then set so that a perfectly perceived pitch produces a realistic spread of contact quality. Perception error should dominate execution error for typical pitches [C].

**Mid-swing adjustment:** after commit, observations continue (until the blackout). The aim point updates from the posterior; θ moves toward the new inverse at bounded rates:
- δ_v and δ_r: ≤ `v_adj` from `Adjustability` (§10).
- Timing: phase rate may change by ≤ ±10% [E] (`SwingTemplate::adj_phase_frac`).
- Adjustment stops at `t_cut` = 60 ms [E] before t* (PLAN range 50–80 ms; `SwingTemplate::adj_cutoff_s`).

**Check swing:** if, before phase `s_abort` = 0.45 [E], the updated `EV_swing − EV_take` falls below `−θ_check` = −0.03 runs [E] (`batter::CHECK_THETA_RUNS`), the batter brakes: phase rate decays to zero over 40 ms [E]. Whether it counts as a swing is judged by the rules engine from the bat's pose (`rules-spec.md`). The swing model only reports the maximum bat rotation reached. *v1 as built:* the abort point is a fraction of the swing's start-to-contact time, 0.6 (`SwingTemplate::abort_time_frac`), not the phase 0.45; under v1's Gaussian speed bump the two differ, and changing it is a behavior change to validate.

**Bunt** (M5): a separate near-static template; the batter positions the bat on the predicted path and applies the bunt-grip COR multiplier (physics spec §8.4).

### 3.7 Body and hit-by-pitch evasion

- Body is a capsule set (head, torso, upper and lower arms, thighs, shins) scaled by height, posed from the stance and the swing phase.
- **Evasion:** if the posterior predicts the ball passing within 0.25 m [E] of the head or torso, the batter starts an evasion after 0.15 s [E] of latency: torso rotation and a step back, limited to 2 m/s [E] capsule speed. A committed swing suppresses evasion.
- Hit-by-pitch is decided by the world (capsule contact). The batter's belief only decides whether to move.

### 3.8 In-game adaptation

Each batter keeps a memory per opposing pitcher and pitch type: `n_k`, the number of type-k pitches seen this game (including from the on-deck circle at reduced weight 0.3 [E]).

- **Within-type spread** shrinks with exposure: `σ_k(n) = σ_∞ + (σ_0 − σ_∞) · exp(−η · n_k)`, η from `Adaptation` (§10). σ_0 is the batter's spread before seeing the pitcher; σ_∞ = 0.5·σ_0 [E].
- **Model correction:** the batter's expected spin for type k moves toward the observed spin cue of each pitch seen: learning rate η / (1 + n_k).
- This is the mechanism for the times-through-the-order penalty (PLAN §8.6). Nothing scripts it.
- Memory is part of `SaveState` ([data-formats §7.1](data-formats.md)).

---

## 4. Catcher

The receiving, blocking, steal defense and foul-tip capture below are the catcher-agent design. The client already animates receiving (player-visuals §8).

- **Receiving:** the catcher sets the target glove position at the intended pitch location (signaled by the pitcher's intent; the catcher and pitcher share the target, a battery convention [C]). Catching is a standard capture (§5.4) with the catcher's `Hands`. With ABS, framing does not exist.
- **Blocking:** for a predicted pitch in the dirt, the catcher moves glove and body capsules toward the predicted bounce path. `Blocking` (§10) sets lateral reach speed and reaction. A blocked ball deflects physically off the body capsules (physics spec §9.4). Wild pitches and passed balls emerge.
- **Throwing on steals:** transfer from `Transfer` (catcher distribution), throw from `ArmStrength` and `ArmAccuracy`. **Pop time is a derived display value** (transfer + throw flight to second base), not a separate attribute [C].
- **Foul tips:** a foul tip is "caught" only if the catcher's capture test succeeds on the deflected ball.

---

## 5. Fielders

### 5.1 Perception after contact

After its reaction time, a fielder predicts intercepts with its own flight model, a typical-spin belief and a noisy read of position and velocity. `Sim::track_now` uses the tracking estimate from his first look on the first flight until someone holds the ball, across its bounces (since 2026-11-21, *Across the bounces* below). The snapshot path in `read_prediction_k` handles every other read (before his first look: anticipation, the jump; a man who first looked after the first bounce; after a wall or a touch; the runners' reads), with error stretched along the line of sight. **No read puts the ball below the ground (2026-11-21):** a snapshot read of a ball seen rolling takes its height (the ball's radius) and vertical velocity (zero) as known; any other read's height is held at the ball's radius at least. So every believed path meets the ground. (Before, the height noise could put a ball rolling at 0.15 m at −0.24 m with an upward velocity read, and the believed path never came down: seed 51001 play 71, the [diagnosis note](calibration/2026-11-20-saved-plays-51001.md) §2.)

**Typical spin, built 2026-11-12 (`sim/crates/bb_game/src/live.rs`; its one-dimensional table, `typical_batted_spin_rpm`, was replaced 2026-11-19 by the tables below, "The unseen spin"):** a ball straight off the bat is predicted with the spin the contact model typically gives at the launch angle the fielder estimates (his read of the velocity with the gravity drop since contact added back): the medians of `sim/crates/bb_game/examples/batted_spin.rs` by 10° bin, from −7,060 rpm (topspin) at −25° to +6,470 rpm at 55° (3,290 at 25°, 4,800 at 35°). After a bounce, a deflection, or a carom off the wall: 600 rpm topspin (in the snapshot read; a fielder tracking the ball across its bounces uses the spin his own bounce law gives it, *Across the bounces* below). The interim model had assumed 1,800 rpm backspin for every rising ball, while the contact model gives fly balls 3,300–6,500 rpm, so every fly carried past the fielder's prediction and his target crept back all flight; with free turning that cost little, with momentum it cost about 1.5 runs per team-game. The table is a belief and must be refit when the contact model changes (R3 asks whether its spin is high).

**The typical spin refit, and a better launch angle (2026-11-15, docs/calibration/2026-11-15-outfield-read.md):** the table above was fitted on the v1 swing; swing v2 (2026-11-14) gives flies about 60% less backspin (medians 1,916 rpm at 25°, 3,013 at 35°, 3,819 at 45°, 4,558 at 55°; `sim/crates/bb_game/examples/batted_spin.rs`, 48 games), so every fly was believed to carry, and read 3.2 m too deep 2 s before landing on 25–35° flies with no noise at all. The table is refit. Two more parts of the same belief were biased the same way and are rebuilt: the launch angle the table is looked up at is no longer atan2(v_z + g·s, v_h) of the velocity *now* (drag has made that too steep by mid-flight) but the angle at which his read of the velocity, flown back the s seconds to the plate with his own physics (the plate cue's back-flight, starting from the old estimate's spin), leaves the bat; and the believed spin now is the table's launch spin decayed as that same back-flight undoes it (`AeroParams::tau100`), not the launch spin. With no noise the read's bias 2 s out is now −0.1 to +0.3 m. The same belief serves runners' reads and the fielders' shared view of a loose ball (`public_path`). The plate cue's spin allowance (`PLATE_SPIN_M`) was re-measured with the new belief (`sim/crates/bb_game/examples/plate_cue.rs`: 0.6 m at 0.5 s, 2.0 m at 1 s, 4–8 m at 2 s, as before): what the observer cannot see is the spread of spins and their side component, not the median, so it stays.

**Refit on the integration of the held branches (2026-11-15, docs/calibration/2026-11-15-integration-of-the-held-branches.md):** the sourced collision efficiency, the inboard barrel falloff and partial reach change how balls leave the bat, so the table was fitted again by the same procedure (48 games): 1,909 rpm at 25°, 2,949 at 35°, 3,770 at 45°, 4,504 at 55°, under 3% from the swing v2 table at 25°–55°. The staleness test now also groups by exit speed (Codex outfield-read review P3-1): a belief by launch angle alone cannot follow it, and on this contact model it is 11–29% too high for contact under 85 mph and 5–16% too low over it, by median, in three samples. *Open, not built:* a belief by angle and exit speed (the observer has his estimate of the launch speed from the same back-flight). A no-draw landing-bias check was tried as the staleness test and rejected: 2 s before landing the read has closed and a table 60% high moved the mean miss there by under 0.5 m; 3 s out the refit table reads flies 0.5–1.3 m deep on average, a stale one 1.0–2.6 m.

**The read closes as the ball comes down (built 2026-11-15; docs/calibration/2026-11-15-outfield-read.md):** real fielders are continuously coupled to the ball, and the information about where it lands sharpens late in the flight (optical acceleration cancellation, Chapman 1968; confirmed for front–back movement by Fink, Foo & Warren 2009, J. Vision 9(13):14). With a threshold on the optical acceleration he can perceive, the landing miss he can tell apart shrinks as the square of the time left. The interim read shrank only with viewing time and his distance to the ball, so 1 s before landing its depth spread was still 2–3 m (σ), against a planning reach of 0.7 m. Now, for a ball on its first flight off the bat, every term of the read error (position and velocity, both axes) is also scaled by c = min(1, T/`READ_CLOSE_S`), T the time the ball takes to come down from its height now with its vertical velocity now, in a vacuum (both are what he sees now, nothing of its future). The velocity term, flown over T and scaled by T, gives the T² of the threshold argument; the position term closes linearly. `READ_CLOSE_S` = 3.0 s [E], fitted to the owner's band (a routine fly is caught 90–99% of the time by fielding ability): on the note's routine flies grade 50 catches .960, grade 20 .917, grade 80 .983 (120 games each). The error draws stay fixed per fielder per play, so his estimate converges smoothly on the ball rather than jittering (the Ornstein–Uhlenbeck process above is still not built). What is kept: a high fly's larger early error (its velocity error is flown over a longer time, and the closing starts only once it is within 3 s of coming down as he sees it), and a wrong first step on a ball over his head (the first reads are as before). After a bounce or a deflection the closing does not apply.

**Interim read values (refitted 2026-11-13 with the plate cue, docs/calibration/2026-11-13-plate-cue.md):** position error 3.5 m at 30 m for outfielders (was 3.0, refitted with momentum 2026-11-12; 0.3 m infield); velocity error `READ_VEL_FRAC` 0.04 of speed per axis (was an unnamed 0.06). *Since 2026-11-15 both, and τ_read, are per-fielder values (`FielderAttrs::read_sigma0`, `read_vel_frac`, `tau_read`) set from his `ReadSkill` grade (§10, `FielderAttrs::with_read`); these are the grade-50 values.*

**Where the interim read's error came from (measured 2026-11-13, `sim/crates/bb_game/examples/read_error.rs`, docs/calibration/2026-11-13-popup-read-and-calls.md):** before the plate cue below, for an outfielder at his first look, one σ of the position-depth term moved his predicted landing point 22–24 m at every launch angle; the velocity terms 3–6 m; the typical-spin belief and the coarser flight step under 1.5 m. The interim model gave the observer no knowledge that the ball started at the plate (22 m of depth error on a ball 16 m from home, 10 m of lateral error on the same ball).

**The unseen spin, and a belief by exit speed (2026-11-19, fly-read job; [fly-read note](calibration/2026-11-19-fly-read.md); `believed_flight`, `SPIN_BACK_RPM`, `SPIN_REST_RPM`, `FlightEnv::unseen_spin`):** with no read error at all, a runner's first look 0.5 s off the bat read 25–50° flies long across the population, a median 2.5–2.8 m on deep flies with even the 10th percentile long, and one deep fly in ten as a home run (Opus catch-fixes2 review P2-1); the fielders' first look shares the model and read the same. The cause was the spin, isolated by flying the true position and velocity at 0.5 s with each part swapped: with the true spin vector the read lands on the true point; with the believed pure backspin it lands +2.7 m long; with the believed backspin's lift but the drag of the true whole spin, +0.2 to +0.6 m. Drag grows with the whole spin (`cdspin` × rpm), and a batted ball's spin is far from pure backspin: on deep flies about 40% of |ω| is side spin and 40% gyro, so a 35° ball has a median 4,700 rpm in all and 2,900 of it backspin. The belief held only the backspin, so it flew every ball with too little drag. It now has two parts, both by launch angle × exit speed (10° × 10 mph bins, bilinear; he reads both from the launch his back-flight gives, and spin depends on both: at 35°, a median 4,900 rpm in all at 80–90 mph and 3,700 from 100 mph up):
- **backspin**, the median of balls launched toward the field (within the foul lines as he sees it go; fouls are mishits whose spin pulled the old medians), as before about the horizontal axis square to the flight;
- **the unseen spin** (side and gyro, whose axis he cannot tell): it counts in his drag, √(|ω|² + unseen²), and gives no Magnus force, since its direction is unknown and its sideways push averages out (`FlightEnv::unseen_spin`, zero in the world; held constant over the flight, both scaled by the decay since contact as before). It is fitted, not a median: per cell, the value with which that cell's balls, each flown from its own launch with the belief as it is looked up, carry as far as they truly do in the median (open air). Each ball's own unseen spin varies by about ±35%, and carry is not linear in it, so the median unseen spin flies 1.5–2 m short. Zero at 5° and below and on pop-ups under 80 mph, where the backspin alone does not carry too far: their belief is as before, apart from the backspin's refit by exit speed.

*Built first, and dropped:* the unseen spin as a spin vector along the launch direction (gyro). It adds the drag, but once the ball turns over it gives a sideways Magnus force of one sign, so a straightaway fly was believed to curve 7.7 m toward left. The tables are from `sim/crates/bb_game/examples/batted_spin.rs` (96 games); refit when the contact model changes.

**The plate cue, built 2026-11-13 (`position_from_plate`, `plate_weighing`; docs/calibration/2026-11-13-plate-cue.md):** while a batted ball is still on its first flight off the bat, s seconds after contact, the observer knows where it was hit and when. Drag, Magnus and gravity act on the ball's velocity alone, not on where it is, so he flies his read of its velocity back s seconds with his own physics and the typical spin above: a ball going that fast now, having left the contact point s seconds ago, must be *there*. He weighs that position against his direct read in three dimensions by how good each is (precision-weighted, a Kalman update): the direct read with the error above (σ_p per lateral axis, ×3 in depth; across his line of sight one draw moves it sideways by σ and up by σ/2 together, as the simulator samples it); the plate's with his velocity read's error carried over s (σ_v·s, ×1.5 in depth, the same coupled draw across) plus the spin he cannot see, `PLATE_SPIN_M` = 1.5 m per axis at 1 s, growing as s^1.5 [E] (`sim/crates/bb_game/examples/plate_cue.rs`: flown back with the true spin the position is exact; with typical spin it misses by a median 0.6 m at 0.5 s, 2.3 m at 1 s, 4–9 m at 2 s; line drives that stay up 3–4 s, 18–23 m). Early the plate wins in every direction, so an outfielder's first-look landing error is about 7 m (one σ), nearly all of it his velocity read: how hard and at what angle the ball was hit. As the ball comes nearer his direct read takes over (the position terms move the landing point 2–5 m in the last two seconds of a pop-up 30–40 m in front of him). After a bounce, a deflection or a wall, only the direct read is used. The spec's launch-state form (errors on exit speed, launch angle and spray, the atlas) is this, expressed through the velocity read; the atlas is not built. *Not kept:* a learned prior of how far balls at each launch angle have typically gone pulled every hard-hit ball toward the typical one (liner triples .069 → .086 in 512 games), and a depth-only version left the lateral error in place.

**The tracking read (from his first look on the first flight until the ball is held, in `sim/crates/bb_game/src/live.rs`).** *Why:* each read used to be rebuilt from the ball's state now plus four error draws fixed for the play, weighed against the plate cue; nothing he had seen was kept. On seed 23080 play 8 the left fielder's depth draw is −2.8σ (near its ±4σ truncation). While the plate cue carried his depth (gain 1.0 to 1.5 s) he was right; as its weight fell (0.8 at 2.4 s, 0.3 at 3.6 s) the same −2.8σ, ×3 in depth, took over every look, and his landing point went +11 m deep at mid-flight (with no draws, within 0.3 m throughout), then back as the closing shrank it. Two seconds of good looks counted for nothing (Codex outfield-read review P2-1). A fielder watching a ball keeps a running estimate and corrects it by what each new look adds.

- **What he holds:** an estimate of the ball's position, velocity and the extra acceleration his flight model is missing (the curvature of the spin he cannot see), x = [r̂, v̂, â], with its covariance P in 3×3 blocks (`Track`), started at his first look (below). A bounce does not end it; a wall carom or a new ball (a touch, a throw that comes down loose) does, and then the snapshot read above applies (below, *Across the bounces*).
- **Between looks** he runs it on with his own physics plus â: one step of `FlightEnv` from (r̂, v̂) with the spin he believes for it (`believed_flight`: the typical spin decayed since contact, with the unseen spin's drag) and â as an extra constant acceleration (`FlightEnv::extra`, zero in the world), and P by F = [[I, Δ, Δ²/2], [0, I, Δ], [0, 0, I]] plus â drifting as white noise of intensity `CURVE_DRIFT`. â's prior is `CURVE_PRIOR` = 2·`PLATE_SPIN_M` = 3 m/s² per axis: the plate cue's spin allowance (1.5 m at 1 s) as a constant acceleration, ½·σ·1². Its drift: the Magnus force, and so its error, roughly halves over a fly's six seconds as the ball slows, (σ/2)²/6 per second [E]. *Built first, and rejected:* a velocity random walk of the same allowance (q = 3·`PLATE_SPIN_M`²) let the velocity wander 2.6 m/s per √s, more than the looks could hold; the estimate never settled. A spin error is a near-constant extra acceleration, and as a state it is learned: an unexpected carry shows up as a run of looks on one side, and â follows them. His read is flown forward with â as well.
- **Looks:** every `LOOK_S` = 0.1 s [E] from contact plus his reaction until someone holds the ball, it hits a wall or is touched (a throw in the air is read by its receiver, §5.5), whatever he is doing and however often he replans (replans read the estimate; they add no information). Each look sees the ball's position and velocity. *Geometry:* his eyes are `EYE_H` = 1.7 m above his feet; d is the three-dimensional distance from them to the ball and u the unit line of sight. Across the line of sight a look is good to σ_p = σ0·(d/30 m) per axis (an angle times the range); along it, the range, to `DEPTH_STRETCH` (3) times that. Velocity: σ_v = vel_frac·|v| across, 1.5× along. A ball high above him is far away (its d includes its height) and its hard direction is steep, mostly height, which is what decides when and so where it comes down; a liner's hard direction is level depth. So a high fly is harder at the top of its flight by the geometry of what he sees, and harder early because the landing point is its velocity times a longer time left: no dial says so.
- **How much a look is worth:** the first look is the snapshot read's first read, its σ and its shrink (velocity as seen, position where a ball going that fast must be, having left the plate, weighed with what he sees: the plate cue). For a fly the shrink is 1 at the first look. For a ball about to come down it is the closing of `READ_CLOSE_S` (T/3 s): a grounder before its first bounce is read as sharply as before. *Built first with the plain σ:* grounder hits rose .291 → .308 [.295 → .306], the infielders' first read of a hard grounder 10× worse than it had been; with the shrink, .298. Every later look is `LOOK_SCALE` = 0.2 times that σ at the same distance: an outfielder's look is good to 0.7 m at 30 m across his line of sight (1.3°), 2.1 m along it. *Built first, and rejected:* each look √(τ_read/`LOOK_S`) = 2.45× worse, which carries the old viewing-time law σ/√(1 + view/τ_read) for a still ball. That is 16° across per look, and the read never settled (on seed 23080 play 8, 10–20 m off at 1–3 s). The old read's late precision came from the closing factor, not from τ_read. So τ_read takes no part in the tracking read; `ReadSkill` scales σ0 and vel_frac, and through them every look.
- **Noise:** each look's error is fresh (four truncated normals across and along, position and velocity, plus the second across axis: six, from a new appended purpose `FielderLook`, sub_index = the look's number in the play, counted on across his tracks so that no look's draws repeat) plus a persistent part: `TRACK_BIAS_FRAC` [E] times the play's fixed draws (`FielderPerception` sub 0, as before) at a single look's σ: the plain σ on later looks, and on the first look its shrink as well, like the fresh part (Codex tracking-read review P2-2: unshrunk, it left a grounder's first read up to eight times worse than the old first read; `a_grounders_first_look_is_read_as_sharply_as_the_snapshot_read`). *Decided, and why:* with fresh noise alone a fielder averages his way to a near-perfect read and never misjudges a ball; with the fixed draws alone (as before) looks repeat the same error and add nothing. A real fielder does both: successive looks average out what jitters, and a whole ball can still be misjudged (the sun, a deceptive spin, a bad first impression). He does not know his persistent error, but he knows he has one (below, *the persistent error as a state*). `LOOK_SCALE` and `TRACK_BIAS_FRAC` = 0.2 are fitted once together to the owner's band (a routine fly caught 90–99% of the time by ability), not to the run total: on seeds 0–99 (scale, bias → routine flies caught) 0.5, 0.2 → .906; 0.3, 0.2 → .941; 0.2, 0.2 → .967; 0.3, 0.1 → .986 (docs/calibration/2026-11-15-tracking-read.md). *Refitted 2026-11-17* with the persistent error carried as a state (below): `TRACK_BIAS_FRAC` 0.6, `LOOK_SCALE` kept at 0.2. On seeds 0–99 (bias → routine flies caught at grade 50), fitted before the full Jacobian: 0.2 → .986, 0.6 → .967, 1.0 → .946. With it, 0.6 gives .979, still in the band, so it was not refitted (docs/calibration/2026-11-17-track-bias.md). Before that change, with the persistent part alone (no fresh noise) the owner's play is read within 1.1 m throughout; with no noise at all, within 1.5 m.
- **The persistent error as a state (2026-11-17, owner's saved play seed 40580 play 15; docs/calibration/2026-11-17-track-bias.md).** *Why:* the first version gave the filter only the fresh part as look noise and let the persistent part in as if it were fresh on every look. Its covariance shrank as if N looks averaged it down by √N, which a repeated error cannot do, so it grew sure of a wrong line and stopped listening: on play 15 the centre fielder's read stayed 2.2–2.5 m off laterally from 1 s to 0.6 s before the ball came down, 8 m from him, and he stopped 1.6 m short of it (an inside-the-park home run). With the persistent part removed he read it to 7 cm. Over 120 plays of a routine fly the mean of (lateral error)²/(his own σ)² was 26.7 where an honest filter gives 1 (`the_tracking_read_reports_a_covariance_that_covers_its_error`). *What it is, physically:* the persistent part is a misjudged angle across his line of sight and a misjudged fraction of the range along it (its σ is a fraction of σ0·d/30 m, both for position and, as a fraction of speed, for velocity). In metres it shrinks as the ball comes in and it turns with his line of sight, so the looks up close carry less of it than the far ones did; that is how a real outfielder's eye works (a ball misjudged off the bat is set right as it comes down, not believed harder). *Built:* the filter carries the four persistent components (position along and across, velocity along and across, in units of their σ) as states, b, beside [r̂, v̂, â]: thirteen states, prior b ~ N(0, I) independent of the ball (his velocity read's first look carries it too, so v̂ and r̂ start correlated with it), constant between looks. A look at position sees r + H_b·b + fresh noise, with H_b the two directions (along ×`DEPTH_STRETCH`, across level) times `TRACK_BIAS_FRAC`·σ0·d/30 m at that look (×the first look's shrink, as the world draws it); velocity likewise with vel_frac·|v̂| and ½·`DEPTH_STRETCH` along. *Chosen over widening each look's covariance by the persistent part:* that is cheaper but still treats the persistent error as independent between looks, so N looks still average it down by √N; it would be less wrong, not honest. With b a state the filter's covariance is the right one for the model the world draws from (a Schmidt–Kalman/augmented filter), and the geometry decides how fast the bias is learned: a far, steady line of sight cannot tell it from the ball, a closing one can. The geometry the filter uses (d, the line of sight, the directions across it, and |v| for the velocity σ) is now his own prediction before the look (on the first look, the plate cue's position and the velocity as seen), not the ball's true position: an extended Kalman filter's linearisation (agents never see world state; the world's draw still uses the true geometry). *Built first, and rejected:* the geometry from the look itself. A look is up to several metres off in range, so it misjudged the scale of his own persistent error by up to a fifth, and that slip is itself persistent and unmodelled: at `TRACK_BIAS_FRAC` 0.6 the mean (lateral error)²/σ² was 2.4 again, and his doubt half the miss 1 s out. From his prediction they are 0.9–1.2, and the doubt matches the miss. *Revised (Codex track-bias review P2-1):* linearising about the prediction is not enough on its own. The persistent error's size depends on the range and its directions on the line of sight (and, for velocity, on the speed), so once he has learned a non-zero b the look's Jacobian in r and v is not I (for b0 = −2 at 0.6 it is 0.58 I). The update used I, and with that bias known, one isolated look's error²/variance was 2.4 across his line of sight. The update now uses the full Jacobian (below), and `a_look_with_a_learned_persistent_error_reports_a_covariance_that_covers_its_error` checks it: three geometries, three non-zero biases, and every sight direction of position and velocity within 0.85–1.15 (0.92–1.11 measured). No new draws, and the draw order is unchanged.
- **The update** is an extended Kalman update with each look, position then velocity (`Track::see`, `Look::expect`). What he expects to see is h(r, v, b): the ball's position, or velocity, moved by his persistent error at the σ and in the directions of a look from his eyes there. It is evaluated, with the fresh part's covariance, at the estimate as it stands, so the velocity look is worked out from the position the position look has just corrected. H is h's full Jacobian in r, v and b, by central differences (`LOOK_JAC_M` = 1 mm or 1 mm/s; h is linear in b). The covariance is dense, 13 × 13, with a 3 × 3 `Mat3` innovation and its inverse; math goes through `bb_math::m`. Deterministic, replayable, no matrix library.
- **His read** is the estimate run on to now (at most a look's interval) and flown forward with his own physics, believed spin and â, as the snapshot read is flown: the plans, calls and intercepts take it as they took the old read. **His doubt** (the landing σ along his line of sight that calls weigh, `CALL_SURE_SIGMAS`) is the whole of P, position, velocity and â with every cross term, carried to where he believes it comes down by his own physics (linearised: each of the nine components moved and flown). *Since 2026-11-17* what his persistent error can do is already in that covariance (the filter carries it as a state), so the old extra term (a one-σ persistent draw in each of its four directions, flown, added on top) is gone; it counted it twice. *Revised (Codex tracking-read review P2-1):* the first version carried only the position and velocity blocks, on the claim that â's share came through its covariance with them; it does not, and a belief uncertain only in â reported no doubt at all. Checked by `a_tracked_doubt_carries_the_learned_acceleration` (a belief uncertain only in â has the doubt a ±1 m/s² change gives the landing point) and `the_tracking_doubt_matches_the_spread_of_tracked_reads` (balls from games, with their real spins: his RMS doubt against the RMS miss of his read, 2 s and 1 s out, within a factor of 1.5; it is 1.1–1.4× the miss).
- Before his first look he has no track: plans made before it (anticipation, the jump) use the snapshot read as before.
- **Across the bounces (2026-11-21; owner's saved play 71 of seed 51001; [bounce-track note](calibration/2026-11-21-bounce-track.md)).** *Why:* the track used to end at the first bounce, and every read after it was a fresh snapshot carrying his whole fixed draw, times `DEPTH_STRETCH` along his line of sight, on every replan. In play 71 the right fielder's read jumped from 4 m off to 15 m off at the bounce he had just watched from 26 m (and 0.24 m below the ground), and he turned for the wall. A fielder watching a ball sees it bounce and roll; his read goes on as it comes, and is no worse after a bounce than just before it.
  - **What he sees of the ball's phase.** Whether the ball is in the air or rolling, the moment it touches the ground, and that it has come to rest are seen (the ball and its shadow meet, it stops); the track's model follows them. Its phases: *first flight* (off the bat, until the first bounce or a wall), *a hop* (in the air after a bounce), *rolling*.
  - **A seen bounce continues the track.** At the moment t_b he sees it touch the ground: (1) *it is on the ground*: his estimate is updated with its height = the ball's radius at t_b, σ `BOUNCE_Z_M` = 1 cm [E, numerical: the height at a seen touch is the ground's; the σ only keeps the update well conditioned]. Through the covariance this also corrects what he misjudged together with the height: on a liner coming at him, the range. (2) *It bounces as he believes a bounce goes*: his own bounce law (physics-spec §9.2, no tilt) on the field's surface under his estimate, applied to his estimate, with the spin he believes it meets the ground with: on the first bounce the typical spin of the first flight, after it the spin his own bounce law left it with at the bounce before (a hopping grounder carries thousands of rpm of topspin; *built first with the flight model's 600 rpm after a bounce*, his predicted hops were so wrong that the gate below fired 115 times on hops in games 0–9; with the spin carried, it fires on hops about once in six games: 34 in games 0–199). The covariance is carried through the law's Jacobian in the incoming velocity (central differences), and what he cannot foresee of a bounce is added to the outgoing velocity's: the tilt of the bounce normal, at the surface's roughness σ (physics-spec §9.1, §9.3: the field is known to him), and the spin he cannot see, `BOUNCE_SPIN_RPM` = 1,000 rpm σ about each level axis [E, unsourced: about half a liner's typical backspin; the honesty measurement of the note checks it]. (3) The learned extra acceleration â goes back to its prior: it was the first flight's unseen spin. (4) His persistent error b and its covariance with the ball carry over: it is his eye's error for the play, not the ball's. Nothing is drawn: the bounce is a deterministic update of his belief.
  - **A hop** is run on as a flight with the spin his bounce law left it with (no unseen-spin drag), â from `CURVE_PRIOR` drifting by `CURVE_DRIFT`, and looked at as before.
  - **Rolling.** Its height (the ball's radius) and vertical velocity (zero) are known: they leave the state, with no variance. Between looks the estimate rolls on by the world's rolling law (`bb_physics::ground::roll_from`, the law `batted_path` rolls a ball by), set where he believes it began to roll: slowing at μ_r·g of the surface there, or, if it would roll into the wall, at the even rate that stops it there. (The world keeps a roll's first surface for the whole roll; physics-spec §9.2's switch at the dirt–grass edge is not built.) What his law may still have wrong (the surface, the wall) he learns as â, its level part along the roll changing how fast he believes it slows: prior σ `ROLL_SLOW_PRIOR` = 0.6 m/s² per level axis [E, unsourced: grass's μ_r less dirt's, times g], drift `ROLL_DRIFT` = (σ/2)² over 2 s [E]. P by the roll step's Jacobian in level position, velocity and â (central differences on open ground: a ball that stops within a step no longer moves with them). *Built first* with the surface under his estimate and a velocity random walk: a ball that rolled from the dirt onto the grass, or into the wall, slowed other than he believed, and 2 s after the bounce his error²/σ² was 13. A ball he sees at rest has its velocity and â known (zero). A look's error across his line of sight includes its vertical part, so with the height known a look of a rolling ball also measures his range through the angle below the horizon, at a look's angular precision; at grade 50 (1.3°) that is weaker than the range look itself at every distance (at 20 m about 5.6 m against 1.4 m), so it adds little, and the filter weighs it as it is.
  - **The velocity look off the first flight** is no better than that of a ball going `LOOK_VEL_FLOOR_MPS` = 3 m/s [E, unsourced: a speed read is a fraction of the speed only while the ball moves well], in the world's draw and in what he knows of his looks. Without it a ball coming to rest was read exactly, and the filter's gain on it ran away (an estimate 30 m off in one look).
  - **The gate.** After a bounce, a look his estimate cannot explain at all (its normalised innovation squared, three degrees of freedom, beyond `TRACK_GATE` = 50 [E: χ² passes 50 once in 10¹⁰ looks of an honest track]) means his belief of that block (position or velocity) has gone wrong: the bounce and the roll are far from linear (a wall stop, the surface the world kept, a stop his estimate made early, a spin he did not expect). He keeps the track and lets go of that block (`Track::let_go`): it is taken again from the look itself, uncorrelated with the rest, its variance per axis the look's squared error (so the look's own σ, which scales with the speed, is worked out at the speed it shows); â goes back to its prior; a velocity let go also widens his position by what it may have carried it wrong since his last look (that error over `LOOK_S`). Then the look is taken again. Only if even then it cannot be explained (never in games 0–199) does he fall back on the snapshot read. On the first flight there is no gate (that read is as it was). In games 0–199 it lets go 801 times in 248 fielders' plays (107 plays): 766 velocities on rolling balls, 34 on hops, 1 position. *Built first, and replaced (Opus bounce-track review P2-1):* the gate ended the track for the snapshot read: 272 times in games 0–199 (33 on hops), mostly when his position was still good (median 0.68 m off) and only his speed was wrong; the snapshot read then was sometimes far worse (a ball at rest against the wall read 19 m beyond it, seed 4 play 19; 93 m at worst). With the let-go, the worst read in the 3 s after the bounce, over those fielders' plays, is a median 1.6 m (it was 5.5 m), at worst 14 m (93 m), and every read over 5 m is a fielder 80–100 m from the ball.
  - **A rolling ball seen moving** keeps a level velocity σ of at least a look's at the floor speed (`read_vel_frac`·`LOOK_VEL_FLOOR_MPS`, 0.12 m/s at grade 50), however sure his estimate is that it has slowed or stopped (Opus review P3-2: an estimate that stopped itself early had no velocity variance left, so no look could start it again). Only a ball seen at rest has its velocity known.
  - **Perfect phase events.** The moment of a touch, whether the ball rolls after it and the moment it comes to rest are taken as seen exactly: no error is drawn on them.
  - **A wall carom, a touch or a new loose ball** (off a glove, or a throw that comes down) ends the track: the ball off the padding or the glove follows no law he can apply from his estimate (where on the wall, the padding, the glove's angle), so his old velocity says nothing about the new one; a bounce on the grass is the same ball going on by a law he knows, from a point he saw. After it he reads the ball by the snapshot read, as before.
  - **A fielder with no track at the first bounce** (his reaction ended after it: an infielder on a hard grounder that bounces at once, an outfielder on a chopper or a ground ball through the infield) reads it by the snapshot read, as before. *Built first, and dropped:* starting a track at his first look then, against a wide prior with no plate cue. On a far ball the persistent error is a fraction of the range, and an estimate started tens of metres short believes that fraction of a short range, so it grew sure of a wrong range (40 m off with σ 1.6 m); and the infielders' fresh tracks of hard grounders, honest but rougher than the snapshot read for their first half-second, raised ground-ball hits from .308 to .334. So the outfielder's depth read of a ball first seen on the ground (TASKS, the depth read after the first bounce; seed 65147 play 10: the right fielder's first read 37 m off) is not covered here.
  - **His read** is flown from the estimate as before: on the first flight with the typical spin, on a hop with the spin his bounce law left it with; rolling, by the world's rolling law from where he believes it is, at the slowing he believes. Every read is on or above the ground. **His doubt** (the arrival margin, the sure call, the set-up) is worked out only for a ball on its first flight, as before: it is the σ of where the ball comes down. A call on a ball still up after a bounce takes his track's doubt of where it next comes down.
  - **Draws.** The looks are the only draws: the same `FielderLook` stream and the play's fixed draws, more of them (sub_index counts on through the play). No new purpose, and every look on a first flight draws as before.

### 5.2 Intercept planning

From its current estimate, each fielder computes candidate intercepts. a chaser reads the ball again after a quarter of the time his own read leaves before it comes down, between 0.1 s and 0.4 s (`REPLAN_FRAC`, `REPLAN_MIN_S`, `REPLAN_S` [E]; 2026-11-15, with the closing read of §5.1: a read that sharpens late is worth having often late), and every 0.1 s within 4 m of the ball (close reads):
- **Fly and line drives:** the earliest point on the predicted descending path, at catchable height (0.3–2.2 m [E]), reachable before the ball. Prefer arriving early and settling; if unreachable, the point that minimizes the remaining gap (dive candidate).
- **Ground balls:** sample the predicted ground path (the agent's own bounce/roll model, with no roughness) every 20 ms; the intercept is the earliest point where the fielder's reach time is ≤ the ball's arrival time minus a set-up margin 0.15 s [E]. A charging preference [E] favors intercepts closer to the infield when the time saved matters for the throw.
- **Reach time** uses the movement model (§5.3) from the current state, not a straight-line distance. *Built 2026-11-12 (`run_gap`, `run_time_to`; revised after the Codex momentum review #3):* in the goal's frame his velocity is u toward it and w across. With no sideways speed: the speed law from u (moving away: a stop at `BRAKE` first). With sideways speed, the turn comes first: w brakes off at `BRAKE` (w/`BRAKE` s, drifting w²/(2·`BRAKE`) m) while his speed toward the goal grows at a_lat, up to top speed; then the speed law over what is left, drift included. The test `reach_estimate_tracks_the_movement_step` checks it against the stepped movement over 85 cases (distance 2–40 m, every mix of toward, away and across): within +0.35/−0.45 s, RMS under 0.15 s (the worst early case, 2 m away moving off at 6 m/s and 4 m/s sideways, went from about −0.35 to −0.42 s when `BRAKE` fell from 6 to 4.75). (The first version, which added the drift but let him accelerate straight at the goal, was up to 1.1 s optimistic.) The 0.15 s set-up margin was tried as a hard requirement and made things worse (it rules out plays he can make); it is not built.
- **Reach time over a short way (spec 2026-11-15, Opus wall-braking review P3-1):** within `STEP_REACH_M` = 5 m of the goal, moving, the estimate is the movement step itself, walls included (`step_toward` run as a late chaser toward the goal, then `wall_rule`, the same as `move_fielders`, tick by tick), until he is within reach, for at most 2.5 s (`STEP_REACH_CAP_S`); the closed form only beyond that. There `wall_delay` adds nothing, since the walls are in the step. `run_time_to` takes the first tick he is within reach, and otherwise scans the first 3 s at 0.05 s for the first crossing before it brackets: the gap is not monotone in time for a man turning round close to his goal. The closed form turns in the goal's frame and the movement step brakes along the velocity, so for a man close to the ball and moving away from it (the overshoot state) it was up to 0.42 s optimistic; the bound in `reach_estimate_tracks_the_movement_step` returns to −0.35 s. *Revised (Codex tracking-read review P2-3):* the first version stepped only until he was heading for the goal, then switched to the closed form. Close to the goal its sideways drift turned him round again (1.33 s against 0.10 s), a reach found while stepping was discarded, the search took a later crossing, and the stepped run went through a wall the movement stops him at (1.39 s against 0.68 s, 1.5 m from the centre-field wall moving at (−4, 4) m/s). `reach_estimate_tracks_the_movement_step_below_two_metres_and_at_a_wall` checks 0.75–1.9 m, every oblique motion up to 8 m/s, in the open and into the wall: within ±0.35 s (worst 0.18 s).
- **Arriving under control (spec 2026-11-15, Opus wall-braking review P2-1):** a chaser with time in hand (the `early` path of §5.3) already brakes onto his spot. He now also keeps a margin for how far his read can still move: his speed toward the spot is held to what stops him `ARRIVE_SIGMAS` [E] = 1 times his doubt short of it, √(2·`BRAKE`·max(0, d − k·σ)), so a read that comes in toward him by that much finds him still able to stop, and one that goes out finds him moving its way, not braking from a sprint the other way.
