As his doubt closes he closes on the spot. The moment he is no longer early (his own plan has him late), the margin goes and he runs, as now.
- **The approach to a ball on the ground (owner's Q32 and Q33; spec 2026-11-20, `work/approach`; measurements and sources in [the approach note](calibration/2026-11-20-approach.md)).** A fielder who takes a ball and throws slows into it when he has time in hand, so that he takes it under control instead of paying the plant (§5.5) for a sprint off the line to his throw; with no time in hand he goes straight at it as before and pays the plant or throws off balance (the hurry rule). Coaching: "Slow down the last two steps before fielding"; "fast, then under control"; the funnel step that ends the approach in fielding position (note §3). It covers every fielder going for a ball he takes on the ground by his own read (a ground ball, a ball that drops in front of him, a ball on a hop, a loose ball, a ball at rest), infielders and outfielders alike (owner, 2026-11-20). While the ball is still in the air since contact there is no approach, whatever his read says of where it comes down: he goes for it as hard as he can (a first build that let him slow for a ball his read had landing in front of him dropped catchable flies and pop-ups: fly-ball hits .102 → .114, pop-up hits .004 → .010 on seed 0). The set-up behind a fly (§5.5) is the catch's own approach.
  - **The throws he expects** (from what everyone sees: the runners and the outs). An outfielder plans for one base (revised 2026-11-20 with rounding, `outfield_round_base`): of the lead runner (the one farthest along, the batter included), the base past the one he is heading for if, by the holder's race rule of §5.6 (Φ((runner − ball)/0.12 s) > 0.3), a throw there from a take at his intercept would give him a play on that runner (a league-typical runner from where he is and how he is moving, `runner_eta`; the ball from the take, his `Transfer` and the throw at his arm, `throw_time`); else the base he is heading for, if that would; else second. The coaching rules: "Ball at you – lead runner. Ball away from you – 2nd base" (note §3). (Until 2026-11-20 he counted second and the base ahead of each runner on base, so with a runner on first only second.) An infielder with fewer than two out plans for the lead force (first with nobody forced, second for the double play, home with the bases loaded); with two out, any force. At a take he counts the throw among these that gets the ball to its base soonest (below): the throw itself is chosen at the take (§5.6), with his plant in it.
  - **The plan** (`plan_approach`, from his own read `belief` and his intercept `t_int`, his own body): from his intercept (the earliest point he reaches, §5.2 above) he looks along his own predicted path every `APPROACH_STEP_S` = 0.04 s for up to `APPROACH_LOOK_S` = 1.5 s [E]. At each point he can reach on his feet before the ball, at time t, the time he has in hand there lets him arrive slower. Reaching a point d away at speed v (his top speed, or what he builds up over d from his motion), with s seconds in hand (how far past the point he would be by the ball's time, over v; within `STEP_REACH_M` of it, where that shortfall is stepped and stops at his reach, the ball's time less his reach time), he can brake to v_c = max(0, v − √(2·`BRAKE`·v·s)): a braked arrival costs (v − v_c)²/(2·`BRAKE`·v) s over running through (the stop's time at `BRAKE` less the distance it covers at v, §5.3's wall delay with the wall at the point). His arrival velocity running through is his motion now turned toward the point by the speed law over the time to it (v_now·e^(−T/τ) + v_max·dir·(1 − e^(−T/τ)), at most his top speed), so a man close to the ball and moving across it is known to arrive still moving across; braking scales it to v_c. The ball's time at a base from a take there is t plus the plant he would owe arriving at v_c (§5.5, the across term and the toss rule included) plus the throw's length at his arm; he takes the point where that is earliest over his expected throws, by more than `APPROACH_GAIN_S` = 0.01 s [E] over his intercept at full speed; at his intercept itself he goes straight at it as before. *Revised 2026-11-20 (Opus approach review P2-3; approach note §7):* (1) v_c is no lower than he can brake to from his speed toward the point now over the way left (`brake_floor`: √(v_toward² − 2·`BRAKE`·(d − 0.7·`REACH`))); braked less than he wanted, he is there early by the rest of his time in hand, and a take is planned only if moving on at v_c he is still within his planning reach of the point when the ball gets there (`braked_take`; otherwise he would be past it). (2) On a ball coming at him (at his intercept it moves toward where he is) he takes no point the ball reaches after passing him (at that point it moves away from him, `ball_passed_him`): a real fielder does not back off a ball he can take in front of him for a better throw (a pitcher who backed off a comebacker to take it deeper let it by him, seed 1048 play 193). (3) The movement brakes him to v_c at his planning reach of the point (§5.3), as the plan times it. Checked against the movement step (`the_plan_times_a_braked_take_as_the_movement_runs_it`: from a run at 0–8 m/s, a take point 4–22 m away straight on or 45° off his run, 0.1–0.8 s in hand, 93 planned cases): he is there −0.65 to +0.08 s from the ball's time, within 0.71 m of the take point when the ball gets there, and at most 0.20 m/s faster than planned. Before (1) and (3), slowed nearly to a stop he stood at his full reach, a metre off the ball's path, and on a short run from a stand he came 0.1–0.3 s after the plan had him. *A margin, tried and not kept:* `ROUND_MARGIN_S` times the share of the ball's motion across his way, kept in hand on every later take as the angle from contact kept it (the review's suggestion). It cut the balls an infielder gives up to the approach further (in 600 games, against the same build with the approach off: 28 lost, 41 gained; without it 64 lost, 75 gained; on `af5825d` 99 and 77), but it took most of the braking away on balls across his way, for nothing the games show: ground-ball hits about level (.320 against .318, seeds 0–299 and 1000–1299 pooled), triples 0.92 against 0.85 a team-game and outfield pickup-to-release 1.15 against 1.12 s (seed 0), and a frozen double play (`seed_13_play_140_…`: the first baseman, no longer braked, took the chopper at 5.6 m/s across the line home and hurried the throw) failed. Without it the excess of balls lost over balls gained is gone, which was the fault. A deeper take costs him the longer throw, so he does not wait on a ball rolling away for nothing. A ball that will be at rest before he gets there has no deadline: his time in hand grows with the time he takes, and he brakes onto it. The intercept and his call stay those of his read: the approach changes where he runs and how fast he arrives, not whether the ball is his (teammates' decisions and calls are unchanged).
  - **The movement** (§5.3): he runs at the take point, timed against the ball there (his `early` test uses its time), and his speed toward it is held to √(v_c² + 2·`BRAKE`·max(0, d − 0.7·`REACH`)), so he is down to v_c within the reach he plans with (revised 2026-11-20, §5.3), even a tick late. (A test switch turns the approach off for old-versus-new comparisons: `a_shortstop_with_time_in_hand_slows_into_a_ground_ball`.) A ball that comes within his reach before he gets there is taken there, as always. He re-plans at every read.
  - **Rounding the ball (owner's Q33, 2026-11-19: the approach is "slowing into and rounding the ball"; built 2026-11-20, `work/approach`; measured in [the approach note §6](calibration/2026-11-20-approach.md#6-rounding-the-ball-q33)).** With time in hand he may instead come at the take point from behind it, away from the base, and through it toward the base, so that he takes the ball moving toward his throw: no plant, and on a set throw the crow hop's gain if he comes through faster than `HOP_MPS` (§5.5). Coaching: "Round it, square it"; "Running straight at the ball: ... off-balance throws. Fix: round it, then come through the ball"; "just a couple steps so I can get a little bit around the baseball" (note §3).
    - *The base he rounds it for:* an outfielder's one base (above); an infielder's nearest expected throw. A toss (a target within `TOSS_MAX_M`, §5.5) is not rounded: only motion away from it counts.
    - *The ways through it:* with u the unit vector from the take point p toward the base, he may come through p along u, or along u turned toward his straight run by `ROUND_SLANT_DEG` = 20°, 40° or 60° [E]. Motion off the line up to the plant's free speed (`PLANT_FREE_MPS`, 3.2 m/s, §5.5) costs nothing, so part of the turn often does the work of all of it at a fraction of the cost (a full right-angle turn costs about 1 s). On a way a at angle ψ from u he comes through no faster than `PLANT_FREE_MPS`/sin ψ, so that he owes no plant.
    - *The plan:* each way a comes through w = p − `ROUND_M`·a (`ROUND_M` = 3 m [E, unsourced: the coaching's "couple of steps"]). The turn at w is §5.3's turn law for a heading change: turning through θ (the angle between his run to w and a) over the chord `ROUND_M` to p takes a curve of radius `ROUND_M`/(2 sin(θ/2)), which his lateral acceleration allows up to v_t = √(a_lat·`ROUND_M`/(2 sin(θ/2))) (`turn_speed`; 3.9 m/s for a right-angle turn at a_lat = 7 m/s²). With v_r the least of his arrival speed v running straight in, v_t and the way's free speed, it costs against running straight in (`round_cost`): the extra way, |w − pos| + `ROUND_M` − |p − pos|, at v; braking from v to v_r at `BRAKE`, (v − v_r)²/(2·`BRAKE`·v); and the last `ROUND_M` at v_r, `ROUND_M`·(1/v_r − 1/v). He can take that way when his time in hand at p covers the cost and a margin, `ROUND_MARGIN_S` = 0.3 s [E, unsourced: a ground ball's read error and the plan's error against the movement], w is inside the field (no rounding at the wall), he has room to brake from his speed now to the turn's speed before it (by the turn's radius v_t²/a_lat from w), and his run out to w passes p no nearer than `ROUND_CLEAR_M` = 1.5 m [E: his reach and half a step]. (The first build had no such rule: a man coming at the ball from the base's side ran past the take point toward a point behind it, and the ball met him going away; 37 of the 40 rounded takes in 60 games.) Taking it so, he arrives along a at v_r (or his pace, the way over the time he has, if lower), and his ready time at the base is the ball's time at p, the plant he owes arriving so, and the throw at his arm. (Not the crow hop's gain: counted in the plan, it bought outfield pickups with nobody on 0.16 s later for a gain most of those throws, made with no play at less than full effort, do not get; triples rose 0.82 → 0.89 [0.81 → 0.88] a team-game.) He takes the straight take with the earliest ready time, as above, unless a rounded take beats it by `ROUND_GAIN_S` = 0.15 s [E: the bound on the plan's error against the movement in the test below; a dead ball in the corner rounded for a planned 0.01 s came 0.05 s late, and the batter made third]. Once rounding, he keeps rounding unless running straight is better by more than `APPROACH_GAIN_S` (it does not flip at every read), and once coming through (below) the way he is on costs nothing more. A straight take braked under the free speed has the same ready time, so a rounded one wins only where braking alone cannot bring his motion off the line under the free speed in the time he has and a turn, or part of one, can; on the throw itself he then has the crow hop's gain as well (§5.5). The test `the_plan_costs_a_rounded_take_as_the_movement_runs_it` checks the cost against the movement step over 82 cases (a run of 0–8 m/s toward a take point 6–20 m away, the base 20–120° off his line): the stepped run comes through within his reach −0.06 to +0.11 s from the plan's time, heading within 24° of the base.
    - *A ball all but still* (rolling slower than `ROUND_MIN_BALL_MPS` = 3 m/s at p by his read [E]) needs no margin and has no time to be taken by: he is not paced to it but goes round it as fast as the turn allows and picks it up coming through. (Paced to a stopped ball, he waited behind it, each re-read putting the take a little later, while a teammate came in and the runner took third.)
    - *The movement:* going out he runs at w, his speed held to √(v_t² + 2·`BRAKE`·max(0, |w − pos| − r_t)) so that he is down to the turn's speed v_t (at most his planned arrival speed) by its radius r_t = v_t²/a_lat (at least `ROUND_NEAR_M` = 0.5 m [E]), and his pace leaves the last `ROUND_M` its time at that speed. Within r_t of w he is coming through (kept while he re-plans the same way, `ROUND_SAME_COS`): he follows the line from w through p along his way, steering at the point on it his turning radius at his speed (at least 0.5 m) ahead of where he is, at no more than his planned arrival speed nor than lets him finish the turn onto the way by the take point (§5.3's turn law over the way left: a re-read can move the take point). Both ways he is timed against the ball at p as coming through a set-up behind a fly (§5.5): his pace is the way left over the time left until `ROUND_MARGIN_S` before the ball is there by his read (inside that, until the ball itself), at most his top speed, recomputed every tick. (Paced to meet the ball exactly, as first built, he was a step late as often as not, and a ball that goes on past the take point went by him: 23 of the 26 first balls in play that rounding changed in 100 games were outs made hits.) A ball that comes within his reach on the way is taken there, as always.
    - *What it does (measured, approach note §6):* at the take, rounded takes did what rounding is for (infield plant 0.048 → 0.005 s, release 0.87 → 0.63 s). In games every version that rounded at all lost more balls than it saved (paced to meet the ball, he was a step late and the ball went past him). With the safeguards above it is rare (none in 600 games at `5c83ab4`, 5 at `af5825d`; approach note §6–§7): the sim's fielders reach a ball on the ground with 0.1–0.2 s in hand, and a rounding costs 0.3–1 s.
    - *What the others know:* rounding is his own plan from his own read; teammates and runners read the take as it happens (his motion then, §5.5's plant from it), as for the rest of the approach. A test switch turns rounding off (`NO_ROUND`).
  - **The angle from contact: built and removed (2026-11-20; Opus approach review P2-1; approach note §6–§7).** An outfielder whose read had the ball landing in front of him, out of his reach in the air, chose from contact a line through the take point toward his base (the Coordinator's ruling: part of Q33). Weighed in the air against going straight at full speed, it was chosen in about 2,700 outfield plays in 300 games and given up before the take in all but 1 in 600 games, because once the ball was down the braked straight take matched it. Weighed instead against the braked straight take (with the margin below, the braking only once the ball is down) it was still chosen about 3,300 times in 300 games, his way at the take in 2–4, and first to third did not move (36.0% [37.1%] against 36.6% [36.7%]). It is taken out (its code, parameters and tests); rounding on the ground stays. What a real outfielder's angle buys, the plan here either leaves out (the crow hop's gain coming through toward the base, tried and left out on 2026-11-20) or the sim's fielders do not have (time in hand on a quick single).
  - **The cases**, by this rule: a routine grounder at an infielder (his charge at full speed is mostly across the line to first, so he slows into a later hop and takes it under control); the slow roller charged (no later point is soon enough, so he takes it on the run and throws off balance under the hurry rule: an arm-only throw on the run, without the crow hop's gain, §5.5); the backhand in the hole (the ball goes away from him across the line, so there is rarely a later point; he takes it on the run and plants or hurries; Báez's 0.60 s backhand at 81 mph in 2019, an elite exchange, is about what the hurry rule gives a grade-70 `Transfer` moving away at 4–5 m/s, note §3); the ball in the gap run down going away (later points come as the ball slows; he slows into it and takes it moving away slower, and the stop that is left he pays under the plant, the crow hop or the spin off the deceleration being inside it).
- **An on-time caller is not displaced by a later teammate (seed 1000003096 play 88):** traced on `work/wall-braking` (where the review saw it; on the integration the play is another ball). It is not the call rule. The first baseman calls it sure at 0.66 s. His own snapshot read lowers him to unsure at 2.42 s and to "no catch in the air" at 3.44 s: the mid-flight drift the tracking read replaces. Only then, at 3.74 s, does the general rule leave it to the right fielder, who is ahead in the order everyone can work out. The call rule is unchanged.

### 5.3 Movement

A kinematic point with heading:
- Speed along the path follows `v(t) = v_max · (1 − e^(−t/τ_a))` from rest, with v_max from `Speed` and τ_a from `Acceleration`. Check: v_max = 8.23 m/s, τ_a = 1.0 s gives 27.3 m in 4.3 s, a typical home-to-first time once the swing follow-through is added [D].
- Turning is limited by lateral acceleration `a_lat` from `Agility`; sharp direction changes force deceleration.
- Steering: pursuit toward the current intercept, re-planned at 30 Hz. **First steps follow the first estimate**, so an early depth error produces a visible wrong first step (a `FirstStep` explanation fact).
- *Built 2026-11-12 (`fielder_step`):* velocity is a vector. Speeding up along his direction of motion follows the speed law above; slowing takes at most 4.75 m/s² (`BRAKE`, SOURCED: the mean of measured maximal decelerations of team-sport athletes, 4.2–5.0 m/s², Harper, Cohen, Carling & Kiely 2020, *Sports* 8(6), 76; docs/calibration/2026-11-15-wall-approach-speed.md; it was 6 [E] until 2026-11-15) and turning at most `a_lat` (`Agility`, 7 m/s² at grade 50), each up to the change wanted. So a reversal is a full stop (from 7 m/s: 1.47 s and 5.2 m at 4.75 m/s²), and a sharp turn at speed a curve. Until then fielders turned and reversed instantly at full speed. A non-chaser, or a chaser early for the ball, wants the speed that stops him on his spot at `BRAKE`; a late chaser runs through. *Revised 2026-11-21 (`work/dp-cover`, `cover_step`):* a man covering a base with a force pending there (a runner forced to it who has not reached it) is timed against the throw as a chaser is against the ball: he brakes onto the bag only when he is early for it, his time to the bag (as the chaser's: at his speed toward it, or from rest by the speed law) leaving `EARLY_MARGIN_S` = 0.1 s [E] before the throw gets there by his belief (§5.5, the covering man); otherwise he comes at it to cross it at `COVER_CROSS_MPS` = 4 m/s [E, **unsourced**: about a jog], his speed toward it held to √(`COVER_CROSS_MPS`² + 2·`BRAKE`·d), the form of the wall's cap. Within the touch radius (0.6 m), or once past the bag, he brakes onto it. A cover with no force there, or a ball still loose, keeps the stop. (Until 2026-11-21 every covering man braked to a stop on his bag, 0.4–0.5 s slower over the last 5 m than running through; in the owner's saved play 168 of seed 51001 the covering shortstop took 3.0 s to the bag, the feed waited for him and the pivot had no chance at first. A real middle infielder on a double-play ball takes the bag moving, timing his arrival to the feed. [Saved-plays note §1, Q-A option (b)](calibration/2026-11-20-saved-plays-51001.md#1-plays-66-and-168-no-double-play-attempt).) Anyone slows into a turn he cannot make at speed: a spot d away at θ off his heading needs a curve of radius d/(2 sin θ), so his wanted speed is at most √(a_lat·d/(2 sin θ)) (a spot behind him: θ = 90°). Without this a late chaser who narrowly missed his spot looped round it. On an approach to a ball on the ground (§5.2) his speed toward the take point is also held to √(v_c² + 2·`BRAKE`·max(0, d − 0.7·`REACH`)), so that he is at his planned speed v_c when he is within the reach he plans with (0.7·`REACH`, as the plan times the take; revised 2026-11-20: braked to it at his full reach, a man slowed nearly to a stop stood a metre off the ball's path, and on a short run he came there 0.1–0.3 s after the plan had him); rounding it (§5.2) he runs at a point behind the take point, braking to the speed his turn there allows, then along the line through the take point toward the base, at the pace that brings him there `ROUND_MARGIN_S` before the ball. Coming through a set-up behind a fly (§5.5) his top speed is held at the speed with which the movement step (the speed law up to it, braking at `BRAKE` down to it) brings him to the catch point as the ball comes down by his read (`paced_speed`, at most his top speed, recomputed every tick), so he speeds up if he is behind and never passes the point before the ball. After catching a batted ball, and for a relay man catching a throw, the target becomes where braking stops him, v²/(2·`BRAKE`) ahead, and he brakes onto it (he plants; he does not run back to the planned spot). He is on his spot only once within 5 cm and below 0.5 m/s (`SETTLE_V`); faster, he carries on through it (Codex momentum review #1: arriving used to stop him dead).
- Dive: if the gap at the predicted catch time is between arm reach and arm reach + 1.5 m [E], the fielder may dive; a dive adds reach, inflates glove σ (§5.4), and costs 1.5 s [E] to get up before a throw.
  *Built 2026-11-13 (`try_captures`, `fielder_step`):* a dive carries the body: over 0.3 s (`DIVE_LUNGE_S` [E]) he travels toward the ball until his glove is at it (arm's reach short of the ball, at most the 1.5 m of dive extension), then lies there, still, until he is up. The lunge never crosses the wall: the body stops 0.3 m short of it (`BODY_R` [E]), which matters where the fair and foul fences meet at an inward corner. He throws from where he landed. A ball he holds while down is by the turf (0.3 m), not at chest height. The get-up runs from the dive. Until he is up the fielder cannot dive again, but he can still grab a ball that comes within arm's reach at or below dive height (1.2 m). A ball rimmed off his glove that stays close is the usual case (§5.4: bobbles that stay close can be re-fielded). Whichever attempt secures the ball, he can throw only once he is up: at the end of the get-up plus his transfer (for a thrown ball he takes, the receiver's shorter transfer, also from when he is up). After a rimmed ball he may try again 0.5 s later; after a clean miss, not until he is up. Before this, a fielder who rimmed a dive could dive again from the ground half a second later, and that catch started a second full get-up. On seed 7475 play 25, the CF held a single 2.4 s while the runner went from first to third.

  *When he dives, rebuilt 2026-11-13 (owner backlog A5; docs/calibration/2026-11-13-dive-rate.md):* fielders had dived 22 times a game against 2 to 3 in real games. Past his reach (1.0 m) a chasing fielder now has two ways to a batted or loose ball:
  - **A stretch on his feet**, up to `STRETCH` 0.4 m [E] further, at any catchable height. He stays up, and it costs him `STRETCH_COST_S` 0.3 s [E] before he can throw (he is off balance, §5.5).
  - **A dive**, up to 1.5 m further, for a ball at or below 1.2 m. The body takes 0.3 s to lay out, so he has only the extension that the time since he knew he must dive allows: every 0.1 s, with the ball within 15 m, he asks whether going for it gains him anything (`Sim::dive_gains`), and the answer has to have held without a break. The pitcher, finishing his delivery, cannot lay out until `PITCHER_RECOVER_S` 0.7 s [E] after contact.
  - **Whether it gains him anything** is his own judgment, from his own read and his own running. A ball still in the air: only a catch is an out, so it does unless he can catch it on his feet before it comes down. A ball on the ground: for a stretch, only if staying on his feet would get him the ball later than its 0.3 s cost; for a dive, only if the dive is expected to get him the ball sooner than staying on his feet (`Sim::dive_pays`, below). So nobody dives at a roller he is about to pick up.
    - *What a dive at a ball on the ground costs him (revised 2026-11-23, [of-ground-dive review](reviews/2026-11-23-rev-of-dive-of-ground-dive-review.md) P2-1; [note](calibration/2026-11-23-of-ground-dive.md) §7).* A dive does not always end with the ball in his glove. The dive he weighs is the one he is about to make (revised 2026-11-24, [second review](reviews/2026-11-24-rev-of-dive-2-of-ground-dive-review.md) P2-2, P3-5): the earlier of his plan's first dive point (at the plan's dive reach, `REACH` + 0.6·`DIVE`) and the ball's first pass by his read within his dive reach (at its closest, where the capture lays out), which is `REACH` plus the extension his lunge time allows by then (as the capture counts it, from when he first knew he must dive), at or below `DIVE_MAX_Z`, not past a wall, while the ball is faster than `LUNGE_MIN_MPS` 3 m/s (the capture's own floor for a lunge), at that distance. Both are measured from where his own movement will have him then, and his landing is kept off the wall as the capture's lunge is. (Before, the plan's first dive point alone was weighed; as the ball came level with him the imminent pass dropped out of the plan's scan, the point jumped to a dive seconds later, and the must-dive hold broke as the ball passed: a pitcher stopped laying out at a chopper up the middle.) By his own read of that point (where the ball will be and how fast) and his own hands (the §5.4 σ there), he weighs three outcomes. **A catch** (the glove within `r_pocket`): he has the ball when he is up, `DIVE_GETUP_S` after the dive. **A knock-down** (within `r_rim`): he is up after the same get-up, then runs from where he lies to the ball, which left his glove along its path at 0.3 of its speed (the deterministic part of the §5.4 deflection; the push's direction is his own glove error, which he cannot know, so he expects none) and rolls on by his own flight and roll model. **A miss**: he is up after the get-up, then chases the ball, which goes on as his read has it. The expected time to have the ball is these times weighted by the three chances; he dives only when it is earlier than his standing take (`intercept_scan`). Any of these takes, standing or after the get-up, that does not reach the ball before it comes to rest by his read ends at its resting point, reached at his top speed (before 2026-11-24 a read with no standing take before the ball came to rest sent him in at once; in the paired probe most of the remaining dives at balls he would have had on his feet within 2.7 s were of that kind). If the ball never passes within his dive reach by his read, there is no dive to price. With a standing take he stays on his feet; with none before the ball comes to rest, the resting-point take stands for it, and he may go (if the ball then comes within reach after all) only if that take is more than a catch's get-up away (revised 2026-11-24, second review P3-1: before, with no standing take he was let go at once, and laid out at a ball rolling at 3–4 m/s that he would have had at rest a moment later). No new parameter: `DIVE_GETUP_S`, the §5.4 radii, the deflection law and his own running. This applies to every fielder's dive at a ball on the ground. A ball still in the air keeps the rule above whenever his read has it pass within the capture's envelope (`REACH` + `DIVE`, at or below `DIVE_MAX_Z`, from where his movement will have him) before its first bounce, off the ground or off a wall (a ball that has touched a wall is no catch for an out). Only a dive his read can make nowhere but after the bounce (a short hop in front of him) is weighed as a dive at a ball on the ground (revised 2026-11-24, second review P2-1: the first version asked whether the plan's dive point came after the bounce; the plan plans dives only to 1.9 m, so a pop-up or liner passing 1.9–2.5 m away read as a short hop, the split flickered between reads, the must-dive hold broke, and catchable pop-ups and liners dropped). Before this, a dive on the ground was charged the get-up alone (the cost of a catch). With the rim gate (next item) outfielders then laid out at balls they would have had on their feet within about 2.7 s, where a knock-down gains nothing and a miss costs extra bases (review: 4 better and 8 worse of 35 such knock-downs, 7 worse of 12 misses, in 600 games).
  - **An outfielder has nobody behind him.** He knows his own hands: he dives only when he gives himself at least `OF_DIVE_MIN_P` 0.5 [E] of the ball (the §5.4 σ, with the ball's speed and distance as his own read has them); otherwise he plays it on his feet. He decides once, when a dive first offers; having decided to go, he goes. Infielders always go. The rule does not look at the inning, the score, or who is backing him up.
    - *What "the ball" is depends on where it is (revised 2026-11-23, owner's saved play 71 of seed 51001; [note](calibration/2026-11-23-of-ground-dive.md)).* **A ball still in the air:** only a clean catch counts, so he weighs the chance that the glove is within the pocket (`r_pocket`, §5.4); a liner off his glove in the air is still a hit. **A ball on the ground** (it has come down, the fact `dive_gains` also uses): a clean pick and a knock-down both stop it, so he weighs the chance that the glove is within the rim (`r_rim`, §5.4: a catch or a deflection, which leaves the glove at about a quarter of the ball's speed (measured on outfield ground-ball rims: a median 3.8 m/s out of 15.9) and stays near him, where he can play it once up). A miss is not free: he is down for the get-up while the ball goes on. That cost, and the knock-down's, are weighed against his standing take in the previous item. No new parameter: the same `OF_DIVE_MIN_P` and the §5.4 radii. With average hands (σ_hands 0.018 m) the rim chance is at least 0.74 anywhere in the dive envelope, so an average outfielder lays out for a ground ball he can reach in the lunge, as real outfielders do to keep a ball in the gap from reaching the wall; only poor hands (σ_hands above about 0.025–0.027 m at full stretch, the σ multiplier there being 3.8 for a slow ball to 4.05 at 40 m/s) keep him up. Before this he weighed the pocket alone for a ball on the ground too: in play 71 the right fielder, with the dive earned 0.3 s ahead and the ball passing 2.11 m from him at 16 m/s, gave himself 0.37 for a clean catch (0.84 for a glove on it), stayed on his feet, and the ball rolled to the wall for a triple.
  - Either way the glove meets the ball where it passes closest, as for a standing catch.

- *Walls (added 2026-11-14, Gemini play-area review P2-3; physics-spec §9.5; reworked 2026-11-15, Q5 and the collision review's P2-2):* fielders see the walls.
  - **One limit over the wall, for plans and every capture** (`OVER_WALL_REACH_M` = `REACH` − `BODY_R` = 0.7 m): his body stops `BODY_R` 0.3 m inside the wall and his glove reaches `REACH` 1.0 m from the body's centre, so the glove gets at most 0.7 m past the wall's plane. A fielder leaning over a rail reaches about an arm's length beyond it; he cannot stretch his body through it. A point of his own predicted path farther past a wall than that is no intercept, and the runners' estimate of when a fielder gets the ball skips such points too. Capture uses the same number for a catch on his feet, a stretch (`STRETCH` 0.4 m) and a dive (`DIVE` 1.5 m): before 2026-11-15 capture allowed the stretch's 1.4 m and the dive's 2.5 m from the body past the wall while the plans assumed 0.7 m, and a foul fly was caught 0.93 m beyond a wall (collision review P2-2). Test: `nobody_catches_a_ball_farther_past_a_wall_than_his_arm_reaches_over_it` (a ball 0.68 m past the plane is caught, 0.72 m is not, standing and stretching; built from a constructed state, not a seeded game).
  - **Speed at the wall.** Moving, his speed toward the nearest wall is at most `sqrt(v_c² + 2·BRAKE·distance)` (`wall_speed_cap`; distance is his body's clearance), where `BRAKE` = 4.75 m/s² (above) and the wall-contact speed `v_c` = `WALL_CONTACT_SPEED_MPS` = **2.0 m/s [E, unsourced]**. No public measurement of an outfielder's speed at wall contact exists (docs/calibration/2026-11-15-wall-approach-speed.md); the owner chose "about 2 m/s" (Q5, 2026-11-15): a fielder who wants a ball at the wall arrives at a jog, not at rest, and the wall (which stops him at 0.3 m) takes the rest. Before this the cap was `sqrt(2·BRAKE·distance)` at 6 m/s², arrival near 0. A fielder no longer runs into the stands wall at 7 m/s after a pop 12 m into the seats (tested: his speed over the last 0.02 m is at most `sqrt(2² + 2·4.75·0.02)` = 2.05 m/s plus one sample's braking; and one running flat out at the center-field wall arrives at about 2 m/s); outfielders chasing to the fence slow for it in the same way. The plan knows it (`wall_delay`): against running on at speed v with clearance c at the stand point it costs `(v − sqrt(v_c² + 2·BRAKE·c))/BRAKE − ((v² − v_c²)/(2·BRAKE) − c)/v` seconds (0.38 s at c = 0.3 m and v = 8 m/s, 0.47 s at the wall; it was 0.39 s and 0.67 s at 6 m/s² and v_c = 0).
  - **Planned, not built (owner, Q5 c, 2026-11-15): judgment of the wall as a per-fielder trait.** A fielder judges the wall from his own imperfect sense of where he is, so a careless one sometimes arrives hard and a cautious one gives up early. A *wall awareness* (or fearlessness) rating will scale the two per-fielder values this section fixes league-wide: `v_c` (the speed he accepts at contact) and `BRAKE` (the deceleration he brings to it, a rating's share of the sourced 4.2–5.0 m/s² range), and perturb the distance he believes he has to the wall; the wall cap and `wall_delay` will then take them from the fielder, not from the constants.

### 5.4 Capture

The glove moves toward the predicted ball position at capture time, limited by a hand speed of 6 m/s [E] within the reach envelope (arm length 0.42·H [E] plus lean and dive extension).

At the capture instant, the glove pocket center has a Gaussian error in the plane perpendicular to the ball's velocity (purpose `GlovePlacement`):

```
σ_g = σ_hands · (1 + k_v·v_ball/40 + k_late·lateness + k_reach·reach_frac + k_hop·short_hop + k_dive·diving)
      · F_composure · K_hands
```

| Factor | Meaning | Default [E] |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| `v_ball/40` | ball speed in m/s, normalized to 40 | k_v = 0.5 |
| `lateness` | how late the final estimate settled relative to capture, s, normalized to 0.3 s | k_late = 0.8 |
| `reach_frac` | fraction of maximum reach used | k_reach = 0.6 |
| `short_hop` | 1 if the ball bounced within 1 m before capture | k_hop = 1.0 |
| `diving` | 1 if diving | k_dive = 1.0 |
| extension (built 2026-11-13) | off his feet or stretching: how far past reach the glove is, as a fraction of the 1.5 m dive | `K_EXTEND` = 1.5 [E], added with k_dive; a stretch counts as `diving` = 1 |

**Outcome (pure geometry):** with `d` the distance from ball center to pocket center in that plane:
- `d ≤ r_pocket` (0.06 m [E]): **catch**.
- `r_pocket < d ≤ r_rim` (0.12 m [E]): **deflection**, solved by the physics collision model with glove e_n ≈ 0.15 (physics spec §9.4). Bobbles that stay close can be re-fielded.
- `d > r_rim`: **miss**. The ball continues and may hit the body capsules (a block).

**Sanity check [D]:** for a 2-D Gaussian, P(d > r) = exp(−r²/2σ²). A routine chance with σ_g = 1.8 cm gives P(miss pocket) = exp(−5.6) ≈ 0.4%, and some of those deflections are recovered. That fits PLAN §8.7's "routine plays well above 98%" with room for harder chances to fail far more often.

### 5.5 Transfer and throw

**The throwing hand (built 2026-11-13, owner backlog A1; `throw_release_point`).** A fielder throws with his throwing hand (`FielderAttrs.throws`, from the roster, data-formats §2.4): the ball leaves at release height `THROW_ARM_OFFSET_M` = 0.35 m [E] to his arm side of his body's centre, with him facing his target (a right-hander's hand is to the right of the line to it). His aim is solved from that point. The glove hand sets how far he must turn to throw (below). *Not built:* what the glove hand does to fielding. The glove's reach is still a circle around his body, the same to either side, so a backhand play is no harder to make than a forehand one (it belongs with fielding errors, backlog A17).

- **Set-up time** (built 2026-11-13, backlog A3; `bb_agents::throwing`). A fielder is *set* for a throw (balanced, his stride behind it) a time `set = Transfer + turn + footwork` after he has the ball and is on his feet:
  - `Transfer` is his catch-to-release for a throw straight ahead (§10; a fielder taking a throw, already facing his next target, needs 0.7 of it and no turn).
  - **The turn (the glove hand).** At release a thrower is side-on, glove shoulder to the target. Taking the ball he is square to the way he faces, so a target straight ahead needs a quarter turn (the normal case, inside `Transfer`), one off his glove shoulder none, one off his throwing shoulder a half turn: `turn = TURN_S · (φ − 90°)/90°`, φ the angle from his glove-side direction to the target, `TURN_S` = 0.12 s [E]. He faces the way he is running (above 3 m/s [E]), else the ball coming to him (if it comes faster than the same 3 m/s along the ground), else the plate. So a right-handed first baseman fielding a grounder needs up to 0.24 s longer to throw to second than a left-handed one, and a shortstop's throw to first, off his glove shoulder, is the quick one.
  - **Footwork (the plant):** his motion off the line to the target (away from it and across it, Q32; restored 2026-11-20 with the approach of §5.2, below) he must stop before a set throw; +0.3 s [E] when he took it on the ground from a dive (on top of the get-up time; it replaces the plant, since he gets up from a standstill). The rule is below, "The catch sets up the throw: the rules"; it replaced a flat +0.2 s [E] for a fielder moving away from the target faster than 2 m/s [E] (2026-11-19). A fielder who owes footwork is *off balance* until he is set.
- **Hurrying.** He can let go before he is set, as early as `RUSH_MIN_FRAC` = 0.6 [E] of `Transfer + turn` (no step, arm only). `hurry` runs linearly from 1 at that earliest release to 0 at `set`; `off_balance` = 1 if he owed footwork. A hurried throw is slower and wilder:
  - speed = `ArmStrength` · (1 − hurry · (0.10 + 0.08·off_balance)) [E] (*added 2026-11-13*: the spec had given a rushed throw no speed loss; a throw without the stride or the legs under it has less on it);
  - direction σ = `ArmAccuracy` · (1 + hurry · (k_rush + k_off·off_balance)), k_rush = k_off = 1.0 [E].
  - Worked example: an infielder (0.65 s) moving away is set at 0.85 s and can let go at 0.39 s; at 0.39 s the throw has 82% of his arm and three times his direction σ, at 0.62 s 91% and twice.
- **The catch sets up the throw (owner, Q32, 2026-11-19: decided; the rules below).** The owner's model of a throw is in [docs/baseball-throw.md](baseball-throw.md): a throw is energy carried from the ground up (back-leg drive, the lead-leg block turning forward momentum into rotation, hip-shoulder separation), so what the fielder's body is doing when he takes the ball decides how hard and how straight he can throw. This replaces the flat footwork term above:
  - **Momentum against the target costs time or quality.** A fielder who takes the ball moving away from his target, or across it, must either take the time to stop under his braking limit (`BRAKE`, §5.3), turn and plant his back foot before a set throw, or throw before he is set: off balance, slower and wilder (the hurry rule above). The cost grows with his speed against the target, not the flat 0.2 s over 2 m/s. Lateral drift leaks energy too: the trunk fights the sideways momentum to square up.
  - **Momentum toward the target helps.** A fielder coming forward through the catch (a crow hop or shuffle) carries his momentum into the lead-leg block. Statcast's `ArmStrength` (a player's hardest throws) is taken to be this throw; a flat-footed or drifting catch gives less. The size of the gain or loss needs a source [E].
  - **Good fielders set up behind the ball.** An outfielder with time to spare before a catch that will lead to a throw settles a few steps behind the catch point and comes through the ball toward the target, so he takes it moving forward. Whether a fielder does it, and how well, will become part of his ratings.
  - **Accuracy** comes from a stable head and a firm front side; drift off the line (moving across, off balance) widens the direction error.
  - **The cutoff.** On singles and sacrifice flies with a play at the plate, the outfielder throws *through* the cutoff man: low, at his head or chest, so the ball carries home on the fly or one hop. The catcher reads the runner and calls it: silence and the cutoff man lets it go; "Cut!" when the runner will score easily or the throw is off line, and the cutoff man takes it and throws to second or third to hold a trailing runner, not home. On deep balls (about 280 ft or more) the outfielder throws to a relay man. A high throw over the cutoff man's head is a mistake: it takes away the cut.
  - Before building: measure how fast and in which direction outfielders are moving at the catches that lead to a throw (the [throw-race note](calibration/2026-11-19-throw-race.md) traced a race 0.3–0.5 s quicker for the ball than MLB's tag-up outcomes imply on 250–300 ft flies), and source the crow-hop gain.
- **The catch sets up the throw: the rules (built 2026-11-19, `work/catch-throw`; measurements and sources in [the catch-throw note](calibration/2026-11-19-catch-throw.md)).** They make the decided design above exact. Nothing here draws a random number: no new `Purpose` or sub-index.
  - **His approach.** At the catch (on his feet with the ball), with ground velocity v and u the horizontal unit vector to the target of his throw's first leg (the base, or the relay man): toward a = v·u; across c = |v × u|; off the line v_off = |(min(a, 0), c)| (`throwing::off_line_mps`). Moving away or across counts; moving toward counts nothing.
  - **The plant (replaces the flat footwork):** footwork = max(0, v_off / `BRAKE` − `PLANT_OVERLAP_S`) (2026-11-19 to 2026-11-20, under Q33, v_away = max(0, −a) in place of v_off). Two throws count only the motion away (`throwing::exchange_footwork_s`, Q33's rule): **a toss** (a target within `TOSS_MAX_M` = 15 m [E, unsourced; coaching has feeds near the bag flipped or tossed underhand on the run]: thrown with the arm alone, it needs no planted stride), and **the double-play pivot's and the relay man's throws on**, whose sourced times (`PIVOT_S_50`, `RELAY_S_50`) are measured whole exchanges, the pivot man's footwork across the bag and the relay man's turn included. Built with the across term first, these took the 2B's feed to second and the shortstop's pivot 0.10–0.25 s slower than main and broke a frozen double play (`throw_choices.rs`, seed 3 play 140). `BRAKE` = 4.75 m/s² (§5.3, sourced; `throwing::BRAKE_MPS2`, which the movement law now uses). `PLANT_OVERLAP_S` = 3.2 / 4.75 = 0.67 s [E, unsourced]: the part of the stop that fits inside the glove-to-hand of his transfer (about an infielder's whole `Transfer`, most of an outfielder's), so a man moving off the line under 3.2 m/s (`PLANT_FREE_MPS`) owes nothing. Moving away or across at 5 m/s he owes 0.38 s (the old flat rule 0.2 s); at 7 m/s, 0.80 s (a full stop is 1.47 s, §5.3). **`PLANT_OVERLAP_S` is the plant's calibration knob** (Opus catch-throw review P3-4): it sets every plant, and the only empirical anchor is Tango's ≈1.2 s for an outfielder's pickup-to-release plus the infielder's tag (catch-throw note §3). Re-checked by the approach job (2026-11-20, [approach note §5](calibration/2026-11-20-approach.md)): with the approach and the across term, a free speed of 2.0 / 2.6 / 3.2 m/s gave an outfielder's pickup-to-release 1.11 / 1.06 / 1.02 s (main 1.08 s) and triples 0.93 / 0.84 / 0.76 a team-game (main 1.02, MLB about 0.14); 3.2 m/s is nearest Tango's ≈0.95 s. Under Q33 it was 2 / 4.75 = 0.42 s, the old flat rule's no-charge point. The dive term is unchanged. The pivot man and the relay man owe the plant of their motion away only (above). The turn is unchanged.
  - **Drift and accuracy (Q32; built 2026-11-20, `work/approach`).** Drift off the line widens the direction error and costs speed through the hurry rule's off-balance terms, now in proportion to the drift he still carries: a release at hurry h comes `elapsed` = set − h·(set − earliest) after he had the ball; he has been braking at `BRAKE` since, so he still carries v_res = max(0, v_off − `BRAKE`·elapsed) off the line, and the off-balance weight is w = min(1, v_res / `PLANT_FREE_MPS`) (`SetUp::with_drift`, `off_weight`). Direction σ × (1 + h·(k_rush + k_off·w)); speed × (1 − h·(0.10 + 0.08·w)). A set throw has none (the plant has stopped it: v_res = 0 at set). Before, `off_balance` counted 1 whenever any footwork was owed; now a hurried release after a small drift is less wild (from 3.6 m/s at an infielder's earliest release, w = 0.55), one after a big drift the same as before (from 7 m/s, w = 1). Taking it on the ground from a dive, or where the motion is not known (the pivot's and relay man's no-early-release set-ups, a teammate's or runner's estimate), w = 1 as before. A release at hurry 0 is set and has none. No source for the weight's shape [E, unsourced]: `PLANT_FREE_MPS` is the drift the transfer absorbs (`PLANT_OVERLAP_S`).
  - **Motion across the line: decided in Q32, deferred by the owner's Q33 (2026-11-19), restored 2026-11-20 with the approach (§5.2).** Q32 has a fielder moving across the line to his target stop that motion too (v_off above). Built first without the approach it charged plays real fielders do not pay for, because the sim's fielders reached a ball at full speed and never slowed into it: outfield pickup-to-release rose from 1.01 s to 1.46 s against an empirical ≈1.2 s with the tag (Tango 2020), runs 4.24 → 4.69 [4.47 → 4.94], and three frozen infield plays failed ([catch-throw note §6](calibration/2026-11-19-catch-throw.md#6-the-owner-question-q33-and-the-answer)). The owner's Q33 charged only motion away until the approach was built; with it, the across term is back (the [approach note](calibration/2026-11-20-approach.md) measures both).
  - **The off-balance throw** is the hurry rule below, unchanged: he can let go from 0.6 × (`Transfer` + turn), slower and wilder, and more so off balance. Its 10% speed loss with no step behind the throw lies between the measured standing throw without a run-up, 93% of one with it (Wagner et al. 2011, read), and 89% (Bayios and Boudolos 1998, read in Wagner).
  - **Coming through the catch:** his release speed at full effort is `ArmStrength` + gain, gain = `MOMENTUM_GAIN` × (min(a, `HOP_MAX_MPS`) − `HOP_MPS`)⁺, before effort and hurry scale it. `HOP_MPS` = 3.0 m/s (read: the body's speed toward the goal in a handball throw with a run-up, against 1.5 m/s without, Wagner et al. 2011): a crow hop from a stand reaches it inside his `Transfer`, and `ArmStrength` (Statcast's top throws, made with a crow hop) is that throw. `MOMENTUM_GAIN` = 1.6/1.5 = 1.07 (read: the same study's +1.6 m/s of ball for +1.5 m/s of body). `HOP_MAX_MPS` = 5.0 m/s [E, unsourced]: the gain is at most 2.1 m/s (4.8 mph), inside the 6.1 mph median spread between an outfielder's hardest throw and his top-10% mean (Savant 2025, read). No accuracy cost (Wagner et al.: mean radial error 0.30 m with and without the run-up). So a flat-footed catch followed by a crow hop gives `ArmStrength`; the throw that "gives less" is the hurried one without the hop, and a drifting catch costs the plant or the hurry. None after taking it on the ground from a dive. **The gain is the crow hop's, a set throw's step** (2026-11-20): a release at hurry h keeps (1 − h) of it, so the arm-only throw on the run (the slow roller barehanded and thrown sidearm off one foot, the backhand thrown before the plant) has none; only the hurry rule's speed applies to it.
  - **The set-up behind a fly (built 2026-11-20, `work/approach`; first built and taken out on `work/catch-throw`, [catch-throw note §5](calibration/2026-11-19-catch-throw.md#5-after-the-change-workcatch-throw-against-main-ab5c374); why it dropped catches and the fix: [approach note §2](calibration/2026-11-20-approach.md)).** An outfielder under a fly he has called sure (his call tier 3, §5.7), with fewer than two out and a runner on base, expects to throw to the base ahead of the lead runner. When his own read (`t_int`, `int_pt`) gives him time to get to a spot behind his catch point, away from that base, be settled on it, and still come through with `SETUP_SPARE_S` = 0.5 s [E, unsourced] in hand, he goes there (`fly_setup`). *The spot's depth* is `SETUP_BACK_M` = 2.5 m [E; coaching "one or two steps behind", not a measurement], deeper if his acceleration needs it to come through at `SETUP_THROUGH_MPS` = 4 m/s [E], a little over the crow hop's 3 m/s, leaving as below (`setup_depth`, at most `SETUP_MAX_M` = 5 m [E]): 3.3 m for an average outfielder (8.2 m/s, τ 1.0 s). *The timing* is the movement model's, never a flat pace (revised 2026-11-20): settled on the spot means what is left of his reaction, his motion across the line to it braked off at `BRAKE` and along it the run to a stop there (`settle_time`; nearer than his braking distance, he carries past and comes back); he comes through when his run from where he is to the catch point, from his actual motion (`run_time_to`: his acceleration, any stop and turn), plus the slack a sure call keeps (`CALL_SLACK_S` = 0.3 s, §5.7) is what is left before the ball comes down by his read, so that he stays sure of it coming through. On his way to the spot or waiting on it (within `SETUP_AT_M` = 1 m [E] he counts as there; he decides every tick, not only at his reads) he goes on only while he can still be settled on it in time; otherwise, or caught short of it when it is time to go, he plays the ball plainly, unless he is already behind the ball: the catch point ahead of him within `SETUP_BEHIND_DEG` = 30° [E] of the line through it to the base. Behind the ball with no time to settle on the spot (a fly in front of him he runs in on), he comes through from where he is when he has his run, the slack and `SETUP_SPARE_S` in hand. *Only on a settled read* (2026-11-20, Opus approach review P2-2): he enters the set-up, either way, only once his read of where the ball comes down is good to within the spot's least depth, `ARRIVE_SIGMAS` times his doubt (the σ of his tracked read along his line of sight, §5.1; a read before his first look has none) within `SETUP_BACK_M`, so that the spot he goes to is behind where the ball will come down unless his read is more than a σ off; until then he plays the ball plainly, going at the catch point under control (§5.2, arriving under control), and drifts back to the spot from under it. (Chosen on a first read 9–12 m off, the set-up took a man a metre or more off his plain path in the first two seconds and he could not get back when the read moved: seed 207 play 66, a pop-up, and seed 1042 play 28, frozen plays. A gate at `SETUP_AT_M` (1 m) catches those too but on a high fly comes too late to come through faster than the crow hop's 3 m/s: `an_outfielder_with_time_sets_up_and_comes_through_the_catch` comes through at 1.4 m/s; at the spot's depth 5.4 m/s.) *Coming through* he is timed against the ball (§5.3): his top speed is held at the speed with which the movement step (the speed law up to it, braking at `BRAKE` down to it) brings him to his latest read of the catch point as it comes down (`paced_speed`), at most his top speed, recomputed every tick; so he speeds up if a re-read leaves him behind and never runs past the point before the ball. From the spot he comes through at about 4 m/s; from where he is, faster or slower as the time allows; when he has no time he does not set up at all. The catch comes first. It uses only his own read and what everyone sees (runners, outs); his read error can make him early or late, as a real fielder's can. With no room behind the point (a wall) he plays it plainly. Whether a fielder does it, and how well, will later be a rating. *Why the second build lost catches* (approach note §5–§6; frozen plays `seed_155_play_23_…` and `seed_264_play_109_…` in `throw_choices.rs`): it timed the come-through at 4 m/s over his distance without his acceleration and let him make for the spot whenever he could reach it, settled or not. A centre fielder running across to a spot 2.5 m behind a deep fly met a late re-read that put the catch point under him at 6.5 m/s, ran on past it toward the new spot, could not get back, lost his call and the left fielder took it on the bounce; a left fielder whose first read had a sinking liner high and deep started for the spot behind it and took it on the short hop. Timed and gated as above, both are caught as on main. *Why the first build dropped catches* (traced on three of the 15 games in 100 that it changed, all fly balls it turned into hits): it started him through when a 2.5 m run from rest at 4 m/s (about 1.3 s) was left, while he was still moving in toward the spot at 3–4 m/s, and sent him at a point 3 m past the catch point at a fixed 4 m/s; he reached the catch point 0.4–0.6 s before the ball, ran on past it, and the ball fell behind him.
  - **Where the man in the throw stands** (revised 2026-11-20, Opus catch-throw review P2-1). `relay_job` (§5.7) names him: toward home the first baseman, or the third baseman on a ball the left fielder fields (revised 2026-11-21; before, a ball from x < −15 m); toward third the shortstop; toward second, on a ball through the infield on the ground, the middle infielder on the ball's side (built 2026-11-21, §5.7); on a deep ball (over `RELAY_DEEP_M` = 80 m from home, hit over or past an outfielder: §5.7, `deep_ball`) the nearer middle infielder as the relay man, and with a play at home the first baseman behind him as the cutoff man home, `through_spot` short of the plate on the line from the relay man (the double cut, built 2026-11-22, §5.7). The relay man of a deep ball stands at the relay spot, the throw's length less `RELAY_OF_THROW_M` = 45 m short of the base (`cut_spot`). On a shallower ball the cutoff man of a throw through stands `CUTOFF_M` = 14 m (about 45 ft) short of the base, or halfway on a short throw (`through_spot`), so that a throw passing him at his head carries to the base on the fly or one hop, as the owner's write-up has it (docs/baseball-throw.md). The first build put every man at the relay spot, so a throw home passed him 45 m from the thrower, landed 13–35 m short of the plate and reached it rolling after two to four bounces.
  - **The route.** A man is lined up in a throw when he has the relay job for that base, stands between the holder and the base and within `CUT_LINE_M` = 5 m [E] of its line (a man lined up toward home is not in a throw to second, nor to third). The route and the direct throw's cut height use this one test (`men_in_throw`; revised 2026-11-22, Opus double-cut review P3-1: the route had taken any man known on any cutoff job, off the line, or lined up for another base ahead of the man lined up for this one). With two men lined up toward one base (the double cut, §5.7: the relay man and the first baseman behind him), the relay route goes to the nearer of them (revised 2026-11-22; before, the first-numbered man with a cutoff job, which would have been the first baseman). Only when nobody is lined up for the base may the route go through a man standing on its line the same way with a cutoff job for another base, by time alone, the throw not kept low for him: the ball back to second with no play, through the relay man lined up toward home, who stands on that line for a ball to centre (§5.7, "the quicker way"). A man off the line is never the route. Then the holder has two routes:
    - *Low through the cutoff man:* the throw is aimed as usual (chest height at the base) unless that passes the man in it higher than `CUT_HEIGHT_M` = 2.0 m [E; the owner's "low, at his head or chest"]; then it is launched to pass at 2.0 m where he is going, his spot by the known convention (`relay_spot`), not where he stands at the release (he is often still on his way in, and a throw kept low 25 m out rolls in; Opus catch-throw re-review P3-3), and comes on by the flight and bounce physics. Through a cutoff man 45 ft out it lands 7–10 m short and reaches the base on one hop, 0.02–0.06 s after the plain aim would; past a deep ball's relay man it comes down 13–35 m short and rolls in, which is why the relay wins there. A high throw over his head is never chosen.
    - *To the relay man:* as built (the relay man's exchange above).
    - He takes the route with the smaller believed time plus miss cost (with a relay man lined up for another base, by time alone, as before). A throw's miss cost is the expected time to run, from a stand at league speed (`run_time`), whatever of its miss is beyond the receiving reach (1.5 m): the miss X ~ N(0, L·σ) for a throw of length L and direction σ (`ArmAccuracy` at full effort), E[`run_time`((|X| − 1.5)⁺)] by a 48-point midpoint rule (revised 2026-11-20, review P3-2: the first build ran the mean excess, which overstated each throw's cost about twice). The direct route has one long throw; the relay two short ones. By the physics with the cost (note §4), the throw through a cutoff man 45 ft out is quicker than the relay up to about 246 ft at 34 m/s, 276 ft at 37 m/s and 302 ft at 40 m/s; the owner's "about 280 ft" lies among them. In the game the man who goes out decides it first: up to `RELAY_DEEP_M` (80 m, 262 ft) it is a cutoff man 45 ft out and the throw goes through him; beyond, a relay man and the throw goes to him. With no cutoff man in the throw (a throw to second, any infield throw) the plain aim is used, as now.
    - The cut call (the catcher's "Cut!") is not built: the cutoff man never intercepts a throw aimed past him (a separate task).
  - **Receiving a throw that comes down short.** The receiver's read flies his read of the throw on past where he sees it coming down, with the bounce and roll physics (no tilt), and takes it where it is nearest his spot at catchable height, on the hop or rolling, when it comes down more than `STEP_IN_M` = 3 m [E] short of where he would take it in the air; within that he steps in and takes it in the air, as before. A relay man out in the open always takes it in the air.
  - **What the players know** (each follows the truth where a player could know it):
    - the holder's race (`throw_race`, the effort choice): his own plant and gain (his own body), the cuttable throw's time when he knows a cutoff man is lined up (teammates' jobs as he knows them, `seen`), the miss costs from his and the relay man's `ArmAccuracy` (known to teammates);
    - the fielders' and runners' believed time of a cuttable throw: tables like `ThrowTable`, from the same flight and bounce physics in a reference atmosphere and park (`ThrowTable::build_cut_to`, a belief): through a cutoff man home, thrown from straight-away centre; through a cutoff man to third, thrown from left-centre (`THIRD_TABLE_FROM`; throws to third cross the dirt differently, re-review P3-4); past a deep ball's relay man. Which applies is the base and which spot the man in the throw is going to (`Cut::of`). Against the world's throw along the real line (test `the_cut_tables_match_the_worlds_cuttable_throw`): home from −40° to +40° within 0.02 s; third from left to centre within 0.03 s and nearer the world on average than the plain table; the world's own throw through `throw_to` with the man still on his way in within 0.05 s (`the_throw_through_is_kept_low_where_the_cutoff_man_is_going`);
    - a runner's read of a held ball (§6.4): the plant from the holder's visible motion at the catch (as the old footwork term was), the gain from it (no gain after a dive he saw), and the cuttable throw only when a man with the relay job for that base visibly stands on its line (`cut_seen`: the jobs the fielders show, `known_job`, and where they stand), the test the holder's throw uses; his typical throw (`typical_throw_time`) and his read of an appeal throw (`catch_hold_s`) likewise. (The first build had every runner believe it on every outfield throw to third or home from 50 m out, where the world threw it on 7 of 190; it raised tags from second for a reason false in the world, review P1-1);
    - a runner's read of a throw in the air (§6.4) names the base it is going to from the direction of the throw: of the bags on its line ahead of where he first sees it (within `LOW_THROW_READ_M` = 8 m [E] of the line), the one nearest where it comes down through catching height (else the nearest bag, as before), timed there. When a man with the cutoff job for that base visibly stands on its line (`known_job`) and the ball comes down 8 m or more short of the bag, he knows it is the low throw meant to arrive on a hop: he follows it on through the bounce with the same physics and times it when it comes within a receiver's reach of the bag (or at its closest pass). A fielder the ball comes down at, not short of that point by more than `LOW_THROW_SHORT_M` = 1 m [E] and within `LOW_THROW_MAN_M` = 4 m [E], is the man it is thrown to (a relay), and the relay read covers it. With nobody in the throw a noisy read that comes down short is read as before, so ordinary throws are read exactly as before. (Revised 2026-11-20, Opus catch-throw re-review P2-1, P3-1. The first versions followed any read coming down 3 m, then 8 m, from every bag and named the first bag it passed within 2.5 m: a read passing second a couple of metres wide was named home, first or third. That made runners bolder on ordinary throws, +0.07 triples and +0.05 outfield assists a team-game on both seed sets. Meanwhile the throw through a cutoff man was never followed, because he stood within 4 m of where it came down, and runners read it at the plate 0.5–0.75 s early. Probe, 1,000 noisy reads 0.3 s after release: plain throws to second from centre, left-centre and right-centre now name the wrong base exactly as often as the old read, 18–100 in 1,000. The throw through a cutoff man home is read 0.07–0.10 s before it reaches the plate, against 0.48–0.69 s before.)
    - a fielder's set-up before the catch, and his approach to a ball on the ground (rounding it included), are not read by the runners: they read the catch as it happens (his motion then, the across term included through `footwork_s`), as before. A runner's read of a ball not yet fielded (`pickup_time_to`) stays the fielder's believed intercept plus the typical transfer (`TYPICAL_TRANSFER`, 0.75 s), with no plant: it was so before and does not see an approach. That the world's pickup-to-release is now longer than that belief on balls taken across the line is measured in the approach note (§5) and left as it is (a runner who believes the ball quicker than it is holds, which is the cautious error);
    - the holder's race and effort choice (`throw_race`) use his own plant, drift and gain (`set_up_for`, `throw_speed` with the hurry), so the across term and the drift weight are in his own judgment; teammates' estimates of a feed (the continuation, the pivot man's set-up) use the same rules from what they see;
    - the approach and the set-up are each fielder's own plan from his own read; nobody else reads them.
- **Whether he hurries** is his own judgment of the race (§5.6's times, with what hurrying costs his arm): with no play on, or at least `SAFE_MARGIN_S` = 0.25 s [E] in hand from a full set-up, he gets set. With less he lets go as soon as waiting longer would leave him under that margin, and at his earliest if he never had it. He does not hurry unless it gets the ball there at least 0.01 s sooner (`HURRY_GAIN_MIN_S`; it does not when the man covering the bag is not there yet). A holder close enough to carry the ball to the bag himself (within 6 m) goes after his plain `Transfer`: he sets up for no throw.
- **Throw speed:** the fielder chooses an effort e between `EFFORT_MIN` = 0.80 [E] and 1.0 of `ArmStrength` (rule in the Built paragraph below). Directional σ falls linearly with effort: σ × (1 − (1 − e)/0.2 × (1 − r)), r = 46.9/61.1 = 0.768, so 0.768 at e = 0.80. Two estimates [E]: that the measured ratio of total target error (Freeston, Adams and Rooney 2014: 46.9 against 61.1 cm at 26.4 against 33.0 m/s) carries over to the directional σ alone (total error also holds speed and timing scatter, calibration sources note), and that the error is linear between those two speeds.
- **Trajectory:** ballistic solve with drag and typical throw backspin (1500 rpm [E]), aiming at the receiver's chest target. Long outfield throws may choose a one-hop trajectory when it arrives sooner (the bounce is physical).
- **Execution noise** (purpose `ThrowExecution`, `motor::execute_throw`; draw order speed, then direction): direction σ as above · F_composure (not built); speed σ 3% [E].
- The receiver runs the standard capture test. A first baseman uses §5.8.

**Built 2026-11-12 (`bb_physics::throw`, `sim/crates/bb_game/src/live.rs`):** throws fly with drag and 1500 rpm backspin, aimed by the thrower's own solve (the flatter elevation that reaches chest height at the target, found by stepping up from below the vacuum answer, since with drag and backspin the height at the target peaks before 35°; none past 35°). A set throw is released at an effort e between 0.80 and 1.0 of his arm (built 2026-11-14, speed for accuracy): `ArmStrength` is Statcast's arm strength, the mean of a player's hardest throws (top 1% at first base, 5% at second, short and third, 10% in the outfield; Baseball Savant leaderboard), so e = 1 is a max-effort throw. He throws as hard as the play needs and no harder, by his own read of the race: for a force with a second force at first possible behind it (§5.6 continuation, at any p) full effort, the feed being a race for that out, since easing it can only make the second out less likely (2026-11-14, Opus infield-fixes review P2-1: the first rule, full effort only for p > 0.3, eased a first baseman's feed home at p 0.11 by 0.18 s and lost a 3-2-3 that full effort completed by 0.04 s); for any other out attempt the least effort whose believed arrival (receiver's readiness, 0.05 s handling and any tag time included) still beats the runner by `SAFE_MARGIN_S`, else full effort; with no out to try for, full effort while any runner is visibly moving (he could take a base on the throw; at the floor this cost 0.24 runs a team-game in a counterfactual) and the floor only with every runner standing; through a cutoff man full effort. Direction error grows with effort as measured: σ × (1 − (1 − e)/0.2 × (1 − 46.9/61.1)), 0.768 at e = 0.80 (`EFFORT_MIN`, `EFFORT_MIN_ERROR_RATIO`, `effort_sigma_mult`; Freeston, Adams and Rooney 2014: 26.4 against 33.0 m/s, total target error 46.9 against 61.1 cm). Hurrying still costs speed and accuracy on top. The old vacuum throw at 0.92 of the arm stood in for drag. Speed noise (3%) now moves where the throw comes down. The receiver starts on the spot the throw was aimed at; after his reaction time he reads the throw every 0.25 s (the batted-ball read model of §5.1's interim version: a noisy estimate of where it is and how it moves, flown on with his own throw physics) and goes to where he believes it is catchable, between scoop height (0.15 m) and 2.4 m, nearest that spot. He never sees the realized path (Codex review 2026-11-12 #1). *Since 2026-11-12 (momentum):* he weighs his read against the knowledge that throws come near where they are aimed: his believed catch point is the aim point plus k times the read's offset, k = σ_p²/(σ_p² + σ_r²), with σ_p = 5% of the throw's length (`THROW_MISS_FRAC` [E], at least 0.5 m) and σ_r his read's depth error now plus its velocity error flown to the spot. From far off he barely moves; as the read firms up he goes where it says. Without this a catcher's first read of a 57 m relay throw sent him 17 m behind the plate. Taking a throw at a bag, he keeps his foot on it when both the weighed catch point and his read alone are within 0.9 × the 1.5 m receiving reach (`BAG_REACH_FRAC`; §5.8's stretch); otherwise he goes to the weighed point (Codex momentum review #4: the weighing alone could pin him to the bag while a throw passed 1.7 m to the side). Fielders and runners judge throw times from a table of the same physics in a reference atmosphere (`ThrowTable`, a belief). One-hop throws are not chosen yet: a throw beyond the arm's reach leaves at 35° and bounces.

**A4 carries (2026-11-14):** the proximity shortcuts described above are replaced by the common comparison in §5.6. A carry within `carry_max_m` = 8 m [E] needs no throw setup; its estimated arrival includes remaining get-up (the existing dive movement lock), current heading/momentum and the movement law to the existing 0.6 m force-touch radius. There is no automatic carry on proximity and no automatic replacement of a selected throw within 6 m. Carrying to touch a bag is a **force** option only while the runner is forced there. Carrying to an unforced runner's bag is a **tag plan**: be on the bag before him and tag him as he comes in (the tag is glove/runner contact within `tag_reach_m` = 1.2 m [E] while he is off the bag). It is weighed as a tag, never as a touch: `TWO_OUT_TAG_W` with two out, no force continuation, and only for a runner visibly moving to that bag (§5.6). Throws to unforced runners remain eligible; a moving tag away from the bag and rundowns remain deferred.

*Deviation from the first A4 text (2026-11-14, Opus worker):* that text admitted a non-force carry only when the holder was already within `tag_reach_m` of the bag. The A4 probe on main `52d6632` (seeds 0–255) found 37 sustained non-force carries starting farther out, and **36 ended in a tag out at that bag** (31 at second, mostly batters stretching a single, the middle infielder holding the ball 1.3–7.8 m from the bag). Banning them gives away a routine tag no real fielder would pass up. The 17 flagged "non-force carry over a sure force" cases were 13 one-tick readings of a stopped runner's stale speed (removed by the visible-target gate) and 4 sustained carries, 3 of them tag outs. With the common comparison a tag plan can no longer bypass a force: with two out a sure force beats a sure tag (1.0 against 0.9); with fewer than two out the existing base weights prefer the lead runner (third 1.1 against first 0.9) when both are sure.

**The double-play pivot (built 2026-11-14, `work/dp-pivot`; sources in [the tag-up and pivot note, sections 6–7](calibration/2026-11-14-tag-ups.md#6-double-play-pivots)).** A force at second followed by a throw to first is a pivot, its own action and not a relay. It applies to the man at second with the ball while a force there is still to be made (a runner forced from first who has not reached it): the receiver of a throw there (the feed). A fielder who carried the ball to the bag himself (6U-3, 4U-3) caught nothing there: he keeps the set-up he spent holding the ball (§5.6) and throws as an ordinary holder from the bag (Gemini pivot review P2-2, 2026-11-14). Only a throw to first is the pivot; a pivot man who throws to third or home is an ordinary thrower, and a feed receiver who takes it off the bag but throws rather than going to the bag is an ordinary receiver (P2-1). A throw to second with no force on is an ordinary receiver's (an earlier version made every receiver at second a pivot man, and a cutoff there could not throw home before touching the bag). If the force is gone before he has the bag, he is an ordinary receiver from his catch.
- **He must have the bag.** The pivot's clock starts when he holds the ball with a foot on second (within the 0.6 m force-touch radius): at the catch if he took the feed on the bag, otherwise when he reaches it.
- **Pivot time** `pivot_s` = **0.80 s** at a `Transfer` grade of 50, scaled by the same grade rule as `Transfer` (×1.08 per 10 grade points; 0.69 s at grade 70). [E] anchored on the sources: 0.80 s is the middle of the 0.7–0.9 s coaching norm (low confidence, unmeasured), and grade 70 lands near the one measured elite pivot, J.J. Hardy's 0.67 s (FanGraphs 2014, one play). No split by pivot man or by feed type was found; none is invented.
- **Turn and footwork** use §5.5's rules, from the way he faces and moves as he gets the bag: he faces the way he is moving (faster than `PIVOT_FACE_MIN_MPS` = 1 m/s [E], below `FACE_MIN_MPS` because a covering man early for the feed brakes onto the bag at √(2·BRAKE·0.6 m) = 2.39 m/s at the touch radius; one late for it crosses at about 4.7 m/s there, §5.3), or else the feed coming to him. The footwork is owed only to a man moving away from first faster than `AWAY_MPS` = 2 m/s as he takes it; a second baseman at the Savant double-play depth (42.5 m) braking onto the bag moves away from first at 1.3 m/s at most, so he owes none (the earlier text said +0.2 s for a second baseman; the movement law does not produce it). A shortstop coming across the bag toward first owes none either, and his turn is the straight-ahead quarter turn already inside `pivot_s`. A man crossing the bag (§5.3) moving away from first faster than the plant's free speed (3.2 m/s) owes the plant: a second baseman taking the bag from the first-base side at the crossing speed pays part of what crossing gains him, a shortstop coming across toward first nothing (as in real double plays).
- **No early release.** The measured pivot times are already the quick release a pivot man makes in a double-play race, so the pivot has no further hurry: its earliest and its set release are both `pivot_s + turn + footwork`, at full arm and normal accuracy. (The general hurry rule would let it go at 0.6 of that, 0.48 s, which is the too-quick pivot this replaces: 0.7 × `Transfer` = 0.455 s, with no turn and a 0.27 s hurried floor.)
- **The runner from first (seam, set to nothing).** A slide (A6) will cost the pivot man time and accuracy. The seam is `PIVOT_SLIDE_DELAY_S` = 0 s and `PIVOT_SLIDE_SIGMA_MULT` = 1, applied to every pivot. There are no slides yet, and no break-up effect is modeled.
- **The holder's estimate** of a double play (§5.6, the continuation) uses this same model, from what he can see. The pivot man gets the bag at the later of the feed's arrival and his own arrival there (within the touch radius, by the receiver's estimate below, timed to that feed). He faces and moves the way he takes the bag (braking onto it or crossing it) if he is still on his way, or faces the feed if he is already there. His `Transfer` is known to his teammates.
- Other receivers at a base (first, third, home) keep 0.7 × `Transfer` and no turn: no source.

**The covering man and the receiver's estimate (built 2026-11-21, `work/dp-cover`; the owner's saved plays 66 and 168 of seed 51001, [saved-plays note §1](calibration/2026-11-20-saved-plays-51001.md#1-plays-66-and-168-no-double-play-attempt); measurements in [the dp-cover note](calibration/2026-11-21-dp-cover.md)).**
- **When the covering man believes the throw comes** (`Sim::cover_feeds`), which decides whether he brakes onto a force base or crosses it (§5.3): while a teammate holds the ball, from what he sees of him, as the holder would throw with time in hand (set): up from his catch (after a dive's get-up or a stretch's cost), then set with his known `Transfer` (0.7 of it for a throw he took, `pivot_s` for a feed at second with the force on), the turn from the way he faced taking it and the plant from how he was moving, then the throw's flight at his known arm, from where he stands; the holder's set-up clock and his choice are his own. (A first version counted only the transfer: a pitcher covering first crossed the bag while the first baseman who had fielded the ball diving was still getting up, caught the throw 1.5 m past the bag and lost the batter; seed 23 play 268 on the branch.) With the throw in the air to him at that bag, from his own read of it (`receiver_read`: when his read has it at the spot he takes it), and before his first read what he believed as it was let go. With the ball loose, or with the holder visibly carrying it to that bag himself (running at it faster than 3 m/s, within about 25° of the line, not yet on it; Opus dp-cover review P3-3), none: he brakes onto the bag as before.
- **The receiver's estimate** (`receiver_ready`, `cover_reach`): a teammate judges when a covering man is within receiving reach of the bag (`RECEIVE_REACH` = 1.5 m) by stepping the movement itself from the man's visible position and velocity and his known speed: §5.3's rule where a force is pending, timed to *the man's own* belief of the throw (worked out by the holder as the man does, from what the man sees of him; timed to the holder's own hurried release it was 0.1 s early for a man who in fact braked: Opus dp-cover review P3-2), braking onto the bag otherwise; for at most 4 s (`COVER_STEP_CAP_S` [E]), the rest at top speed. It decides when the holder lets the throw go and every throw option's ball time. **The hold:** a man not yet within reach counts as ready `RECEIVE_MARGIN_S` = 0.1 s [E, unsourced] after he gets there, and the holder waits while his throw would reach the bag before that (it was 0.05 s *before* he got there: with the exact estimate, throws arrived as a man coming from the side reached 1.5 m and passed out of his reach, the pitcher's feeds to second 39 of 115 untaken on seeds 0–255, seed 12 play 105; Opus dp-cover review P1-1). A man already within reach is ready now. A force is made at the touch: a throw to a force base is timed to the later of the ball's arrival, the man's readiness and his coming within the touch radius (`BAG_TOUCH_M` = 0.6 m [E], the world's own force-touch, pivot-start and pivot-wait radius) by the same stepping, as a carry is timed to the touch radius; the double play's second force at first likewise. (Until 2026-11-21 it was a run from rest at league-typical speed, 8 m/s and τ 0.9 s, to 0.5 m from the bag: a man already running at the bag was believed late, by 0.7 s in saved play 66, and the shortstop took the sure out at first over the lead runner.)

**The relay man's exchange (built 2026-11-19, `work/relay-exchange`; sources and measurements in [the throw-race note](calibration/2026-11-19-throw-race.md), §4, §6 and §8).** The cutoff man who takes a relay has his own catch-to-release, as the pivot man does.
- **Which throws.** Every throw a holder sends through his cutoff man (a `Role::Relay` fielder; the thrown ball's `relay` flag). It applies whatever the relay man does next: throws home, throws to another base (a cut and throw), or holds. A throw aimed at a base and caught there (a receiver at the bag) is not a relay, and nor is the pivot: those keep their own rules above. The sim has no cutoff man who intercepts a throw aimed past him. A throw that goes by him ends his job, and he takes his own next job at once (§5.7).
- **Relay time** `relay_s` = **0.75 s** at a `Transfer` grade of 50 (`RELAY_S_50` [E]), scaled by the same grade rule as `Transfer` (×1.08 per 10 grade points; 0.64 s at grade 70).
