| vs. LHB, Standard (`firstBase=0`, `n=19,287`) | 123.9 ft / +37.6° | 148.2 ft / +19.1° | 116.1 ft / -27.1° | 152.6 ft / -6.2° | `batSide=L&firstBase=0&shift=0` |
| vs. LHB, Double-play (`firstBase=1`, `n=6,172`) | 88.9 ft / +41.0° | 143.1 ft / +19.0° | 113.8 ft / -27.2° | 148.8 ft / -5.7° | `batSide=L&firstBase=1&shift=0` |

Before each pitch (`bb_game::play::home_positions_for`), the defense takes the batter's plate side (`Right` / `Left`) and selects `DoublePlay` when a runner occupies first base with fewer than two outs (`runner_on_first && outs < 2`), otherwise `Standard`. When second base is also occupied (`runner_on_second` in `1st & 2nd` or bases loaded), the runner on first cannot steal second unopposed, so `1B` plays behind the runner at `Standard` depth while `2B`, `3B`, and `SS` hold `DoublePlay` depth. Fielders reach their positions between plays (no simulation of that walk).

What stays `[E]` or omitted:
- Savant's `firstBase=1` bucket is `"1B Only"` (second and third empty, pooling 0, 1, and 2 outs, dominated by `< 2` outs), while `firstBase=27` (`"Other"`) pools `2B`-only, `3B`-only, `2nd&3rd`, `1st&2nd`, `1st&3rd`, and bases loaded (mixed with RISP/infield-in). Applying `DoublePlay` when `runner_on_first && outs < 2` and keeping `1B` at `Standard` when `runner_on_second` is used here rather than pulling pooled `firstBase=27` values.
- Savant's `Shaded: Yes` (`shift=1`, heavily used vs. pull-heavy LHB where `3B` backs to 131.4 ft / -21.9°) awaits per-batter pull-tendency manager shading (PLAN §10.1), so average hitters use `Standard` (`Shaded: No`, which already rotates the infield toward the batter's pull side relative to center).
- "Infield in" / corners-in presets have no separate published filter in Savant's positioning leaderboard (`Strategic` pools multiple alignments in Statcast's glossary and `firstBase=27` pools 2B/3B/RISP states) and are not added as unsourced guesses; custom manager presets remain PLAN §10.1.
- Outfielders (`LF` 290 ft / -30°, `CF` 320 ft / 0°, `RF` 290 ft / +30°), `P` (60 ft / 0°), and `C` (0, -0.9 m) keep their existing positions. The secondary ground-ball break's range `INFIELD_BREAK_R` = `MAX_INFIELD_DEPTH_M` + 5.0 m [E] (46.60 + 5.0 ≈ 51.6 m) follows from the deepest Savant pre-pitch spot so secondary middle infielders (and `1B` in `[2, 3, 4, 5]`, on the first-base side only, §5.7) break on infield grounders while keeping `Cover(2)` protected when a partner already chases. It is used for that break only. The settle rule, backups, the no-play return throw and the runner's deep-fly read use the infield/outfield boundary `INFIELD_R` = 47.40 m, the edge of the infield dirt (rules-spec §5.8), which is not tied to the alignment table (Opus infield-fixes review P2-2; for a few hours on 2026-11-14 all five used 51.6 m).

### 5.10 Anticipation: the jump (owner request, built 2026-11-12)

The owner asked that fielders get a jump on the ball, anticipating where it will be hit from the pitch location and the batter's side, with a rating for it alongside range. `Anticipation` is that rating. Range is not a separate attribute: it comes out of `Speed`, `Acceleration`, `Agility`, `Reaction` and `Anticipation` together.

**Three reads, combined (`sim/crates/bb_game/src/live.rs`, `jump_at_contact`; the third added 2026-11-13):**
- **Before contact: the pitch.** He reads where the pitch crosses the plate (horizontal, σ `antic_sigma`, purpose `FielderAnticipation` sub-index 0). With the batter's side it gives a prior on the ball's direction: the batter's typical pull (1.23° to his pull side, `MEAN_PULL_DEG`) plus the shift for that location (`anticipated_pull_deg`: within −2.8° to +0.6° by 10 cm bin on the v2 swing; v1's table ran −6.5° to +7.7°; means by bin from `sim/crates/bb_game/examples/spray_by_location.rs` with left-handers mirrored, a belief about the contact model), scattered by 20.5° (`SPRAY_SCATTER_DEG`). *Refit for the v2 swing, 2026-11-14 (docs/calibration/2026-11-14-swing-model.md); MLB pulls inside pitches much more than the v2 sim does (−1.3° to +14.3° pull by location in the 2025 Statcast sample), mostly because the sim's batters are early on outside pitches.*
- **At contact: the bat.** He reads the bat's horizontal angle at the instant of contact (the barrel's yaw, knob to tip, `atan2(x, y)`), with error σ `swing_sigma` (degrees of bat angle, sub-index 1; derived below from how well a person can tell where a moving object was at the moment of an event). He reads the bat, not the ball: the orchestrator (`sim/crates/bb_game/src/game.rs`) samples the executed swing at the actual contact time and passes the bat's true yaw into the play (`LiveInput::bat_yaw`); `jump_at_contact` adds each fielder's read error, and his belief (`jump_from_reads`) sees only that noisy angle, the noisy pitch location and the batter's side. The ball's launch direction is never an input before his reaction gate (§5.1). *Revised 2026-11-12 (Codex anticipation review #1):* the first version read the true outgoing spray plus noise, which carried the collision's result (spin and contact point move spray by several degrees at the same bat angle).
- **What the bat angle means (his belief).** The face angle is the barrel's yaw less 90° for a right-handed batter (mirrored for a left-hander; negative = his pull side): 0 when the bat is square to the pitch line. Over 5,521 fair balls in play (96 games, `sim/crates/bb_game/examples/spray_by_bat_angle.rs`, refit for the v2 swing 2026-11-14; v1 gave 5.2° + 1.36 × face, 18.6°) spray ≈ 6.91° + 0.939 × face (`SWING_SPRAY_A_DEG`, `SWING_SPRAY_B` [E]), with 18.0° of scatter about the line (`SWING_SPRAY_RESID_DEG` [E]; r = 0.48; the face itself varies 10.5° between swings). The scatter is the collision: where on the barrel and how far under or over the ball the bat met it. So the bat's cue for the spray has σ = √((0.939 σ_read)² + 18.0²): about 48° at grade 80, 89° at grade 50, 171° at grade 20 (with the derived read σ below; the pitch prior is 20.5°). The fit is a belief and must be refit when the swing or contact model changes. It is a marginal fit, so fusing it with the location prior counts some of the location's information twice; the overlap is small (location moves spray about 7°, the face 9.5° × 1.36).
- **During the swing: the swing's lead (owner decision 2026-11-13, built the same day).** He times the bat's launch against the ball in flight: how long before the pitch reaches the plate the bat starts forward. The true quantity is the lead L = t_plate − t_start (s), with t_plate the pitch's free flight crossing the judgment plane and t_start the executed swing's start (`Swing::t_start`, 10% of peak angular speed); the orchestrator (`sim/crates/bb_game/src/game.rs`) computes it and passes it into the play (`LiveInput::swing_lead`), and `jump_at_contact` adds each fielder's error, σ `lead_sigma` (purpose `FielderAnticipation` sub-index 2, truncated at 4σ). Over fair balls in play L has mean 159 ms and sd 8.2 ms, about twice the spread of the batter's timing at contact (4.1 ms), because it carries both halves of being early: starting early and meeting the ball out in front.
  - *What it means (his belief).* L is a second read of the same thing the bat angle shows, the bat's face at contact: face ≈ 184.10° − 1,213.3°/s × L (`LEAD_FACE_A_DEG`, `LEAD_FACE_B_DPS` [E]), with 3.1° of scatter (`LEAD_FACE_RESID_DEG` [E]; 11,603 fair balls in 200 games, `sim/crates/bb_game/examples/spray_cues.rs` with left-handers mirrored, refit for the v2 swing 2026-11-14; v1: 161.93° − 1,063.1°/s, 3.6°; refit when the swing model changes). So a lead read gives a face estimate with σ = √((1,213.3°/s × σ_lead)² + 3.1²), batter-relative (the lead is the same for either side; the face is mirrored for a left-hander as above). It is not a separate spray cue: the two face estimates (bat angle, lead) are fused precision-weighted on the circle into one, and only that is mapped to spray through the line above, once. Fusing them as two spray cues would count the batter's timing twice (the unit test `the_swing_lead_moves_the_jump_toward_the_side_it_reads` works both by hand: −12.46° against −15.05°).
  - *How well it can be read (error per grade).* The judgment is where a moving object (the ball, about 6 m out at the launch) was at the moment of an event on a different object (the bat starting forward): the task of Linares, Holcombe & White (2009) with the cue away from the moving object, their Exp. 1: 75 ms (CI 60–86). **Grade 50 = 75 ms** (`LEAD_READ_TIME_SIGMA_50_S` [E]); with the Anticipation ρ of 1.25, 38.4 ms at grade 80 and 146 ms at grade 20 (10 ms at the lab grade 160). The face error it implies (1,213.3°/s on the v2 swing): 47° at grade 80, 91° at 50, 177° at 20, about the same as the bat-angle read's (47°, 92°, 181°; on v1 41/80/156 against 39/77/150), so each roughly halves the variance the other leaves. The lead's twice-larger spread does not escape the limit: the Exp. 8 value (40 ms, a cue on the object's own path) does not apply, because the bat's launch is not on the ball's path. *Assumption, as for the bat angle:* a fielder's explicit timing is no better than a practised lab observer's.
  - *Honest information.* Both reads are timing judgments of the batter's timing, which in the sim varies by only 4–8 ms, against a fielder's 20–150 ms. With the lead added, a grade-80 fielder's best linear estimate of the spray from his noisy reads improves from 22.33° (pitch location alone) to 22.16° (with the bat angle) to 22.00° (with the lead too); at grade 50, 22.42° → 22.40° → 22.35° (`sim/crates/bb_game/examples/spray_cues.rs`, 200 games). Every other honest cue measured carries less (below, "Cues assessed").
- **Cues assessed (2026-11-13; docs/calibration/2026-11-13-anticipation-cue.md).** Over 11,588 fair balls in play (spray sd 22.5°): the called pitch (type and intended location, from the catcher's signs) carries nothing beyond where the pitch actually crossed: type means differ by 1.3° at most and everything visible before contact together (location, called target, height, speed, count, type) leaves 22.02° against 22.12° for horizontal location alone; the speed change from the previous pitch, the count and the batter's earlier balls in play this game carry nothing measurable. Everything informative is the batter's timing (face, contact depth, timing offset, lead: r ≈ 0.51–0.58, each leaving about 18.3°, and jointly no better than one), and every honest read of it is a timing judgment: the bat angle (above), the contact depth relative to the plate (the ball moves 38 m/s, so 4.1 ms of spread against the same 20–80 ms reads), and the swing's lead. The lead carries the most per millisecond of reading error and is the one added.
- **Combining the reads.** He first fuses his two face reads (bat angle, lead) into one face estimate, then maps it to one spray cue, then combines that with the pitch prior. Each fusion is precision-weighted, on the circle: the estimate is the first moved by k = σ_1²/(σ_1² + σ_2²) of the **shortest** angle from it to the second, and offsets from his own line are shortest angles too, so nothing jumps at the ±180° seam (Codex anticipation review #3). **Back-half policy (revised 2026-11-13):** his model covers balls hit forward. A face read that by itself puts the ball more than 90° from straightaway (more than about 62° of face from square for the bat angle; a lead far outside the batter's range for the lead, whose face estimate is not wrapped, so a wild one cannot come back round into the forward half) is off his model: he **discards that read and falls back on the others**, the remaining face read if it is plausible and the pitch prior in any case. So an antipodal prior and cue never meet, and an implausible read never makes him stand still. (Until 2026-11-13 an implausible bat read made him stay set; with the derived read error that happened on about 39% of reads at grade 50, 10% at 80 and 66% at 20, so a poor reader leaned less often rather than leaning on what he did know. Coordinator decision 2026-11-13, following the owner's.) The discard rate per read is the same as before for the bat angle (normal approximation, face mean −7.4°, sd 9.3°) and about the same for the lead (its face error per grade is within 5% of the bat angle's); both reads are discarded together on about 1% of plays at grade 80, 16% at 50 and 44% at 20, and those fielders lean on the prior alone. If the estimate puts the ball within 20° (infield) or 25° (outfield) of his line from home but off to one side (`JUMP_ZONE_DEG`), he starts toward that side across his line, at top speed × min(1, offset / (2 σ_posterior)) (`JUMP_FULL_SIGMAS`) × `jump_gain` (1). A ball at him or well away from him, and he stays set. The pitcher and catcher do not jump. Without a bat read (lab set-ups with no swing) he leans on the pitch prior alone; without a pitch read nobody leans.
- **How well the bat can be read (derivation, owner decision 2026-11-13).** The bat's angle at contact cannot be seen in a frozen frame: it has to be paired with the moment the ball meets it, and the bat is turning fast. Three limits, from the sim and the vision literature:
  - *(a) Timing: when contact happened.* At contact the bat's horizontal angle sweeps at 2,312°/s (mean over 2,747 fair balls in 48 games, sd 209°/s, `sim/crates/bb_game/examples/bat_read_geometry.rs`, refit for the v2 swing 2026-11-14; v1 gave 1,915°/s; `BAT_YAW_RATE_DPS` [E], a fixed property of the swing model, refit when it changes). An error of σ_t in pairing the bat's angle with the moment of contact is an angle error of 2,312°/s × σ_t. The task is judging where a single moving object was at the moment of a cue, against stationary references (the field), which is the task of Linares, Holcombe & White (2009, *J. Vision* 9(13):9, "Where is the moving object now? Judgments of instantaneous position show poor temporal precision (SD = 70 ms)"): the scatter of reported positions equals the distance the object moves in about 70 ms, nearly independent of the trajectory and the cue (Exp. 1, cue a colour change at fixation: mean 75 ms, CI 60–86); with the cue a transient on the object's own path (Exp. 8, rings flashed around the trajectory: closest to a ball striking the bat) it fell to 40 ms (CI 26–53). Brenner & Smeets (2010, *Vision Research* 50:1101–1108, "How well can people judge when something happened?") find the same order: synchrony and temporal-order SDs of 40–50 ms are typical, 20–30 ms at best; Brenner & Smeets (2015, *J. Vision* 15(3):8) summarise "temporal precision is 20 ms at best for simple visual tasks" (judging whether two stimuli were simultaneous or which came first). The much finer precision of interception (about 6–7 ms SD hitting a falling ball, Brenner, Driesen & Smeets 2014, *Front. Hum. Neurosci.* 8:342) and of aligning two bars moving together (about 5 ms, Chung, Levi & Bedell 1996, as analysed by Brenner & Smeets 2010) is not available here: the first is an action guided throughout, the second a relative position between objects moving together, and Linares et al. show the single-object judgment does not get that benefit. **Grade anchors:** grade 50 = 40 ms (`BAT_READ_TIME_SIGMA_50_S` [E]: Linares Exp. 8, a practised lab observer with the cue on the object). With the Anticipation ρ of 1.25 this gives 20.5 ms at grade 80 (the best published value for any explicit visual timing judgment, 20 ms) and 78 ms at grade 20 (Linares Exp. 1, the cue away from the object). The three published values sit on the ρ rule without adjustment. *Assumption:* a professional fielder's explicit timing is no better than the best lab values; there are no published measurements of fielders on this task. **Bat-angle read σ:** 47° at grade 80, 92° at grade 50, 181° at grade 20 (v2 swing; 39°, 77°, 150° on v1).
  - *(b) Space: distance and viewing angle.* A change δψ of the bat's angle moves the barrel by R·δψ sideways (R = 0.99 m, the contact point's mean horizontal distance from the swing's pivot), of which the fielder sees the part across his line of sight (fraction v; median 0.71 at first base, 0.49 third, 0.34 second, 0.14 short, 0.39 left, 0.14 centre, 0.60 right, at the standard positions; a square bat points its barrel's motion straight at the middle of the field). At the resolution limit of normal vision (1 arcmin, the 20/20 definition; *assumption*, generous for an object not tracked by the eye) the angle error from this is atan(1′ · D / (R · v)): 0.7° at first base, 5.4° at short, 11.5° in centre, at the median view. Added in quadrature to (a) it changes the grade-80 σ by at most 4% (centre field, 39.2° → 40.9°) and the grade-50 σ by at most 1%. Only in the edge-on tail (centre field's tenth percentile, v = 0.03: 44°) is it comparable. **So one σ per grade is the model; the viewing term is not built.** It becomes worth building only if the timing term is sharpened, e.g. by a cue that is read relative to something moving with the bat.
  - *(c) The pre-reaction window.* The read has to be made from the contact instant itself, before the reaction gate (0.25 s infield, 0.35 s outfield) opens the ball read of §5.1; nothing later can refine it. Light travel is negligible; the sound of contact reaches centre field after 0.28 s, too late to steer a jump that begins at contact, and it would only time the contact, not show the bat. The values in (a) are trial-to-trial SDs of single judgments, which is what a fielder has: one look per swing, no averaging over repeats.

  *Consequence:* the bat's angle at contact, read honestly, carries little: its spread between swings (sd 9.3°) is far smaller than even an elite read error (39°). A fielder's jump is driven mostly by the pitch prior. Before 2026-11-13 `swing_sigma` was 15° at grade 50 (7.7° / 29° at 80 / 20), set for the earlier read of the ball's spray and never re-derived for a bat-angle read.
- **Cover jobs are not protected (Codex anticipation review #4).** The angular zone does not know who will cover a base: zones overlap, cover roles are given only after the first read, and until his own reaction time ends a fielder keeps going with his jump even after he has been given a base to cover. A man who leans away from his bag pays for it with the stop and turn of §5.3, exactly like a man who leans the wrong way for a ball. That is accepted as part of the bet: anticipation trades a quicker first step on the balls he reads right against a later one on everything else, including his cover jobs. (The first version of this section claimed the zone kept a man with a cover job from being pulled off it; it never did.)

**Movement:** from contact until his reaction time ends he goes for that velocity with the speed law (§5.3); then he reads the ball (§5.1) and plans from where he is and how he is moving, so a misread costs the stop and turn of §5.3. The defense's first assignment is made at the quickest fielder's reaction time; a slower fielder is still carried by his jump then, so every plan made before his own gate opens (reach, the fallback time to the ball's resting point, the call's time to spare) starts from his position **and velocity** stepped through the rest of his jump with the movement step itself (`Sim::planning_state`; Codex anticipation review #2: the first version planned from where he was at the assignment, missing up to the last tenth of a second of his lean).

**Why the swing as well:** fielders leaning on the pitch location alone did worse than fielders who waited (2,048 games: 5.35 runs per team-game with no lean, 5.36 average, 5.45 at grade 80), and shading position before the pitch did too. Location moves the expected direction only about 7° against 22° of scatter, so for a ball in his area the side he leans to is right only a little more than half the time, and a wrong lean (carried through the reaction time, then braked at `BRAKE`) costs more than a right one gains. Most of the scatter comes from the swing's timing, which the bat shows. (Measured since the bat read replaced the spray read: see the anticipation-review calibration note, docs/calibration/2026-11-12-anticipation-review-fixes.md.)

**Measured with the swing's lead and the revised back-half policy (2026-11-13; seed 5, every fielder at one grade; docs/calibration/2026-11-13-anticipation-cue.md):** 1,024 games: no jump 5.57 runs per team-game (GB hit .412); Anticipation 20: 5.52 (.411); 50: 5.55 (.410); 80: 5.49 (.412); lab 160: 5.47 (.406). 4,096 games: no jump 5.55; 20: 5.56; 50: 5.58; 80: 5.51; 160: 5.49. The grades still do not rank on the 20–80 scale beyond whole-game noise; grade 80 may gain a few hundredths of a run. The lead is honest but, like the bat angle, is a timing judgment of a batter whose timing varies by 4–8 ms, so it adds little; the fallback to the prior gains nothing, because the prior alone is worth about what waiting is.

**Measured with the derived bat read (1,024 games, seed 5, base `2e715df`), every fielder at one grade, superseded:** no jump 5.57 runs per team-game (GB hit .412); Anticipation 20: 5.59 (.411); 50: 5.52 (.411); 80: 5.54 (.414); lab grade 160: 5.36 (.405). On the 20–80 scale the jump is now worth about 0.05 runs or less and the grades do not rank beyond whole-game noise: the bat's angle at contact cannot be read well enough to matter, and what remains is the pitch prior (docs/calibration/2026-11-13-bat-read-derivation.md).

**Measured with the bat read at the old σ (15° at grade 50), superseded:** no jump 5.34 runs per team-game (GB hit .410); Anticipation 20: 5.30 (.408); 50: 5.27 (.409); 80: 5.16 (.406). The honest cue carries about half the value the spray read did; nothing was retuned to recover it (docs/calibration/2026-11-12-anticipation-review-fixes.md).

**Measured with the first (spray) read (2,048 games, seed 5), superseded:** no jump 5.35 runs per team-game (GB hit .408); Anticipation 20: 5.31 (.406); 50: 5.22 (.399); 80: 5.03 (.384). `sim-cli games --anticipation <grade>` / `--no-jump`.

*Batters of both sides (2026-11-13, owner backlog A1):* games now have left-handed and switch hitters (data-formats §2.4), so the handedness half of this section runs in every game. The beliefs above (the spray fits) were fitted on right-handed batters and are mirrored for a left-hander; refit them on both sides when they are next refit.

Pitch type (the called pitch) was assessed as a cue on 2026-11-13 and carries no spray information in the sim (type means within 1.3°; see the cue measurements in this section).

---

## 6. Runners and Base Coaches

### 6.1 Leads

- **Primary lead** distance chosen from the runner's belief of pickoff risk: `lead = base_lead(Speed) − k_pick · pickoff_threat_belief` [E], with an aggressiveness shift from the manager.
- **Secondary lead (Q27, built 2026-11-18):** runners are moving at contact. `RunnerStartParams` sets the additional distance and forward speed by outs: **1.0 m and 3.0 m/s with 0–1 out, 1.5 m and 5.0 m/s with two out** [E]. The primary leads at release are 5.5, 6.5 and 4.5 m from first, second and third [E]. Before the pitch there is no force to advance on contact, so runners on any base use the same secondary lead for their outs. Once the read is in, a force constrains the advance as usual. A catch clears the force and can leave a moving runner exposed to an appeal. The batter-runner starts from his box under §6.2.
- The runner from third takes his lead 0.6 m foul [E]. His route blends to the foul line over 2 m at the bag and rejoins the line by 12 m toward home [E]. Observations, tag geometry and position tracks use that route. The release-to-contact track joins the primary-lead position to the live position and velocity. The runner can already be taking steps at release. The path uses constant acceleration or a rising speed curve on a long flight with a fast finish. The existing timeline carries its samples on live-ball pitches. [Runner-timing trace and fit](calibration/2026-11-17-runner-timing.md).
- **Jump:** the runner detects the pitcher's commitment to the plate after `T_jump` from `JumpRead` (purpose `RunnerPerception`). A runner who starts before the commit point on a pickoff move gets picked off physically (M5).

### 6.2 Running

The base path is a sequence of segments with a rounding arc at each base, radius set by speed and `Agility` [E]. Movement uses the §5.3 model, accelerating from the runner's actual speed when he breaks out of the secondary lead. A slide decelerates at 6 m/s² [E] (not `BRAKE`: the body is on the ground) and lowers the body for tag geometry. A base is reached when the body touches the base volume (physics spec §9.4). Tag outs are geometric: glove-with-ball contact with the runner's capsules before the base touch.

*(M4, 2026-11-11: the batter starts in his box, not on the plate. His offset from the base path shrinks linearly to nothing over his first 4 m of running (`BOX_EXIT_M` [E]); tags, his view of the ball, and the recorded track all use that position. He is charged the base-path distance, a few centimetres short of the route's true length.)* *Revised 2026-11-15 (owner's Q1, tag-up review P2-3): that was wrong by up to half a metre each way. He runs straight from his box to the point 4 m up the first-base line and is charged that route: his path coordinate starts at 4 m minus the straight line's length (−0.55 m right-handed, +0.85 m left-handed for boxes at (∓0.95, 0.30)), so first base is 27.98 m away for a right-handed batter and 26.58 m for a left-handed one.*

*(Built 2026-11-13, `Sim::step_overrun`: the overrun of first.)* A batter-runner who decides at first to stop there runs through the bag: he touches it, leaves it on the next tick, and carries on straight down the first-base line, braking from his crossing speed at `OVERRUN_DECEL` = 4 m/s² [E] (from 8.2 m/s he pulls up 8.4 m, 28 ft, past the bag), then walks back at `OVERRUN_RETURN_MPS` = 2.5 m/s [E] (reached with the running time constant) and touches it again. He makes no turn toward second, so the rules engine sees an overrun and he cannot be tagged out on the way back (rules-spec §5.5); the fielders see that too and make no play on him. The play does not settle until he is back on the bag. He makes no new decision while out there; one waiting on a runner ahead decides once he is back. Not yet modelled: a voluntary turn toward second on a hit and back (he decides at the bag to go on or run through; the turn is a right angle, not a curve), a slide into first, and a runner who sees an overthrow during his overrun and goes for second. A batter-runner who went on and is sent back because the runner ahead holds second already retreats to first along the path; the samples judge whether he went far enough to have made an attempt (1.5 m from the line, about 2.1 m of travel toward second), as rules-spec §5.5 describes. (Codex overrun-samples review #1.)

*Position samples (built 2026-11-13).* Every other 60 Hz tick (30 Hz, `SAMPLE_EVERY_TICKS`), after the runners move, the live sim sends each runner still on the base paths to the rules engine as a `RunnerPosition` at his recorded position, all at the same time (rules-spec §2). They are what lets the engine judge the overrun and passing between bases from positions (§5.5).

### 6.3 Advance decisions

At each decision point (contact, ball fielded, throw released, passing a base), the runner evaluates "advance" against "hold":

```
P̂(safe) = Φ( (t̂_ball − t̂_self) / σ_BRIQ )
advance if  P̂(safe) · ΔRE_safe + (1 − P̂(safe)) · ΔRE_out  >  ΔRE_hold + θ_aggr
```

- `t̂_self` uses the runner's own speed (known accurately). `t̂_ball` comes from observing the fielder and the ball, with noise from `BaserunningIQ`.
- `θ_aggr` comes from the team's running-aggressiveness setting.
- **Base coaches** make the send-or-hold call at third (and at second when the runner cannot see the ball), with a team coach perception σ [E, average] and the same rule. The runner follows the coach.
- **Fly balls:** the runner estimates P(catch) from the fielder's position and the ball (own estimate). High P(catch) → return and tag up; uncertain → go partway and brake into the hold using the return approach law (§6.4); a likely hit → run.
- **Batter-runner** always runs; rounding first depends on the same rule.

**Built 2026-11-15 (`bb_agents::running`, `bb_game::live`; owner's Q2, Q8 (c), Q9 (a); sources and arithmetic in [the runner decision model note](calibration/2026-11-15-runner-decision-model.md)).** The rule above is built in this form, which differs from the formula in two named ways:

- **Values.** `AdvanceValues { hold, safe, out }` from the `RUN_EXPECTANCY` belief table (2021–24 major-league average, 24 values, §8's `RE`): the bases as the runner sees them (each other runner at the base he is visibly moving toward, a trailing runner never past him, a lead runner never behind him), the outs on the board, a run for reaching home. Break-even = (hold − out) / (safe − out); the note's table is a unit test.
- **Belief.** P̂(safe) = Φ(margin / `sigma_race_s`), margin = his believed ball time (tag included) less his own time, plus his timing read's error (`RUNNER_TIMING_SIGMA_S` 0.15 s, one draw a look). His own time is his own law of motion from his speed now (the old 0.75 factor for a running start is gone). `sigma_race_s` = 0.20 s [E].
- **Caution replaces θ_aggr, and is in spreads of the race, not in runs** (`caution_z` [E]): he goes when Φ(margin / σ − caution) beats the break-even. *Why not a margin in runs or in probability:* a fixed amount added to a break-even of 94% makes it "never", and added to the run values it forbids a sure advance that gains little; in spreads a sure thing is always taken and the outs still order the decisions. *Why any:* major-league runners are safe 94–97% of the time they go at every number of outs, though the break-evens run from 34% to 94%. The default, 1.0, is provisional: it puts outs on the bases at the league's 0.27 a team-game, and is not yet fitted to attempts and success by situation (except the two-out send from second, below), which waits for the race itself to be right ([runner decision-model note §9](calibration/2026-11-15-runner-decision-model.md#9-the-first-part-the-decision-by-run-value-and-the-expected-play)). Per-runner and per-team aggressiveness (ratings, manager settings) will shift it. A runner ahead who is visibly on his way home is a run in all three values, except that with two out it is lost if this runner would be put out first. A runner follows another home only when the man ahead will be there `FOLLOW_GAP_S` 0.3 s [E] before he could be.
- **The two-out send from second (owner's Q38 (a), decided 2026-11-23; built 2026-11-23, `send_two_out_z` [E] = −0.2).** With two out on the board as he sees them, a runner who started the play on second and decides on home (rounding third, or between third and home) uses `send_two_out_z` in place of `caution_z` for his chance of making home. Between third and home his chance of getting back to third keeps `caution_z` (`chooses_between`; Q38 review P3-1): the coach's send makes going home look better, not the return. At third he goes when Φ(margin / σ + 0.2) beats the break-even (41.5% on a single), that is when he believes he is no more than about 0.08 s behind ball plus tag, where the general caution wanted him 0.16 s ahead. *Source:* the coaching rule, "with two out the coach decides before the pitch to send the runner from second home on a single" and holds him only when he is clearly beaten (Guilliams, [decision-model note §1.6](calibration/2026-11-15-runner-decision-model.md#16-what-coaching-says-a-runner-knows-and-looks-at)); and Retrosheet 2024's two-out sends from second on outfield singles, by fielder: 91.3% (left), 96.2% (center), 92.9% (right), with 5.6%, 0.6% and 2.2% of those sent put out at home ([runner-start note §1](calibration/2026-11-23-runner-start-open.md#1-two-out-sends-from-second-on-singles)). *Fit, an outcome fit (Q38 review P2-1, P3-3):* swept over 1.0 … −1.0 on seeds 0–299 and 1000–1299. −0.2 sends 93.1% at the corners with 4.3% of the sends put out (MLB pooled 92.1% and 3.9%), and center 98% → 99% (MLB 96.2%) ([two-out send note](calibration/2026-11-23-two-out-send.md)). **Values from about 0.1 to −0.3 fit within noise**; −0.2 is a pick inside that range. *Why below zero:* at 0.1–0.3 the corners rose only to 89–90%. It is fitted to the share sent and the share put out. It does not repair a race shown to be wrong: the sim's real throws home are about 0.28 s quicker than the benchmark the first diagnosis used, so the runners held at the old caution were mostly beaten. And his read at third of a charging outfielder's throw home is about 0.2 s slow (he times it from where the fielder stands at his look; [two-out send note §2a](calibration/2026-11-23-two-out-send.md#2a-the-runners-read-of-the-throw-home-review-p2-1)). If that read is corrected, this value must be fitted again, and lower. *Why only from second going home (interim, Q38 review P3-2):* that is the coach's pre-decision the sources describe, and the other two-out advances already match the league or run past it: first to third with two out 47.7% of all singles with a runner on first (Retrosheet 2024, outfield singles with second open: 42.5%), first to home on a double with two out 92.3% of all doubles (Retrosheet 2024, outfield doubles: 58.9%; a separate gap, not this caution's). The coach's two-out send is really about whoever rounds third, so once first to home on a double is right (TASKS §1), the value should be tried for every runner rounding third with two out. "Started on second" also covers a runner from second who held at third and goes home on a later look from the bag; going then with two out is reasonable. The batter-runner never decides on home on a single. With 0–1 out the general caution stands. Per-team and per-coach aggressiveness will shift it with `caution_z`.
- **Between bases** the same comparison is made between the base ahead and the base behind, each with its own cautious chance: the better expected value wins. When he believes he makes neither (both chances under even) the interim rule stands (the base he has the better chance at while it is worth running for, else away from the ball: §6.4 "Looking again"; `MAX_TURNS`) until the rundown is built (owner's Q11).
- **Score and inning** (`run_weight`, interim until the learned `WE` table): the gain of the extra base is multiplied by a weight from the scoreboard, 1 in innings 1–3, the square root of the late value in 4–6, the late value from the seventh: tied 2.6, one behind 1.3, one ahead 1.5, two behind 0.5, three or more behind 0.4, two or more ahead 1 [E; shape from Silver's marginal win values]. *This replaces the note's `V_safe = RE_safe + w`:* with the run itself scaled down, a runner far behind would stop at third on a ball rolling to the wall; scaling the gain keeps a sure base and only makes the risk dearer.
- **The defense's play** (`with_expected_play`): before a throw is in the air the runner no longer assumes it comes to his base. He runs the fielders' rule of §5.6 (p > 0.3, best p² × weight) on what he sees: the other runners' visible headings and speeds with a typical runner's legs, his own legs for himself. If the best play is on another runner at another base, the ball reaches his base only by a second throw from there (`RELAY_READ_S` 0.6 s [E] and a typical throw) or by a cut (`CUT_EXTRA_S` 1.05 s [E] after a straight throw: the relay man's 0.75 s and the longer way round; 0.9 s before the relay exchange of §5.5). If there is no play anywhere, or the best is on him, the straight throw is his to beat. A throw in the air is projected from the runner's noisy look (§6.4). His expectation is a single best play at his own noisy reads, not a distribution.
- **Read at contact.** Existing runners coast at their secondary-lead speed through the first read. With two out they break into a full run after `RUNNER_CONTACT_REACT_S` = 0.25 s [E]. With 0–1 out they read for `RUNNER_READ_DELAY_S` = 0.50 s [E] and decide from their position and speed at that look.
- **Catch belief and ruling (owner decision, 2026-11-18).** Before a public ruling, the runner samples his own noisy ball projection (`RunnerPerception` sub-index 1) every 1/60 s from the current tick to just before its first bounce or wall contact. A pickup after a bounce is outside the catch window. At each point he takes the fielder's best margin to glove reach. He starts each fielder from his visible velocity component toward that point, including motion away, however far off it is (2026-11-23; before, only within 5 m, and farther out he took the fielder to start from rest: in seed 280 play 10 the left fielder, 12.5 m from the ball and running in at about 7 m/s with 1.5 s to go, was given 6.4 m of run, and the runner's noise-free read fell to 0.004 on a ball he caught; [late-catch note](calibration/2026-11-23-late-catch-read.md)).
  - **His doubts.** He turns that margin into a chance with three doubts in quadrature: where the glove can get to, `RUNNER_CATCH_SIGMA_M` = 0.30 m [E, unsourced: about a glove's width]; the fielder's top speed over the run, `FIELDER_SPEED_SPREAD_MPS` = 0.45 m/s [E: the spread of Baseball Savant's sprint speeds, about 1.5 ft/s about the league's 27 ft/s]; and the curve from the spin he cannot see, the fielders' own prior before tracking learns it (`CURVE_PRIOR`, 3 m/s² per axis, ½·a·t² from now; §5.1). Each path supplies a height at each sample. He grades its margin below the catching ceiling continuously in units of the same glove reach spread. He assumes the standardized horizontal and vertical reach errors share a rank, so the tighter score gives their joint catch score. The spin allowance widens horizontal reach while the path scan searches for the catch as the ball changes height. He uses the world’s ceiling of 2.4 m in the open and 3.0 m within `WALL_CATCH_ZONE_M` = 3.0 m [E] of the wall. *The wall (2026-11-23).* How far a point lies inside the glove's reach over the nearest wall (its clearance plus `OVER_WALL_REACH_M`) is one more margin, in the glove and curve spread together (√(`RUNNER_CATCH_SIGMA_M`² + curve²)); the tightest margin sets the score. A point past that reach is no longer out of the question: the curve he cannot see can bring a ball he projects into the seats back in front of them. Before, every point past it scored nothing, and a projection a couple of metres into the foul seats read a catch at the railing as impossible (seed 108 play 142, a foul fly caught by the right fielder: 0.000 at 2.5–3.0 s).
  - **His own look.** The world supplies one noisy position and velocity from the runner’s viewpoint with the snapshot model (§5.1, `RUNNER_READ_TAU_S` = 0.6 s [E]). The runner bases the line of sight, range, speed, closing error and plate weighting on that noisy motion. Until a public ground or wall touch he uses elapsed time since contact and `RUNNER_PLATE_PRIOR` [E], a typical contact at (0, 0.5, 0.9) m. He flies the central read and the eight paths with a reference atmosphere and typical spin. *The curve he has seen (2026-11-23).* From his second look at a ball still on its first flight, he flies it forward with the typical spin's size about the axis he has seen (the plate cue's flight back keeps the typical spin's axis). He keeps his first catch look (when it was, the velocity he read, that read's error) and, look by look, a least-squares fit of the axis. For each later catch look he flies the first look's velocity on to now with his own model and no spin push; the velocity he reads now, less that flight's, is the push he has seen over the interval. A spin about axis ω (in units of the typical spin's size) adds up to a push of ω × J, where J is the push-free path's direction weighted at each step by how hard a spin of the typical size pushes there (the lift law's k·C_L·u²). Each interval adds its equations to the fit, weighed by the two looks' velocity read errors plus the push's own error from the spin's size he cannot see, `SEEN_SPIN_SIZE_SPREAD` = 0.3 of |J| [E: the spread of a batted ball's spin across its flight about the typical backspin, interquartile range over 1.349 of the median, 0.29 at 25–50° and 0.20 at 50° and above, games 0–95 of 2026-11-23; liners spread far more, since their sidespin is not in the typical push]. The typical axis is his prior, with a spread of one per component; it also supplies the part no interval shows (spin about the flight's own direction). The axis is the fit's solution, normalised. *Why intervals (review P2-2, 2026-11-23):* the first version took the push over the whole flight since his first look. On a pop-up whose looks straddle the apex, the rising and falling legs cancel in that sum, and the axis flipped on 16% of such looks under his read noise. The earlier intervals keep the rising leg's direction: on the test's synthetic pop-ups (spin rolled ±60°, pitched ±30°, sized ×0.6–1.6), 4 of 930 straddling looks flip. Without the size term the fit, weighed by read error alone, put large spurious parts along the flight's own direction, where the intervals barely differ. With the seen axis, the median landing miss on those pop-ups is 1.65 m against 3.40 m with the typical axis, and no band of launch angles is worse. A decision at a base after a catch look uses the same. The curve allowance in his catch score stays `CURVE_PRIOR`, since he still does not know the spin's size. *Why:* the typical spin's axis lies across the ball's heading. On a pop-up the heading is only the drift, and the bat's path set the spin. In seed 1040 play 214 (77°, caught by the catcher) the ball spun about 8,900 rpm about +X while it drifted toward third, and the runner flew 5,400 rpm about ±Y. His projection went 6 m the other way, into the seats behind third, and his noise-free read was 0.000 from 2.0 to 3.5 s. With the axis he has seen, his noise-free read of that ball stays at 0.51 or more all the way down, with the catcher standing where it lands (the test turns it, and four other named plays, through ±30° of spray; [late-catch note](calibration/2026-11-23-late-catch-read.md)). The eight points lie `RUNNER_CATCH_READ_POINT` = 2 σ (√4) either way on each of his four error axes, one eighth each [E]. The mean of their best catch scores is a heuristic. A private path change adds no cue to the read. If he advances from this look, he uses its central projection for the pickup race. Every later look at a loose ball (a relook or a decision at a base) goes through the same observation with that decision's draws: the clock since contact and `RUNNER_PLATE_PRIOR` hold while no ground, wall or glove touch has been seen, and the true contact point and the world path's own start play no part (catch-read fixes review P1-1).
  - **Why (2026-11-18).** With only the 0.30 m spread, a runner's look at a liner 0.5 s off the bat, which is metres off in depth and blind to spin, read most outfield catches as near-impossible. Runners then ran on them, and 59 per 300 games were doubled off on 10–25° catches against 13.5 before. [Runner timing note](calibration/2026-11-17-runner-timing.md#read-error-and-return-distance).
  - **What he does.** At `RUNNER_CATCH_CONFIDENCE` = 80% or higher [E] he heads back; a runner on second heads back from `RUNNER_TAG_FROM_SECOND` [E, by the outs before the catch]: 50% with none out (fitted 2026-11-19 to major-league tag-ups from second; [fly-read note §3](calibration/2026-11-19-fly-read.md#3-the-catch-cut-offs)), 60% with one (fitted 2026-11-19; [fly-read note §4](calibration/2026-11-19-fly-read.md#4-review-follow-up)). Call either his *tag-up cut-off*. Below 20% he makes the normal advance decision. *Run-value illustration:* going all out on a ball that is caught doubles him off. From first that costs 0.44 runs with none out (`RE` 0.54 → 0.10) and 0.23 with one out. The full start gains about 0.31 [0.25] runs times the share of singles it turns into first to third. At a fifth of them the break-even is 12% [18%]. The 80% side mirrors that cut. *Checked 2026-11-19 against Retrosheet 2024* ([fly-read note §3](calibration/2026-11-19-fly-read.md#3-the-catch-cut-offs)): the 20% advance cut-off and the 80% return from first and third stand; from second the return was lowered to 50% with none out, since at 80% a runner on second held partway on nearly every outfield fly and never tagged to third (major-league runners advance on 30% of those catches, 37% with none out). With one out it is 60%. A tag that gains third is then worth 0.05 runs (`RE` 0.33 → 0.38 with two out, against 0.71 → 0.98 with none out), while on a ball that drops a runner back on the bag can lose the run he scores from halfway (1.54 against 1.19 with the batter on first), so the one-out value sits above the none-out one (Opus review 2026-11-19, P2-1: 50% stranded runners on drops). Swept at 60, 65, 70 and 80% on 100 games: lowering it from 80% to 60% raises the one-out tag-advance from 1% to 10% of outfield catches (major-league runners: 26%), and the runners it sends back on balls that drop scored no less, since from a hold they stop at third too; second to home on singles did not move. Splitting the cut-offs by catch type is not built. In between, the catch is close: he goes partway and brakes into a hold.
  - **He keeps watching while it is up (in-flight relook, 2026-11-19; [fly-read note](calibration/2026-11-19-fly-read.md)).** A runner who goes at his first look on a ball still in the air keeps looking at it, as a real runner does. Every `RUNNER_FLY_LOOK_S` = 0.5 s [E, unsourced: the first look's own read time, `RUNNER_READ_DELAY_S`] after the look before, while the ball is still on its first flight as he sees it (no ground, wall or glove touch seen, no catch called), he is still going on (his goal past his base, no partway hold, no tag-up, no retouch) or holding partway, and he has not scored, he takes a new look through the same observation as his first: the ball's visible flight with new draws of his own (`RunnerPerception` sub-index 2^21 + the look's number), the clock since contact and `RUNNER_PLATE_PRIOR`, the fielders' visible positions and motion, his own place on the base path. He scores it as at contact. At his tag-up cut-off or more (`RUNNER_CATCH_CONFIDENCE`; from second `RUNNER_TAG_FROM_SECOND`, above) he goes back to tag up (back to his base, where he plants); above the advance cutoff he pulls up into the farthest hold he can still get back from (the hold below, from where he is now and at his speed now), or goes back if there is none; below it he keeps going and looks again. A runner holding partway (from his first look or from a relook) keeps watching the same way: when he becomes sure of the catch (his tag-up cut-off or more) and can be back on the bag before the earliest catch he reads as plausible (his own legs from his signed speed now, braking in), he goes back to tag up; otherwise he holds and looks again. A relook never sends him on again from a hold: that ends, as at contact, when the ball comes down or is caught, or when a relook sends him back to tag up and a later one frees him (below). *Why:* a runner who read a home run at 0.5 s ran flat out for five seconds while the outfielder visibly settled under the ball; 76 runners in 600 games were doubled off that way at 25–50° ([runner-timing note](calibration/2026-11-17-runner-timing.md#fixes-review-follow-up-and-the-2550-double-offs)).
  - **The tagging runner keeps watching (2026-11-23).** A runner tagging up keeps watching it too, on his bag or on his way back to it, with the same looks every `RUNNER_FLY_LOOK_S`. When the catch score P̂ falls to `1 − RUNNER_CATCH_CONFIDENCE` = 0.2 or less, he weighs leaving the bag now against staying on it with the tag-up's option (review P2-1): staying is worth P̂·V_tag + (1 − P̂)·V_drop and going now P̂·V_back + (1 − P̂)·V_now, each V the better of staying and going on as his advance decision values them (the run table and scoreboard, his cautious chance Φ(margin/σ − `caution_z`)). V_tag: the ball caught (one more out, the batter out), tagging up from the bag on the catch with his anticipated start, against a typical throw with the tag from the catch he reads as likeliest (the best of his eight paths). V_drop: the ball down where his central projection first touches ground or wall, he leaves the bag his call reaction (`RUNNER_CATCH_CALL_REACT_S`) after it from rest, against the pickup and throw. V_now: going now, his advance decision's own race from where he is. V_back: going now and the ball caught, he is safe only if his run back from where he is when he sees the call beats a typical throw to his bag, and out otherwise. Only if going now is worth more does he give up the tag-up and decide as he would at contact, from where he is, with this look's projection for the pickup race; if he goes on, he keeps looking as a runner going on. If that decision keeps him at his base, or going now is worth less, he stays tagging and looks again (review P3-2). The same weighing settles his first look when it reads the catch at 0.2 or less: if the tag-up is worth more, he goes back to tag and watches, instead of deciding as at contact (seed 139 play 147's ball, run on 40 draws: a first look at 0.07 had the runner on third going on a fly that was caught 5.6 s later, off the bag at the catch). *Why:* a runner who tagged up never looked again, and a ball he had read as caught at 0.5 s that then fell in front of the fielder found him on the bag; second to home with 0–1 out was about 9 points under MLB's 49.1% ([runner-start note §2](calibration/2026-11-23-runner-start-open.md#2-the-catch-forecast-from-a-single-noisy-look)). It waited on the late-flight read, above: with the read as it was, freed tag-ups doubled off 8 runners in 600 games on pop-ups of 50° and more. *Why the weighing (review P2-1):* freed at 0.2 or less to decide as at contact, he weighed only going now against staying put, not the tag-up he would give up. With fewer than two out a runner on third left the bag on deep flies at 30–41° that were then caught and lost the sacrifice fly (seed 139 play 147: none out, 39.7°, freed at 5.0 s). From third the race home after a deep drop is easy from the bag, so going early gains nothing there and the tag is worth a run; a real runner stays.
  - **The hold is within return distance.** He holds only as far off as he can get back from (the owner's rule: near enough to get back until the call). He checks each observed alternative whose best catch score reaches the advance cutoff, using its catch time and position together. He runs his own legs from their current signed speed toward a candidate hold through the catch and the reaction interval, then back to the bag. The race includes his drift while braking, a typical transfer and appeal throw from that catch, and his caution (`caution_z` spreads of the race). He takes the farthest hold that works across those races, up to `HALFWAY_M` beyond his lead or 0.6 of the way. With no recoverable partway position he brakes back immediately. From third, he also gets back to set up a tag when he can plant before a plausible catch that is far enough out for an advance from rest to compete with the throw home.
- The public `Catch` ruling or the no-catch ruling from a recorded ground or wall touch resolves the read. The runner carries his intended movement for `RUNNER_CATCH_CALL_REACT_S` = 0.25 s [E: the same quarter second as the two-out runner's contact cue, `RUNNER_CONTACT_REACT_S`] after that fact, then heads back or makes the advance decision. The rules record the batter’s out, remove the force and judge retouch at the actual catch time. Until the runner sees the call, traffic and race estimates use his previous force and out count, and a new advance decision waits. The wait starts from what he sees, not from the ruling: any glove possession with no ground or wall touch seen before it holds his new decisions and relooks for the same reaction interval, whether or not the rules record a catch; a possession that is not a catch is then a new thing to look at (catch-read fixes review P1-3). A runner already planted and anticipating a tag-up keeps his set-start reaction in §6.4.
- **Not built:** the base coach's read for a ball behind the runner (owner's Q10 (a)), the rundown (Q11), the turn decision a stride before the base, per-runner caution.

### 6.4 Tag-ups and tags (2026-11-14; values from docs/calibration/2026-11-14-tag-ups.md)

*Why.* Before A4 a runner who came back to his base to tag up kept the speed he arrived with while standing on it (a stale 6.19 m/s on the traced plays). At the catch he judged the advance with the interim rule's running-start factor (run time × 0.75 when v > 1 m/s: third to home 3.14 s) and his legs left at that speed. A4 zeroed a stopping runner's speed on arrival, which is right, and he became a runner caught flat-footed (4.19 s): caught flies with a run scoring fell from about 60 to 16 per 100 games (docs/calibration/2026-11-14-throw-choices.md, “Diagnosis” and “C. Sacrifice flies and the line-drive and fly-ball double plays”). Neither start is what a tagging runner does.

*The model (built, `sim/crates/bb_game/src/live.rs`; parameters `bb_agents::running::TagUpParams` in `LiveInput::tag_up`, and `FielderAttrs::tag_apply_s`).*
- A runner who tags up stands on the bag with no speed, watching the catch. He knows when it comes, so he leaves on it: his first step comes `react_s` [E] after the catch (an anticipated start, not a reaction to a surprise), and he accelerates from rest as v = v_max(1 − e^(−t/τ)) with his set start's time constant, his own τ times `tau_gain` [E], until he next stops or turns back. A runner moving off the bag uses his actual position and signed speed to get back. He brakes through zero, returns and plants on the bag before a new advance is judged. The rules engine judges whether the ball beats the retouch. If the runner retouches during the call’s reaction interval and is still planted when he sees it, he makes the normal post-retouch decision then. His original touch time is the legal one. If that touch carried him on past the bag (a return at speed), the departure is reported on the same tick, the touch stands, and he comes back and plants; his one advance decision (or set start) is made at the plant, not while he drifts, and no touch is reported where his body is not (catch-read fixes review P2-1). A runner already planted and watching for the catch has the anticipated start described above; one planted on his base at the catch without a tag-up set makes the normal decision from rest once he sees the call. Any runner whose movement crosses a bag and ends the tick off it has touched it and left it. A runner carried behind his own base by braking goes back to it: he never chooses the base before it as a refuge.
- He decides with the same start: t̂_self = react_s + the run time from rest under that time constant (`tag_up_time`), against his own read of the throw (§6.3; his read of the fielder with the ball, below). The running-start factor of the interim rule does not apply to him.
- An unforced runner must beat the tag, not only the ball: he adds `typical_tag_s` [E], his belief of the time a fielder needs to put the tag on, to the ball's time.
- *The ball's side.* A fielder who has just secured the ball can tag a runner only from `tag_apply_s` [E, per fielder] after securing it (the catch, the sweep of the glove). The holder's own estimate of a tag option (§5.6) adds the tagger's `tag_apply_s`; forces need none. Before this the tag landed on the tick the ball was secured.
- *Values (2026-11-14).* `react_s` = 0 [E]: the rolling-start technique is built to cut his reaction to the catch to about nothing, and the Statcast tag-up clock starts at the catch ([tag-up note §1](calibration/2026-11-14-tag-ups.md#1-the-runner-catch-to-home-from-third-and-the-other-tag-ups)). `tau_s` = the generic runner's τ, 0.68 s (`RUNNER_TAU`, fitted to the measured 4.03 s standing 90 ft and the 4.26–4.30 s home to first, note §1, §5; was 0.85 s): a rolling start's gain has no measurement, so it is set to nothing. `tag_apply_s` = 0.25 s for every fielder and `typical_tag_s` = 0.25 s, the one quoted receive-and-tag time (a catcher; low confidence; note §3); the rules engine records a tag out at the contact, so with 0 the out was timed on the tick the ball was secured. *2026-11-15 (Q1):* the set start is the runner's own τ times `tau_gain` = 1 [E] (was a separate `tau_s`), and the league τ is 0.76 s (the runners paragraph at the end of §5.7), so the average runner tags from third in 4.10 s. The neutral values (0, 0.85, 0, 0) reproduced the game before the model exactly (digests of seeds 1, 2, 3, 42 and the 300-game run on seeds 0–299).
- *His read of the fielder with the ball* (§6.3's t̂_ball, ball held): that fielder's position's grade-50 transfer and his own arm (scouting knows an arm; outfield arms are 39.1–40.6 m/s, the old belief a constant 37 m/s and 0.75 s), the arm off by a per-runner, per-fielder, per-play read error of σ `ARM_READ_SIGMA_MPS` = 1.1 m/s [E, unsourced: half the arm-grade spread], relayed through a league-typical man halfway when that is quicker. The holder observation publishes the catch time, heading at the catch, motion, stretch or lunge and whether he visibly received a throw. These are fixed at the reception: the private set-up he takes afterwards (a pivot on reaching second) changes his footwork, not the reception the runners saw (catch-read fixes review P1-2). A reception within ordinary receiving reach (1.5 m) of a bag looks like a feed. At second a feed suggests a typical pivot for a throw to first. He reads a relay only from what he can see: the receiver visibly showed the cutoff job, or took it at least `RELAY_SEEN_M` = 6 m from every bag. He then reads the relay man's exchange from that position's grade-50 `Transfer` (§5.5, `relay_s`, no early release; 0.7 of it before 2026-11-19). Any other reception, at a bag or a few metres off it, is a feed: the typical quick transfer (0.7 of grade-50 transfer) and the turn from the visible heading. Fielding and feed turns use the visible heading, and the footwork estimate uses visible motion. A dive starts the usual get-up estimate at the observed lunge, and a stretch adds the ordinary balance recovery.
- *The runner ahead* (`base_held_ahead`): a runner takes the base ahead as held only from what he can see, the runner ahead at or short of it and visibly not moving forward (signed speed at most `SEEN_MOVING_MPS` = 0.5 m/s [E]) for `HOLD_READ_S` = 0.2 s [E, unsourced: a visual reaction time]. It read the runner ahead's private goal before (Gemini A4 review). The read time keeps a runner who has just decided to go, still slow for about three ticks, from turning the runner behind him back.
- *Tests* (`sim/crates/bb_game/src/live.rs`): the legs reach home at react_s + the hand-solved run time from rest (0.2 + 3.94453 s at 8.2 m/s, τ 0.6 s) within the 60 Hz step; a runner standing on the bag with a stale 6.19 m/s starts from rest (first tick v = 8.2·DT/τ); a runner who needs 3.2 s to get going holds on a ball caught 95 m out where a 0.2 s start goes; with `tag_apply_s` 0.3 s a runner 1 m from the plate at 8 m/s scores before the catcher can tag, with 0 he is tagged. When the sources land, the measured values get their own tests: the sourced third-to-home tag-up time, and the probe's sacrifice-fly rate by fly depth against the sourced table.
- *Measured 2026-11-14* (`sf_probe`, 300 games, outfield catches with a runner on third and fewer than two out; see the [catch-read follow-up](calibration/2026-11-17-runner-timing.md#catch-read-review-fixes) for the latest cohorts): scored 53.9%, put out 1.8%, sacrifice flies 0.205 a team-game, against MLB's 75–80%, 2–3.5% and 0.24–0.27. Beyond 300 ft runners are sent 76–98% and safe 94–100%, as in MLB; between 225 and 300 ft they almost always hold. There the sim's ball and runner are even (about 4.07 s against 4.02 s at 280 ft), while MLB outcomes imply a ball of about 4.4 s. *Diagnosed 2026-11-19* ([throw-race note](calibration/2026-11-19-throw-race.md)): the runner's belief matches the world, and the direct throw home is 0.3–0.5 s quicker than MLB's send-and-safe rates imply. The top-10% arm speed is worth at most about 0.1 s of that on present evidence, and a realistic spread of outfield arms moves nothing. The parts that could hold the rest (the outfielder's release on a fly, the true max-effort throw speed, the catcher's receive and tag, a rolling start) are unsourced, so it waited for an owner decision: the owner's answer (Q32, 2026-11-19) is to model how the fielder catches the ball, §5.5 "The catch sets up the throw".
- *His read of a throw in the air* (`Sim::thrown_read`, `ThrownRead`; 2026-11-15, owner's play seed 24583 play 81; rebuilt after Codex review P2-1). A runner gets a noisy look at the thrown ball's position and velocity with the same distance-dependent errors as his batted-ball read, shrinking with time since the visible release. *At the release (2026-11-23)* he has the ball where it is: he watched it leave the hand of the man he was watching hold it, so his position error (the batted-ball look's, whose depth σ, `DEPTH_STRETCH` times the rest, reached 15 m for a throw seen from 75 m) is capped so that its depth σ is no more than the distance the ball has gone since the release; he still misreads how it is moving. Before this a runner standing on first looking at a center fielder's throw to second put it 21.7 m short and read it as going home, went for second, and was tagged going back (seed 20 play 175, from the base relook's first measurement). He uses the same keyed look for both destinations in one decision and predicts its flight with a model of his own: his observed position and velocity, a typical throw's backspin (`THROW_SPIN_RPM`, 1500 rpm), and the reference atmosphere's drag (`reference_env`, the same physics step the fielders' reads of a throw fly with), never the throw's true spin, destination, receiver or future path. He takes it to be coming down where that path first falls through the catching height `RUNNER_READ_CATCH_Z` = 1.3 m [E] (chest height, where throws are aimed); a throw that never rises above that height lands short, hops once (the bounce law on the park's surface, no roughness tilt: he does not know it), and he takes it where the hop ends. The base it is going to is the one nearest that point, and the time he gives the ball at it is when his prediction gets there. (The first version flew a ballistic path with no drag or spin and claimed the error was a metre or two. It is not: Codex found 1 wrong read in 495 chest-high throws at average arms and 24 in 510 at 45 m/s, e.g. (20, 55) to third at the default right fielder's 40.55 m/s read as second, 13.9 m short; a throw from (0, 80) that hops 5 m before home read as second's. The version before that took the base most nearly along the throw's heading, which from center field read a throw to second as going home, with a relay back to second: 8.8 s against the true 5.6 s, and the batter ran on into the tag.) The one function that names the base the throw is going to is the place a situational expectation can later feed (the throw the defense should make from the outs, the score, the runners and which fielder has the ball, then corrected by what the ball does: owner's design guidance, TASKS.md). Whatever its destination, a throw whose predicted track passes base `b` at its closest within `PASS_REACH_M` = 2.5 m [E, unsourced: a receiver's reach and a step], at no more than `PASS_MAX_Z` = 3.0 m [E] and above the ground, can be caught there on the way, so his time for the ball at `b` is at most the time of that closest pass plus `PASS_CATCH_S` = 0.1 s [E, unsourced]; the same holds for its pass within that reach of the man visibly covering `b` (`known_job` `Cover(b)`), plus his way to the bag at a typical fielder's speed (2026-11-23, review P2-1: a receiver a few metres off the bag takes the throw where it comes to him); a throw lobbed over a base (the review's (−40, 45) to home passes third 4.2 m up) does not count, and neither does one wide of it.
- *Where bodies stop* (`settle_guard`; Codex recheck P1-1). One invariant, enforced in the movement step on the final goals of the tick so that no decision path (the look, the held-base branch, `turned_back`, the cap) can bypass it: two live runners never settle on one base. Each runner changes only his own goal, and reads the others from what he can see (catch-read fixes review P1-4: it read their goals before). He settles on base `b` (1–3; any number may score) when it is his goal and he stands on it or is too near to stop short of it; another runner is seen settling on it when he stands on it, or comes at it too fast to stop short (his speed over `HOLD_READ_S`, then `BRAKE`), whether or not he means to stop there. Of two that settle, the trailing one (the lower start) gives way if he is standing on it, no force is on him to it and the base behind him is free (not home: the batter-runner has none; free means no runner on it and none visibly running at it); otherwise the lead runner goes on to the next base (a force pushes him on, and a runner who cannot stop short cannot give way). A trailing runner on the bag therefore steps back for a lead runner visibly coming onto it even when that runner means to turn for home. The decision side agrees: `return_crowded` counts a trailing runner only if he is on or past the base, or it is the next base ahead of him and he cannot stop short of it or has no way back (a batter 5 m out of home no longer crowds second, Codex P2-3), and the held-base branch does not send a runner back to a crowded base. Tests: the three-runner case, the late arrival, a property test over 3,000 seeded random layouts (runners, places, speeds, directions, goals, a force or not, runners diving back or down on a bag from a dive (since 2026-11-23), the ball held anywhere, looks as it is secured and thrown): after it settles no base holds two live runners, every live runner is on a base, out or scored, and the rules engine never warns (72 of the 3,000 fail with the guard off).
- *Braking into a base* (Codex recheck P2-2). A runner going back to a base he will stop on comes in under his own braking: he caps his speed so the distance left after each tick covers the braking distance at that speed (floor `APPROACH_MIN_MPS` = 1 m/s [E]). His deceleration is limited to `BRAKE`; when a newly chosen stop is already inside his braking distance he may carry past it and return. He plants when he reaches the stop at the slow approach speed, so v = 0 before the next leg is judged, so that leg starts from rest in the estimate and in his legs. The return estimate steps the same law (`run_time_to_stop`). The old retouch plant zeroed 7 m/s in a tick and judged the next leg with the running-start discount (3.0 s against the 4.0 s from rest).
- *Diving back (owner's Q39 (a), 2026-11-23; [runner-start note §3](calibration/2026-11-23-runner-start-open.md#3-returns-brake-onto-the-bag-no-dive-back), [dive-back note](calibration/2026-11-23-dive-back.md)).* On a close return a runner does not brake onto the bag: he runs into it and dives headfirst, the ordinary pickoff and line-drive return. *Why:* braking cost a contested return 0.45–0.75 s, and about 90 runners in 600 games were tagged on grounders a step short of their own bag while braking (MLB about 3).
  - **When.** He decides with the return estimate he already makes, braking included. Between bases (`runner_relook`), when the look sends him back, he dives if his margin there lies between −`DIVE_SAVE_S` and `DIVE_CLOSE_S`. Above `DIVE_CLOSE_S` = 0.5 s [E: about what braking costs him] braking cannot lose him the race. Below −`DIVE_SAVE_S` = 0.4 s [E: the least the dive saves over braking, 0.43 s from 4 m at rest, below] not even the dive can win it. The margin is his read of the ball at the bag plus the tag, less his own time, plus his timing error. The same holds for a runner the turn cap (`MAX_TURNS`) keeps committed to the way back. Beaten by more, he brakes as before and is caught short, the stand-in for a rundown (Q11). *Revised 2026-11-23 (review P2-1):* with no lower bound, 175 of 829 dives in the standard run were runners beaten by more than a second, sprinting and leaving their feet in front of a fielder already on the bag with the ball, and 162 of them were tagged in the air. Coming back to retouch after a catch, he compares his braked return with the throw he believes in, and dives whenever braking could lose him the race (margin under `DIVE_CLOSE_S`), with no lower bound. *Decided 2026-11-23 (Coordinator, on the dive-back note §6):* on a retouch the fielder needs only to touch the bag, so the runner has no rundown or other way to stay alive. His quickest arrival is his only chance, and real runners dive back on double-off attempts whether or not they think they will make it. A runner going back to a tag play can pull up and try to stay alive, so the lower bound stays there. (With the bound, 8 retouches in 600 games that he believed lost by more than 0.4 s, and that his hand would have won, were appeal outs: his retouch belief is pessimistic, a lead in TASKS §1.) That throw is a grade-50 transfer from the catch plus a typical throw to his bag from where the fielder who caught it stands when the runner sees the call, with no tag: the same belief as the partway hold (`catch_hold_s`). That needs no new draw. A runner sent back by traffic (a runner ahead holding, or giving way) has no race with the ball and brakes as before. **Once chosen, the dive stands while his goal is unchanged:** a later look that keeps him going back does not cancel it, whatever its margin, since diving is never slower and a runner already inside his braking distance would otherwise overshoot his bag (review P3-1). A look that sends him on cancels it; so does any other change of goal.
  - **Run-in.** With the dive chosen he runs at the bag on his ordinary speed law, with no braking cap. Moving away, he first brakes through zero (*Turning round*).
  - **Leaving his feet.** He leaves his feet once he is moving toward the bag within `DIVE_BACK_M` = 2.2 m of it [E] (a step and a glide; a dive back "is simply a glide on top of the grass, not a dive or a leap", [Human Kinetics](https://us.humankinetics.com/blogs/excerpt/the-headfirst-slide)). His body then glides at a constant `v_glide` = max(`DIVE_KEEP` × v, `DIVE_PUSH_MPS`): `DIVE_KEEP` = 0.8 [E] for the speed lost at the plant, and `DIVE_PUSH_MPS` = 4.0 m/s [E, unsourced: about a standing broad jump's take-off] for the lunge of a runner who is barely moving.
  - **The touch.** His hand, `DIVE_REACH_M` = 0.4 m [E] ahead of his path coordinate, touches the bag when it reaches the base coordinate. The sim's standing touch is counted at the path coordinate, which stands for his leading foot, so 0.4 m is the hand's extra lead over that foot. He touches at `v_glide`.
  - **Calibration of those values.** At full speed the dive then ties a run-through to within 0.03 s (0.281 against 0.283 s over the last 2.2 m at 8 m/s). Running through is the quickest arrival in the measured order: run through, headfirst, feet-first, run to a stop ([J Sport Health Sci 2016](https://doaj.org/article/23f54a6e68454d06803400e3bd63eb82)); the two slides differ by hundredths. From a 3.5 m lead at rest he is back in 0.99 s ("less than 1 second" to dive back from a normal lead, [Wikipedia, Pickoff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pickoff)). Against braking he saves 0.43 s from 4 m at rest, 0.62 s from 8 m and 0.73 s from 15 m (the note's table: 0.45, 0.63, 0.74).
  - **Tagged in the air.** If he is put out before his hand arrives (a tag on his laid-out body, or an appeal), he lies where he was when he was put out and is up `DIVE_BACK_UP_S` later. A play that ends while he is in the air ends his glide there in the same way.
  - **Down.** At the touch his hand holds the bag and his body stops. He is on the base (his path coordinate is the bag's) and lies there for `DIVE_BACK_UP_S` = 1.0 s [E, unsourced estimate: a push-up to his feet over the bag; the fielders' 1.5 s includes getting set to throw]. Until he is up he makes no decision and does not move, so he cannot go on a misplay. A retouch's decision, and any look the play has given him meanwhile, comes when he is up, standing on the bag at rest.
  - **Tags.** Up to the touch, the tag reaches the nearest point of his body. That is his path position while he is on his feet, and from the moment he leaves them the segment along the base path from his hand back `DIVE_BODY_M` = 2.0 m [E: his length laid out, hand to feet]. The tag reach is the fielder's usual `tag_reach_m`. He is protected, as a standing runner is, once that touch point is within 0.3 m of the bag. After the touch he is on it.
  - **The record.** `LiveOutput::runner_dives` records each dive from the moment he leaves his feet (review P2-2: it was recorded only at the touch, so a dive that ended in the air had none). The record holds who, which base, when he left his feet and where his hand was then, when the glide ended and where his hand was, whether that end was the touch, and when he is up. The timeline carries it as `Dive`, `BaseTouch` (only when he touched) and `DiveUp` markers for the runner (data-formats §5.4), and the client draws the dive from them (player-visuals §11).
  - **Not modelled:** a feet-first slide back; diving to the far side of the bag, away from the tag; a dive that overshoots and loses the bag; a runner on his feet who chooses a quicker run-through. Leaving his feet takes no draw, so there is no new `Purpose`.
  - **Measured (2026-11-23; dive-back note §2):** about one dive back a game. It barely changes the outs going back (73 to 72 tag outs on grounders, 141 to 133 appeals in 600 games), because those runners were beaten by a second or more, not by the braking.
  - **The runner from third** breaking on a ground ball in front of him was examined in the same job and is not a fault overall: he goes and scores at MLB's rate (Retrosheet 2024, 56% scored against 56%; note §3). By fielder the rates diverge both ways (pitcher and first far lower, short far higher), and MLB's cohort includes the infield in; both are open. He is put out back at third where MLB runners are put out at home or in a rundown (the rundown, Q11).
  - *Tests* (`sim/crates/bb_game/src/live.rs`):
    - A constructed close return (after the runner-start note's typical case, which turned out to be a runner 1.15 m short, not a 0.13 s race): a runner on third coming back 4.4 m out at 7.4 m/s, with the third baseman on the bag able to tag 0.13 s after the dive's hand-worked touch, about 0.4 s before his braked arrival. Braking, he is tagged; diving, he is safe, and he touches at the hand-worked time.
    - A runner held to the way back by the turn cap and beaten by a second by his own read brakes in, with no dive (review P2-1). A runner coming back to retouch who believes the throw beats him by about a second still dives. A dive chosen on a close look stands when a later look keeps him going back with time to spare (P3-1).
    - A runner tagged in the air has his dive recorded, untouched, lying where he was tagged and up `DIVE_BACK_UP_S` later (P2-2).
    - A return with a wide margin still brakes and plants.
    - A diving runner holds on a misplay until he is up.
    - A tag on a runner between bases after a non-goal crossing (runner-start note §5).
- *Turning round* (Codex P2-2). A runner whose goal turns behind him does not flip his direction at speed: he brakes through zero at `BRAKE` = 4.75 m/s² (the fielders' sourced figure, so one stopping law for every athlete; a runner has no separate measurement) and then accelerates the other way on his usual curve. 8 m past second at 7 m/s he drifts 5.16 m on before coming back, and a retouched runner who goes on again plants on the bag first. The first version let `goal` change under his speed: he went from +7.0 to −7.03 m/s in one 1/60 s tick while his estimate paid for a stop from rest (1.59 s against the 1.07 s his legs took), which made the new rule's returns look cheaper to the legs than to the race.
- *Turning round once* (`MAX_TURNS` = 1 [E]), for a trapped runner. A runner whose fresh look finds no base he believes he reaches safely turns round once between two bases (the count resets when he stands on a base), then stays committed to the base he is heading for; a look that finds a base he believes safe may take it (Codex recheck P2-4). This is a stand-in for a rundown, which is not modelled (the fielders do not close on a trapped runner): without it a runner caught between third and home turned at every throw while two fielders threw to the base he was heading for, and 91 of 300 plays ran to the 40 s limit (`play_timeout`).
- *Looking again* (`runner_relook`). A runner between bases decides again when the play changes under him: the ball is secured by a fielder (any `GloveSecured`), or a throw is released, since he last looked. Every look is a new decision (`decisions` increments, so its draws, the timing error at sub-index 2n and the read of a loose or thrown ball at 2n + 1, are its own; data-formats §2.3). It is the same race as at a base, from where he is: his time to the next base against the ball's time there, and his time back to the base behind him against the ball's time there, both from his own signed velocity (`run_time_from`: with a running start only if he is moving that way; going the other way the time includes the stop), each plus the tag he must beat, each with the same timing error and the 0.35 s margin. He goes on if he beats the ball to the next base, otherwise back if he beats it to the last; at neither, to the base he has the better chance at as he sees it (`p_safe`), while that chance is at least `TRAPPED_MIN_P` = 5% [E, unsourced: a chance worth running for]; with less at both, he keeps away from the ball, toward the base it takes longer to reach (a rundown is not modelled: either way he is probably out). *Revised 2026-11-23 (review P2-3):* it was always the base the ball takes longer to reach, whatever his own distance, so a runner 6.5 m from second on a coin flip there turned for first, hopeless, and runners 11–14 m from home on a bang-bang throw turned for third and were tagged going back. Choosing by the margin alone instead would send a runner 8 m past second into the third baseman standing on third with the ball (−1.7 s there, −3.0 s back). A forced runner (the base ahead is the one after his own, and every base behind him is held) has no choice and keeps going; he does not go back to a base that is no refuge (`return_crowded`): a runner who started behind him stands on it, or is coming at it and cannot stop short of it (his position and speed, `HOLD_READ_S` for his reading the turn, then `BRAKE`), or is coming at it with no base of his own to go back to (the batter-runner, or the base he left held by another runner); the lead runner then goes on, and a trailing runner who can stop gives way, stopping and going back to his own base if it is free (`runner_traffic`; once the lead runner is visibly coming back to a base, `base_held_ahead` counts it held); the check runs every tick while the bodies move, not only at the look (Codex P1-1: a trailing runner arriving after the look left two on second and the rules engine erased one; seed 1000003226 play 194 was the same fault); a runner whose base ahead a runner has not yet cleared leaves it to the held-base rule (`base_held_ahead`); tag-ups, a halfway hold, a retouch, a wait for the runner ahead and the batter's overrun keep their own rules.
